### LAWCAT: Efficient Distillation from Quadratic to Linear Attention with Convolution across Tokens for Long Context Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18467v1)
- **Authors**: Zeyu Liu, Souvik Kundu, Lianghao Jiang, Anni Li, Srikanth Ronanki, Sravan Bodapati, Gourav Datta, Peter A. Beerel
- **Abstract**: Although transformer architectures have achieved state-of-the-art performance across diverse domains, their quadratic computational complexity with respect to sequence length remains a significant bottleneck, particularly for latency-sensitive long-context applications. While recent linear-complexity alternatives are increasingly powerful, effectively training them from scratch is still resource-intensive. To overcome these limitations, we propose LAWCAT (Linear Attention with Convolution Across Time), a novel linearization framework designed to efficiently transfer the capabilities of pre-trained transformers into a performant linear attention architecture. LAWCAT integrates causal Conv1D layers to enhance local dependency modeling and employs normalized gated linear attention to improve generalization across varying context lengths. Our comprehensive evaluations demonstrate that, distilling Mistral-7B with only 1K-length sequences yields over 90\% passkey retrieval accuracy up to 22K tokens, significantly extending its effective context window. Similarly, Llama3.2-1B LAWCAT variant achieves competitive performance on S-NIAH 1\&2\&3 tasks (1K-8K context length) and BABILong benchmark (QA2\&QA3, 0K-16K context length), requiring less than 0.1\% pre-training tokens compared with pre-training models. Furthermore, LAWCAT exhibits faster prefill speeds than FlashAttention-2 for sequences exceeding 8K tokens. LAWCAT thus provides an efficient pathway to high-performance, long-context linear models suitable for edge deployment, reducing reliance on extensive long-sequence training data and computational resources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LAWCAT (Linear Attention with Convolution Across Time), a novel framework for efficiently distilling knowledge from pre-trained transformer models into performant linear attention architectures. The key innovations are: (1) integrating causal Conv1D layers to enhance local dependency modeling, and (2) employing normalized gated linear attention (GLA) to improve generalization across varying context lengths.  The authors demonstrate that LAWCAT can achieve competitive or even superior performance on long-context tasks like passkey retrieval, S-NIAH, and BABILong, while using significantly less training data compared to training linear attention models from scratch. Furthermore, the resulting LAWCAT models exhibit faster prefill speeds than FlashAttention-2 for long sequences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of combining causal Conv1D layers with normalized GLA for linear attention is reasonably novel.  While both Conv1D layers for local modeling and GLA are established techniques, their specific combination within a distillation framework for long-context linear attention models is a contribution.  The normalization of GLA is a practical improvement over previous implementations.  The paper convincingly shows that this combination addresses a known weakness in standard linear attention.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical benefits for long-context modeling, particularly in resource-constrained settings. LAWCAT enables the creation of efficient linear attention models with minimal training data, a crucial factor for edge deployment and rapid experimentation.  The demonstrated performance improvements on various benchmarks, especially the ability to extend the effective context window beyond the pre-trained model's limitations, are compelling.  The faster prefill speeds compared to FlashAttention-2 make LAWCAT a potentially attractive option for latency-sensitive applications. However, it is worth noting that while the paper is convincingly argued and the results are compelling, there is room to argue on the significance since it still relies on a teacher model for distillation which can potentially hinder the generalizability of results.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation across multiple tasks (passkey retrieval, S-NIAH, BABILong) and model sizes (Mistral 7B, Llama3.2 1B).
    * **Ablation Studies:** The detailed ablation studies clearly demonstrate the importance of both the Conv1D layer and the GLA normalization.  They provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
    * **Efficiency Focus:** The paper successfully addresses the efficiency concerns of long-context modeling, showing that LAWCAT models can be trained with minimal data and exhibit faster inference speeds.
    * **Clear Motivation and Explanation:** The algorithmic motivation for incorporating Conv1D and normalization is well-articulated.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on Distillation:** The primary reliance on distillation, while efficient, might limit the ultimate performance ceiling of the resulting LAWCAT models compared to models trained from scratch. Although the reliance on knowledge distillation allows for efficient training it can be a potential limitation on the generalizability of results and should be critically considered.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides a good intuitive explanation, a more formal theoretical analysis of why the Conv1D and GLA normalization are effective would further strengthen the contribution.  A formal proof of convergence or generalization guarantees would be highly valuable.
    * **Limited Comparative Baselines:**  While the comparison to LoLCATs is important, a comparison to a wider range of recent linear attention methods (e.g., more recent SSMs) would provide a more complete picture of LAWCAT's performance relative to the state-of-the-art.

* **Potential Influence:** LAWCAT could have a notable influence on the development of efficient long-context models, particularly for edge applications.  It offers a practical and accessible approach for leveraging pre-trained transformers in resource-constrained environments. The paper could spur further research into combining convolution and attention for improved local context modeling within linear attention architectures.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's solid contributions in terms of novelty and significance, while acknowledging some limitations. The novel combination of Conv1D and normalized GLA within a distillation framework is a valuable contribution to the field. The demonstrated performance improvements and efficiency benefits are compelling. The ablation studies provide excellent insights. However, the dependence on distillation, the limited theoretical analysis, and the somewhat limited comparative baselines prevent a higher score. Despite these shortcomings, the paper presents a practical and effective technique that is likely to influence future research in efficient long-context modeling.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Discrete-time diffusion-like models for speech synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18470v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaozhou Tan, Minghui Zhao, Mattias Cross, Anton Ragni
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have attracted a lot of attention in recent years. These models view speech generation as a continuous-time process. For efficient training, this process is typically restricted to additive Gaussian noising, which is limiting. For inference, the time is typically discretized, leading to the mismatch between continuous training and discrete sampling conditions. Recently proposed discrete-time processes, on the other hand, usually do not have these limitations, may require substantially fewer inference steps, and are fully consistent between training/inference conditions. This paper explores some diffusion-like discrete-time processes and proposes some new variants. These include processes applying additive Gaussian noise, multiplicative Gaussian noise, blurring noise and a mixture of blurring and Gaussian noises. The experimental results suggest that discrete-time processes offer comparable subjective and objective speech quality to their widely popular continuous counterpart, with more efficient and consistent training and inference schemas.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores discrete-time diffusion-like models for speech synthesis.  It contrasts them with the more common continuous-time diffusion models (DMs) and flow matching (FM) models. It argues that continuous-time methods, while popular, have limitations such as reliance on additive Gaussian noise and inconsistencies between continuous-time training and discrete-time inference. The authors investigate various noising processes in a fully discrete-time framework, including additive Gaussian noise, multiplicative Gaussian noise, blurring noise, and a mixture of blurring and Gaussian noise.  The paper aims to show that discrete-time diffusion-like models offer comparable, and potentially more efficient and consistent, performance compared to continuous-time approaches. Experimental results using the LJspeech dataset are presented.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Addressing a Real Problem:**  The paper tackles a valid and significant issue: the inconsistency between training and inference in continuous-time diffusion models.  The reliance on discretized differential equation solvers for inference, despite continuous-time training, introduces an approximation error that's worth addressing.
    * **Exploring Alternatives:**  The exploration of alternative noise types in the context of diffusion models is a valuable contribution. Multiplicative Gaussian noise, blurring, and their combination are interesting and not as well-studied as additive Gaussian noise. This provides a basis for future research directions.
    * **Consistent Framework:**  The fully discrete-time approach ensures consistency between training and inference, which is a theoretical advantage.  This is a key argument the paper makes and a solid foundation for its investigations.
    * **Thorough Experimentation:** The paper includes objective (MCD, log f0, UTMOSv2) and subjective (MOS) evaluations, providing a reasonable assessment of the proposed methods. The inclusion of a baseline (Grad-TTS-DT) allows for a fair comparison.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and explains the concepts clearly, making it easier to understand the nuances of discrete-time diffusion models.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty in Method:** While the application to speech synthesis and the specific noise combinations are novel, the core ideas behind discrete-time diffusion and using different noise types are already present in the image processing literature (as acknowledged by the authors). The adaptations for speech, while non-trivial, are not dramatically groundbreaking.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The results, while demonstrating comparable performance, do not show a significant, convincing improvement over the continuous-time baseline or even the discrete-time baseline (Grad-TTS-DT). The MOS scores are quite close, and the objective metrics don't point to a clear winner.  A stronger, more definitive advantage would increase the paper's impact.
    * **Lack of Insightful Analysis:**  The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* certain noise types perform better (or worse) in specific scenarios. For example, more explanation on how deterministic blurring affects speech quality. Why does the UTMOSv2 evaluation penalize the Blurring method?
    * **Suboptimal Encoder:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of the encoder used, which potentially obscures the true capabilities of the proposed noising strategies.  Using a more powerful or well-tuned encoder could have yielded more significant results.

* **Significance:**

    The paper contributes to the ongoing research into diffusion models by:

    *   Highlighting the benefits of a fully consistent, discrete-time approach.
    *   Expanding the range of noise types considered for speech synthesis.
    *   Providing a baseline for future work on discrete-time diffusion for speech.

    However, its impact is somewhat limited by the lack of substantial performance gains and the reliance on existing, albeit adapted, methodologies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by exploring a less-trodden path in diffusion models, specifically discrete-time frameworks, and investigating different noise types relevant to speech. It offers a technically sound approach and presents reasonable experimental validation. However, it falls short of being a transformative work due to the limited novelty of the methods used, the lack of substantial performance improvement over the baseline, and the absence of in-depth insightful analysis. The benefits of explored noise methods are not well-addressed, making it difficult to understand their advantages and limitations.

Score: 6/10

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Physics-informed time series analysis with Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks under Ehrenfest constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18483v1)
- **Authors**: Abhijit Sen, Illya V. Lukin, Kurt Jacobs, Lev Kaplan, Andrii G. Sotnikov, Denys I. Bondar
- **Abstract**: The prediction of quantum dynamical responses lies at the heart of modern physics. Yet, modeling these time-dependent behaviors remains a formidable challenge because quantum systems evolve in high-dimensional Hilbert spaces, often rendering traditional numerical methods computationally prohibitive. While large language models have achieved remarkable success in sequential prediction, quantum dynamics presents a fundamentally different challenge: forecasting the entire temporal evolution of quantum systems rather than merely the next element in a sequence. Existing neural architectures such as recurrent and convolutional networks often require vast training datasets and suffer from spurious oscillations that compromise physical interpretability. In this work, we introduce a fundamentally new approach: Kolmogorov Arnold Networks (KANs) augmented with physics-informed loss functions that enforce the Ehrenfest theorems. Our method achieves superior accuracy with significantly less training data: it requires only 5.4 percent of the samples (200) compared to Temporal Convolution Networks (3,700). We further introduce the Chain of KANs, a novel architecture that embeds temporal causality directly into the model design, making it particularly well-suited for time series modeling. Our results demonstrate that physics-informed KANs offer a compelling advantage over conventional black-box models, maintaining both mathematical rigor and physical consistency while dramatically reducing data requirements.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a justified novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to physics-informed time series analysis using Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) augmented with Ehrenfest constraints (KAN-ETS). The method aims to predict the dynamics of quantum systems more accurately and with less training data than traditional black-box models. The key idea is to enforce physical consistency by incorporating Ehrenfest theorems into the loss function.  A new architecture, "Chain of KANs," is proposed to embed temporal causality directly into the model. The effectiveness of KAN-ETS is demonstrated on the problem of modeling high harmonic generation (HHG) in driven Ising-type systems. The results show that KAN-ETS achieves superior accuracy with significantly less training data compared to Temporal Convolution Networks (TCNs) and maintains mathematical rigor and physical consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel combination of KANs and physics-informed learning, specifically tailoring it for time series analysis of quantum dynamics. While PINNs are established, applying KANs in this context, particularly with the Chain of KANs architecture for enforcing causality, is a fresh contribution. Using Ehrenfest's theorem as a constraint is also a clever way to inject physics into the model in a way applicable to various domains. It's not a completely groundbreaking theoretical breakthrough (e.g., no new theorem proving) but a creative application of existing tools.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in its ability to significantly reduce the data requirements for modeling quantum systems and improve the physical interpretability of the models. Black-box models often struggle with capturing underlying physics, leading to unrealistic predictions and requiring vast datasets. KAN-ETS offers a promising alternative, potentially enabling more efficient and reliable simulations in areas like quantum control, spectroscopy, and materials science. The demonstration on HHG is relevant because it is an area where accurate modeling is hard to achieve. Also, the comparison with TCNs highlights its advantages over the state-of-the-art model.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The most compelling strength is the demonstrated reduction in training data requirements. The 95% reduction in data compared to TCNs is a substantial improvement, making the method more practical for systems where data is scarce or expensive to obtain.
    *   **Physical Consistency:** Enforcing Ehrenfest theorems helps to ensure that the model respects fundamental physical laws, leading to more reliable and interpretable predictions.
    *   **Chain of KANs:** The proposed architecture provides a natural way to incorporate temporal causality, which is crucial for time series modeling.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the methodology and results clearly, including detailed architectural information. Code availability enhances reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Application:** The empirical evaluation is focused on a specific class of quantum systems (Ising chains). While HHG is important, it's necessary to demonstrate the effectiveness of KAN-ETS on a wider range of systems and problems to establish its broader applicability.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** KANs can be sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. The paper acknowledges the importance of architecture stability but doesn't provide a comprehensive analysis of how different hyperparameters affect performance, especially λ.
    *   **Complexity:** While KANs have fewer parameters than a standard NN for a given complexity, they are not necessarily simpler to implement and train. The specialized "chain of KANs" architecture also adds complexity.
    *   **Explainability:** While KANs improve on DNNs in terms of explainability, the extracted formulas from the trained networks are not explored in detail.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to physics-informed machine learning. The combination of KANs and Ehrenfest constraints is innovative and shows promising results in terms of data efficiency and physical consistency. While the empirical evaluation is limited, the potential impact of the method warrants further investigation. The biggest appeal is data efficiency and potentially improved adherence to physics constraints. But it needs more study.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's strong points in novelty and significance, offset by the limitations in the scope of application and hyperparameter analysis. It offers a significant advance within its relatively narrow scope, but has not yet been validated on a broad range of problems. It avoids being a higher score (8 or 9) because the lack of broad empirical testing and the practical challenges of implementing and tuning KANs limit its immediate impact. Finally, it is a new development in a hot area that is of interest to the general machine learning community.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Actions Speak Louder than Prompts: A Large-Scale Study of LLMs for Graph Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18487v1)
- **Authors**: Ben Finkelshtein, Silviu Cucerzan, Sujay Kumar Jauhar, Ryen White
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for text-rich graph machine learning tasks such as node classification in high-impact domains like fraud detection and recommendation systems. Yet, despite a surge of interest, the field lacks a principled understanding of the capabilities of LLMs in their interaction with graph data. In this work, we conduct a large-scale, controlled evaluation across several key axes of variability to systematically assess the strengths and weaknesses of LLM-based graph reasoning methods in text-based applications. The axes include the LLM-graph interaction mode, comparing prompting, tool-use, and code generation; dataset domains, spanning citation, web-link, e-commerce, and social networks; structural regimes contrasting homophilic and heterophilic graphs; feature characteristics involving both short- and long-text node attributes; and model configurations with varying LLM sizes and reasoning capabilities. We further analyze dependencies by methodically truncating features, deleting edges, and removing labels to quantify reliance on input types. Our findings provide practical and actionable guidance. (1) LLMs as code generators achieve the strongest overall performance on graph data, with especially large gains on long-text or high-degree graphs where prompting quickly exceeds the token budget. (2) All interaction strategies remain effective on heterophilic graphs, challenging the assumption that LLM-based methods collapse under low homophily. (3) Code generation is able to flexibly adapt its reliance between structure, features, or labels to leverage the most informative input type. Together, these findings provide a comprehensive view of the strengths and limitations of current LLM-graph interaction modes and highlight key design principles for future approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive, large-scale evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) for node classification in text-rich graphs.  It systematically analyzes the performance of LLM-based methods across several key axes: (1) LLM-graph interaction mode (prompting, tool-use (ReAct), code generation); (2) dataset domains (citation, web-link, e-commerce, social networks); (3) graph structural regimes (homophilic/heterophilic); (4) feature characteristics (short/long text); (5) LLM size, and (6) reasoning capabilities. The study goes beyond overall accuracy by probing reliance on features, structure, and labels via truncation and ablation experiments. The key findings include that Code-as-Graph performs best, especially on long-text and high-degree graphs; LLMs are surprisingly effective on heterophilic graphs regardless of interaction method; and Code-as-Graph can adapt its reliance between different input types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  While using LLMs for graph tasks isn't entirely new, the *systematic* and *controlled* nature of this study is a significant contribution.  Prior works often focus on specific datasets or limited interaction methods.  This paper's factorial experiment design across multiple axes is highly valuable.  The ablation studies (feature, edge, label removals) to understand information dependencies are also a strong point, moving beyond simply reporting accuracy. The discovery that LLMs are robust even with heterophilic graphs challenges an assumption made from previous limited work. The code generation and tool-using are novel aspects, that provide an avenue for leveraging LLMs without having to overload context windows.

* **Significance:**  This work provides practical guidance for researchers and practitioners interested in applying LLMs to graph-based tasks.  The finding that Code-as-Graph is superior, especially with long texts or high node degree, is actionable.  The insight that LLMs can be effective on heterophilic graphs is also important because it broadens the applicability of these methods. By identifying the strengths and limitations of different LLM-graph interaction modes across different graph types, the work avoids promoting a one-size-fits-all approach, which is quite valuable. By removing one variable at a time, and by varying levels of removal, the authors have made it easier to pinpoint where the strengths lie for each algorithm.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The factorial design is a major strength.  The study covers a wide range of datasets, graph structures, and LLM configurations.
    * **In-Depth Analysis:** The ablation experiments provide valuable insights into the inner workings of LLM-based graph reasoning methods.
    * **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides practical guidelines for choosing the right LLM-graph interaction mode based on the characteristics of the task.
    * **Reproducibility:** Providing prompt templates enhances the reproducibility of the study.
    * **Challenging Assumptions:** The finding on heterophilic graphs is counterintuitive and important.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper could benefit from some discussion on the computational costs associated with the different interaction modes, especially Code-as-Graph which involves code generation and execution, versus simple prompting. The latency and infrastructure requirements might be significant for real-world deployments.
    * **Limited LLM Architecture Variety:** The choice of LLMs, while representing a range of sizes, could be expanded to include a wider range of architectures (e.g., Mixture of Experts models, models trained with different objectives) for even more complete understanding.
    * **Simplification of Real-World Graphs:**  The paper focuses on node classification. Real-world graphs often involve more complex tasks like link prediction or graph generation.  The extent to which the findings generalize to these tasks is unclear.
   * **Dependency of hyperparameters for prompt engineering**: While some effort was made to optimize hyperparameters, the prompts are specific to the tasks at hand. Hyperparameters and prompt engineering remain a potential weakness for reproducibility, as well as for generalization.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field by providing a strong foundation for future research on LLMs for graph-based tasks.  The insights on Code-as-Graph and heterophilic graphs are likely to inspire new methods and applications. The comprehensive evaluation methodology can serve as a model for future studies in this area.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper's significant contributions, rigorous methodology, actionable insights, and challenge to existing assumptions clearly elevate it above a simple "performance comparison" paper. While limitations like computational cost discussion and LLM architecture diversity exist, the breadth and depth of the analysis, combined with its practical implications, warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Source-Free Domain Adaptive Semantic Segmentation of Remote Sensing Images with Diffusion-Guided Label Enrichment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18502v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjie Liu, Hongmin Liu, Lixin Zhang, Bin Fan
- **Abstract**: Research on unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) for semantic segmentation of remote sensing images has been extensively conducted. However, research on how to achieve domain adaptation in practical scenarios where source domain data is inaccessible namely, source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) remains limited. Self-training has been widely used in SFDA, which requires obtaining as many high-quality pseudo-labels as possible to train models on target domain data. Most existing methods optimize the entire pseudo-label set to obtain more supervisory information. However, as pseudo-label sets often contain substantial noise, simultaneously optimizing all labels is challenging. This limitation undermines the effectiveness of optimization approaches and thus restricts the performance of self-training. To address this, we propose a novel pseudo-label optimization framework called Diffusion-Guided Label Enrichment (DGLE), which starts from a few easily obtained high-quality pseudo-labels and propagates them to a complete set of pseudo-labels while ensuring the quality of newly generated labels. Firstly, a pseudo-label fusion method based on confidence filtering and super-resolution enhancement is proposed, which utilizes cross-validation of details and contextual information to obtain a small number of high-quality pseudo-labels as initial seeds. Then, we leverage the diffusion model to propagate incomplete seed pseudo-labels with irregular distributions due to its strong denoising capability for randomly distributed noise and powerful modeling capacity for complex distributions, thereby generating complete and high-quality pseudo-labels. This method effectively avoids the difficulty of directly optimizing the complete set of pseudo-labels, significantly improves the quality of pseudo-labels, and thus enhances the model's performance in the target domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of source-free domain adaptive semantic segmentation (SFDA) for remote sensing images.  It proposes a novel framework called Diffusion-Guided Label Enrichment (DGLE).  DGLE aims to overcome the limitations of traditional self-training methods in SFDA, which often struggle with noisy pseudo-labels. DGLE starts by generating a small set of high-quality pseudo-labels through a fusion method combining confidence filtering and super-resolution enhancement.  It then employs a diffusion model to propagate these initial seed labels, generating a complete set of high-quality pseudo-labels. Finally, these enriched pseudo-labels are used to refine the segmentation model on the target domain. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of DGLE on several benchmark datasets, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** The paper addresses a significant and practical challenge in remote sensing: adapting models to new domains when source data is unavailable due to privacy or storage constraints. SFDA is a crucial research direction.
*   **Novelty of Approach:** The DGLE framework introduces a novel combination of techniques. The use of a diffusion model for pseudo-label propagation is a creative way to overcome the limitations of traditional self-training. The initial pseudo-label fusion approach leveraging super-resolution shows thoughtful consideration.
*   **Sound Methodology:** The paper provides a clear and well-described methodology. The steps involved in DGLE are logically presented, making the approach easy to understand.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results are compelling. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmark datasets, surpassing previous methods by a reasonable margin.
*   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the DGLE framework. This strengthens the paper by demonstrating the effectiveness of the individual elements.
*   **Clarity of Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The authors clearly articulate the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions a balance between performance and efficiency, the computational cost of using diffusion models is known to be significant. The practical implications of this cost for real-world deployments might be a concern and warrant more detailed discussion. The paper mentions the experiments were conducted on a single RTX 3090, further emphasizing the potential compute requirement.
*   **Reliance on Super-Resolution:** The method relies on a pre-trained super-resolution model. While effective, it introduces an additional dependency. The performance of DGLE might be affected by the quality and generalizability of the chosen super-resolution model.
*   **Limited Novelty in Diffusion Model Application:** While the *application* of a diffusion model for pseudo-label *propagation* is novel in *this context*, the *diffusion models themselves* are a relatively mature technology. The core innovation lies in *how* they're used rather than introducing a fundamentally new diffusion model architecture. The discussion of DM's could be considered incremental in its scope as it adapts existing tech to a specific new purpose within SFDA, this point needs acknowledgement.
*   **Lack of broader application:** While several SFDA frameworks are compared against, the paper doesn't address alternative methods for *image enhancement* and compares to few *image enhancement* methods that could be used to support segmentation tasks
*  **Hyperparameter explanation needed:** the paper could benefit from a more detailed explaination of hyperparameters selection

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of SFDA for remote sensing image segmentation. It introduces a novel and effective framework that addresses the limitations of existing self-training approaches. The strong experimental results and clear methodology make DGLE a promising approach for practical applications. The paper's ideas are likely to stimulate further research in this area, especially in the use of diffusion models for pseudo-label generation and refinement.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper is well-executed, addresses a relevant problem, and achieves strong results. The innovation of using diffusion models for pseudo-label propagation is significant. However, the computational cost and reliance on a super-resolution model are limitations. Also, the application of diffusion models, while innovative in the context of SFDA, is not groundbreaking in the broader field of diffusion modeling. This combination of strong positives and notable but addressable drawbacks justifies a score of 7.5. The potential impact of the paper on the SFDA community is high.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Coherence-driven inference for cybersecurity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18520v1)
- **Authors**: Steve Huntsman
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) can compile weighted graphs on natural language data to enable automatic coherence-driven inference (CDI) relevant to red and blue team operations in cybersecurity. This represents an early application of automatic CDI that holds near- to medium-term promise for decision-making in cybersecurity and eventually also for autonomous blue team operations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Coherence-Driven Inference (CDI), combined with Large Language Models (LLMs), to cybersecurity tasks, specifically red and blue team operations. The core idea is to use LLMs to automatically construct weighted coherence graphs from natural language data (propositions about a cybersecurity situation).  CDI then reasons over these graphs to identify inconsistencies and prioritize information, aiding in decision-making. The paper presents toy examples and illustrates how LLMs can reconstruct coherence graphs and how CDI can be used to make inferences about operating systems, possible attacks (like LDAP enumeration), and potential courses of action. The approach aims to leverage the strengths of LLMs (rich representations, fast processing) and symbolic reasoning (CDI for hard computations, explainability), while mitigating the weaknesses of LLMs (hallucinations, inability to reason). The authors promote that their approach is explainable, versatile, capable of handling abstraction, ambiguity and uncertainty, is based on a flexible cognitive model grounded in decades of research and is generalizable by construction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

While the individual components (LLMs and CDI) are not new, their combination in this specific way for cybersecurity is relatively novel.  The idea of using LLMs for graph construction, followed by symbolic reasoning using CDI, represents an interesting architectural approach that the authors call neurosymbolic.

*   **Strengths:** The paper clearly identifies a problem (the limitations of LLMs in reasoning and the need for explainability in cybersecurity) and proposes a reasonable solution.  The use of median coherence graphs for robustness is a good practical consideration. The paper provides several toy examples to illustrate the concept and demonstrates its applicability to red and blue team operations.

*   **Weaknesses:** The toy examples are, well, toy examples. The complexity of real-world cybersecurity data is significantly higher than the propositions considered. The paper does not adequately address the scalability issues of CDI with larger graphs.  The performance benchmark provided refers to another paper. There is a lack of rigorous evaluation with real-world cybersecurity datasets or scenarios.  The claim that LLMs are "generally avoids hallucinations" is weakly supported and potentially misleading. While the paper addresses the potential ethics and limitations, it does so superficially.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to be significant if it can be scaled and validated in real-world settings.  The combination of LLMs and CDI offers a promising approach to improve cybersecurity decision-making. The explicit goal of creating explainable and interpretable AI systems is a crucial aspect in security.

*   **Strengths:** The approach promotes explainability, which is valuable in critical cybersecurity applications where trust and understanding are essential. The modularity of the approach (LLM + CDI) allows for flexibility and potential improvement of each component independently.

*   **Weaknesses:** The current state of the paper is primarily conceptual. The lack of real-world evaluation significantly limits its immediate impact. Many alternative methods may produce similar if not better performance. The focus on "median" rather than probabilistic treatment of uncertainty may reduce overall interpretability or result quality.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents an interesting idea with potential for impact, but it is primarily conceptual and lacks sufficient validation. The toy examples are useful for illustrating the approach, but they don't demonstrate its scalability or effectiveness in real-world cybersecurity scenarios. The absence of a robust evaluation and comparison with existing methods significantly limits its significance. I'm additionally skeptical that the benefits that the authors attribute to CDI cannot be obtained from other methodologies. The paper needs more experimentation and an improved discussion of limitations. I am choosing a low score to emphasize the current limitations, but am cognizant that future work could increase the impact of this paper.

Score: 3

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 3/10

### Automatic coherence-driven inference on arguments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18523v1)
- **Authors**: Steve Huntsman
- **Abstract**: Inconsistencies are ubiquitous in law, administration, and jurisprudence. Though a cure is too much to hope for, we propose a technological remedy. Large language models (LLMs) can accurately extract propositions from arguments and compile them into natural data structures that enable coherence-driven inference (CDI) via combinatorial optimization. This neurosymbolic architecture naturally separates concerns and enables meaningful judgments about the coherence of arguments that can inform legislative and policy analysis and legal reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a neurosymbolic architecture called coherence-driven inference (CDI) to address inconsistencies in areas like law, administration, and jurisprudence. It leverages large language models (LLMs) to extract propositions from arguments and structure them into coherence graphs.  These graphs represent relationships between propositions, and combinatorial optimization techniques are then used to identify the most coherent partitions of the graph, essentially identifying which data should be accepted or rejected to minimize inconsistencies. The paper argues that this approach separates local inference (done by LLMs) from global coherence optimization, offering a verifiable and trustworthy AI system. The authors illustrate the approach with several examples, including analyzing arguments from a hypothetical Oval Office meeting, the Melian Dialogue, the play Inherit the Wind, and the Brown v. Board of Education case. They provide evidence that LLMs can accurately assess the consistency of propositions and that CDI can reveal underlying fault lines in arguments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several existing ideas into a practical, functional architecture. Using LLMs for proposition extraction and consistency scoring is not entirely new (as acknowledged by the authors), but applying combinatorial optimization on these LLM outputs for CDI, coupled with a focus on arguments in legal and policy domains, constitutes a significant step forward. The explicit separation of concerns (local LLM inference vs. global optimization) is also a notable feature. The authors present empirical evidence for the effectiveness of LLMs in extracting coherence graphs.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work is substantial. Inconsistent arguments are a pervasive problem in law and policy.  A system that can automatically identify and highlight these inconsistencies could be a valuable tool for policymakers, legal professionals, and the public.  The explainability of CDI, stemming from the interpretable nature of coherence graphs and the ability to compare near-optimal solutions, is a key advantage.  The focus on reproducibility and stability (achieved through median aggregation) also enhances the practical utility of the approach.  The authors make a strong case for the practical applicability of CDI even with current LLM capabilities.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inconsistency in arguments.
*   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The CDI architecture is well-defined and motivated.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides empirical evidence (though limited) to support the claims regarding LLM performance and the utility of CDI.
*   **Relevant Examples:** The examples are well-chosen and illustrate the potential of CDI to address real-world problems.
*   **Explainability:** The approach emphasizes explainability, which is crucial for adoption in legal and policy contexts.
*   **Clear writing:** The paper is well-written and organized.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scale and Scope of Experiments:** The empirical evaluation is primarily illustrative. A more comprehensive evaluation with larger datasets and more diverse arguments would strengthen the claims. The experiments are restricted to few real scenarios.
*   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The performance of CDI is highly dependent on the quality of the LLM's outputs.  While the authors demonstrate good LLM performance on consistency assessment, there's still a risk of bias or errors in the LLM's judgments. A thorough robustness analysis of the architecture relative to LLM quality would be beneficial.
*   **Manual Edge Alignment:** As mentioned in the text, manual alignment of edges in coherence graphs is performed, which undermines some of the automation that the authors were trying to achieve.
*   **Simplifications in Graph Structure:** The quantization of weights in coherence graphs (e.g., in §III-A) and the graph structure selection might lead to oversimplifications. There is a trade-off between computational tractability and expressiveness.

**Potential Influence:**

If the approach can be scaled up and validated more rigorously, it could have a significant influence on the field of computational argumentation, particularly in legal and policy domains. It could also stimulate further research on neurosymbolic AI architectures and the integration of LLMs with combinatorial optimization techniques. The paper's emphasis on explainability and verifiability aligns with the growing demand for trustworthy AI systems.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and promising architecture for coherence-driven inference. The practical examples demonstrate the potential of the approach to address a real-world problem. While there are several aspects of the design that would benefit from significant improvement and further investigation through more comprehensive evaluation, the paper's potential for impact is undeniable. The authors successfully bridge the gap between LLM-driven inference and classic combinatorial optimization techniques to create an architecture that is both explainable and automated. The score reflects the relative novelty and potential significance, while acknowledging the limitations in the empirical evaluation and dependence on LLM reliability.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### CCQA: Generating Question from Solution Can Improve Inference-Time Reasoning in SLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18536v1)
- **Authors**: Jin Young Kim, Ji Won Yoon
- **Abstract**: Recently, inference-time reasoning strategies have further improved the accuracy of large language models (LLMs), but their effectiveness on smaller models remains unclear. Based on the observation that conventional approaches often fail to improve performance in this context, we propose \textbf{C}ycle-\textbf{C}onsistency in \textbf{Q}uestion \textbf{A}nswering (CCQA), a novel reasoning method that can be effectively applied to SLMs. Inspired by cycle consistency, CCQA generates a question from each reasoning path and answer, evaluates each by its similarity to the original question, and then selects the candidate solution with the highest similarity score as the final response. Since conventional SLMs struggle to generate accurate questions from their own reasoning paths and answers, we employ a lightweight Flan-T5 model specialized for question generation to support this process efficiently. From the experimental results, it is verified that CCQA consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods across eight models on mathematical and commonsense reasoning benchmarks. Furthermore, our method establishes a new practical baseline for efficient reasoning in SLMs. Source code can be found at https://github.com/scai-research/ccqa_official.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Cycle-Consistency in Question Answering (CCQA), a novel inference-time reasoning method for Small Language Models (SLMs). CCQA generates a question from the solution provided by the SLM, then measures the similarity between the generated question and the original question. The solution with the highest similarity score is selected as the final answer.  A fine-tuned Flan-T5 model is used for question generation since conventional SLMs struggle with this task. The experimental results on mathematical and commonsense reasoning benchmarks show that CCQA outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods across various SLMs.

**Novelty and Significance Assessment:**

The paper proposes a clever approach (CCQA) to improve the reasoning ability of SLMs, leveraging the principle of cycle consistency.  The core idea - generating a question from a solution and comparing it to the original question - is intuitive and provides a mechanism for evaluating the quality of the SLM's reasoning path, something that's not directly addressed by many existing methods.  The use of a separate question generation model (fine-tuned Flan-T5) is justified by the SLM's limitations in generating accurate questions, and contributes to the overall practicality of the approach.  The experimental results show consistent improvements over existing methods like CoT, Self-Correction, SC, and USC across several benchmarks and SLMs, suggesting that CCQA is effective in practice.  The analysis of performance under LCV (Low Confidence Voting) conditions provides insights into CCQA's robustness when SLMs produce inconsistent outputs, a common issue.

However, the paper also has some limitations. The heavy reliance on a fine-tuned Flan-T5 model could be seen as a potential drawback, as it introduces an additional dependency and model size overhead. While the paper argues that this overhead is acceptable given the SLM limitations, it does reduce the overall "smallness" of the system. Also, the gains, while consistent, are not always dramatic; some datasets exhibit only marginal improvements. While novel, this method is a band-aid solution to the core issues of SLMs. The novelty is also somewhat incremental; cycle consistency has been used in other domains, and this paper adapts it to SLM reasoning.

While the paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating a practical method for improving SLM reasoning, the improvements over the strongest baselines are in some cases marginal. The design choice of employing a separate question generation model adds complexity and resources which dilutes the overall significance of the method.

### Solving Math Word Problems Using Estimation Verification and Equation Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18565v1)
- **Authors**: Mitchell Piehl, Dillon Wilson, Ananya Kalita, Jugal Kalita
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at various tasks, including problem-solving and question-answering. However, LLMs often find Math Word Problems (MWPs) challenging because solving them requires a range of reasoning and mathematical abilities with which LLMs seem to struggle. Recent efforts have helped LLMs solve more complex MWPs with improved prompts. This study proposes a novel method that initially prompts an LLM to create equations from a decomposition of the question, followed by using an external symbolic equation solver to produce an answer. To ensure the accuracy of the obtained answer, inspired by an established recommendation of math teachers, the LLM is instructed to solve the MWP a second time, but this time with the objective of estimating the correct answer instead of solving it exactly. The estimation is then compared to the generated answer to verify. If verification fails, an iterative rectification process is employed to ensure the correct answer is eventually found. This approach achieves new state-of-the-art results on datasets used by prior published research on numeric and algebraic MWPs, improving the previous best results by nearly two percent on average. In addition, the approach obtains satisfactory results on trigonometric MWPs, a task not previously attempted to the authors' best knowledge. This study also introduces two new datasets, SVAMPClean and Trig300, to further advance the testing of LLMs' reasoning abilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Solving Math Word Problems Using Estimation Verification and Equation Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method, EVOSS (Equation Verification of Symbolic Solvers), to improve the accuracy of Large Language Models (LLMs) in solving Math Word Problems (MWPs). The approach involves three key steps: 1) Decomposing the MWP into simpler statements and generating equations using an LLM; 2) Solving the generated equations using an external symbolic solver; and 3) Verifying the answer by prompting the LLM to estimate the answer and comparing the estimation with the symbolic solver's result.  If the answer and estimate don't align, a rectification process is triggered.  The authors claim state-of-the-art results on established MWP datasets (GSM8K, SVAMP, Algebra) and present a new trigonometry-focused dataset (Trig300) and a cleaned version of SVAMP (SVAMPClean).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several techniques into a cohesive framework, especially the *integration of answer estimation as a verification mechanism*. While LLMs have been used for equation generation and external solvers are a standard component, the use of estimation for verification, inspired by mathematical pedagogical practices, is a unique contribution.  The introduction of the Trig300 and SVAMPClean datasets, while not groundbreaking individually, provides valuable resources for further research. The idea to decompose the question into components is not entirely novel as the BRIDGE method also does this.

**Significance:** The improvement in accuracy on existing datasets is a significant outcome.  Reaching state-of-the-art results, even if the margin of improvement is "nearly two percent on average" suggests a genuine advantage in problem-solving. The introduction of a trigonometry dataset adds a new dimension to MWP research and could stimulate more work on non-standard MWPs. The method builds upon the existing landscape of prompt engineering techniques in order to overcome error prone equation generation from LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, experiments, and results.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The authors provide solid empirical evidence to support their claims, with comparisons against multiple baselines and ablation studies to understand the contribution of each component.
*   **Practical Inspiration:** The estimation-based verification step is grounded in sound pedagogical principles, making the approach intuitively appealing.
*   **New Resources:** The Trig300 and SVAMPClean datasets are valuable additions to the research community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvement in accuracy is significant but relatively small. It demonstrates solid progress, but does not represent a paradigm shift.
*   **Dependence on External Solver:** The reliance on a symbolic solver limits the applicability of the method to problems that can be expressed in a symbolic form and does not allow it to tackle more reasoning based problems.
*   **Limited Exploration of Failure Cases:** While the paper discusses rectification, it doesn't delve deeply into the specific types of errors that occur and how the rectification process addresses them. A more detailed error analysis would strengthen the paper.
*   **No comparison against fine-tuned models:** The paper focuses on prompt engineering and does not compare the approach to the performance obtained after fine-tuning a language model. A comparison would provide a clearer picture of the improvement obtained using the method.
*   **The performance on Trig300 is good for EVOSS but generally low:** The baseline methods achieve a very low accuracy on the trigonometry dataset, which raises the question of whether those are indeed strong baselines.

**Potential Influence:** The EVOSS method could influence future research by encouraging the exploration of verification techniques in MWP solving.  The Trig300 dataset has the potential to become a benchmark for trigonometry-related problems.

**Justification for Score:**
The paper presents a well-executed and novel approach to MWP solving with verifiable empirical gains. While the improvement over state-of-the-art is incremental, the creative application of estimation as a verification mechanism, the construction of new datasets, and solid empirical evaluations are worthwhile contributions. The dependence on symbolic solvers is a limitation, but understandable for the method proposed.

### Explainable Graph Neural Networks: Understanding Brain Connectivity and Biomarkers in Dementia
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18568v1)
- **Authors**: Niharika Tewari, Nguyen Linh Dan Le, Mujie Liu, Jing Ren, Ziqi Xu, Tabinda Sarwar, Veeky Baths, Feng Xia
- **Abstract**: Dementia is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder with multiple etiologies, including Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease, frontotemporal dementia, and vascular dementia. Its clinical and biological heterogeneity makes diagnosis and subtype differentiation highly challenging. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have recently shown strong potential in modeling brain connectivity, but their limited robustness, data scarcity, and lack of interpretability constrain clinical adoption. Explainable Graph Neural Networks (XGNNs) have emerged to address these barriers by combining graph-based learning with interpretability, enabling the identification of disease-relevant biomarkers, analysis of brain network disruptions, and provision of transparent insights for clinicians. This paper presents the first comprehensive review dedicated to XGNNs in dementia research. We examine their applications across Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and multi-disease diagnosis. A taxonomy of explainability methods tailored for dementia-related tasks is introduced, alongside comparisons of existing models in clinical scenarios. We also highlight challenges such as limited generalizability, underexplored domains, and the integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) for early detection. By outlining both progress and open problems, this review aims to guide future work toward trustworthy, clinically meaningful, and scalable use of XGNNs in dementia research.
- **Summary**: This paper provides a comprehensive review of Explainable Graph Neural Networks (XGNNs) applied to dementia research. It examines XGNN applications in Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and multi-disease diagnosis, while introducing a taxonomy of explainability methods tailored for dementia-related tasks and highlighting challenges such as limited generalizability and integration with Large Language Models (LLMs) for early detection. The review aims to guide future work toward trustworthy, clinically meaningful, and scalable XGNN use in dementia research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty and Significance:** The paper's primary strength lies in its comprehensive synthesis of XGNN applications in the specific context of dementia research. It's presented as the first dedicated review of this topic, which positions it as a valuable resource for researchers entering or already working in the intersection of graph-based learning and neurodegenerative disease. The taxonomy of explainability methods is a useful contribution for organizing and understanding the different approaches. However, the individual components (GNNs, XAI, dementia research) are not novel *per se*; the novelty stems from the integrated perspective and the specific focus.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensiveness:** The paper surveys a significant body of literature, providing a broad overview of XGNN applications in various dementia subtypes.
*   **Taxonomy of Explainability Methods:** The introduced taxonomy provides a structured framework for understanding and comparing different XAI techniques in the context of dementia research. This could be highly useful for researchers choosing appropriate methods.
*   **Focus on Clinical Relevance:** The paper consistently emphasizes the clinical applicability and interpretability of XGNNs, highlighting their potential for biomarker identification, disease characterization, and transparent decision-making.
*   **Identification of Challenges:** The discussion of limitations (generalizability, data scarcity, etc.) and future research directions adds value by pinpointing areas where further work is needed.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the review is thorough, it lacks empirical validation of the proposed taxonomy or comparative assessment of different XGNN methods beyond summarizing existing results.
*   **Depth of Technical Detail:** The description of individual XGNN architectures and explainability techniques may be somewhat superficial for experts in either GNNs or XAI. The paper primarily focuses on the *application* of these methods rather than their technical intricacies.
*   **Overstated Novelty:** While it claims to be the "first comprehensive review", there's always a risk of similar efforts existing, even if less extensive. Thorough investigation to prove the statement is imperative.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Guiding Future Research:** By outlining current progress and open problems, the review can steer researchers toward promising avenues for XGNN development and application in dementia research.
*   **Facilitating Interdisciplinary Collaboration:** The paper bridges the gap between machine learning and clinical neuroscience, potentially fostering collaboration between researchers from different backgrounds.
*   **Promoting Trustworthy AI:** By emphasizing explainability and clinical relevance, the review can contribute to the development of more trustworthy and clinically useful AI tools for dementia diagnosis and management.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper provides a useful synthesis and organization of existing knowledge, its novelty is primarily in the focused combination of existing techniques rather than the introduction of fundamentally new methods or theoretical insights. The lack of empirical validation or in-depth technical analysis also limits its impact to some degree. Therefore, while valuable, it doesn't represent a groundbreaking advance.

### Explore the Reinforcement Learning for the LLM based ASR and TTS system
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18569v1)
- **Authors**: Changfeng Gao, Yabin Li, Keyu An, Zhifu Gao, Zhihao Du, Han Zhao, Xiangang Li
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large language models (LLMs) have played an important role in automatic speech recognition (ASR) and text-to-speech (TTS) systems. While reinforcement learning (RL) has significantly enhanced LLM performance in text-based tasks, its application to ASR and TTS remains underexplored due to the complexity of training audio-based models. In this study, we propose a lightweight RL framework tailored for audio-based LLMs that can process audio inputs and generate audio outputs. Based on this framework, we evaluate the effectiveness of reinforcement learning on both ASR and TTS tasks. For the ASR task, we experiment with different rule-based reward functions within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework and investigate the impact of RL data construction. For the TTS task, we compare GRPO with Differentiable Reward Optimization (DiffRO) and further combine the two approaches to achieve improved performance. Our experiments demonstrate that RL can significantly enhance the performance of both ASR and TTS systems, even with limited training data and a small number of optimization steps.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of reinforcement learning (RL) techniques to improve the performance of Large Language Model (LLM)-based Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) systems. It introduces a lightweight RL framework designed for audio LLMs that handles audio inputs and generates audio outputs or text tokens. For ASR, the paper compares different rule-based reward functions within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework and investigates the impact of RL data construction strategies. For TTS, it compares GRPO with Differentiable Reward Optimization (DiffRO) and combines the two for enhanced performance. The experiments demonstrate that RL can improve the performance of both ASR and TTS, even with limited data and optimization steps, highlighting the importance of reward design and RL data construction. The authors also present a framework to efficiently allocate GPU resources, improving the training speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper offers incremental but valuable contributions to the field. The exploration of RL for LLM-based ASR and TTS is not entirely novel, as prior works exist. However, the paper's novelty lies in:

*   **Lightweight RL Framework:** The proposed framework specifically designed for audio LLMs, addresses the complexities of processing audio and managing computational resources for ASR and TTS.
*   **Detailed Comparison of RL Techniques:** The paper conducts a more comprehensive analysis of RL techniques (GRPO, DiffRO, and their combination) on both ASR and TTS compared to many existing works, particularly focusing on reward engineering and RL data construction.  Many studies focus on which RL methods were used without doing an in-depth analysis.
*   **GPU Resource Management:**  The design of a resource-efficient training method is novel and significant.  It reduces overall training time by improving utilization of devices.

**Significance:**

The significance of the work stems from:

*   **Practicality:** The focus on efficiency (lightweight framework, GPU resource management) makes the methods more practical for real-world application and deployment of LLM-based speech systems.
*   **Improved Performance:** The demonstrated improvements in ASR and TTS through RL, even with limited resources, are valuable and encourage further research in this area.  It addresses the problem of the LLM-based ASR system could change keywords or fall in hallucinations which serious impact on the user experience.
*   **Insights into Reward Design & Data Construction:**  The paper provides useful insights into how reward functions and data construction impact RL performance in audio-based LLMs, which can guide future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem & Objectives:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of applying RL to audio LLMs and sets specific objectives.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are reasonably thorough, covering different RL techniques, reward functions, and data construction strategies.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the experimental results, highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The use of "small but high-quality" datasets raises questions about the generalizability of the results to larger, more diverse datasets.  The potential impact of the "high-quality" selection process on the results should have been addressed.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are positive, the improvements achieved might be considered incremental rather than groundbreaking.
*   **Subjective Evaluation:** Some aspects, such as the improvement in "subjective experience" when combining GRPO and DiffRO, could benefit from more rigorous evaluation.
*   **Lack of Ablation on Framework Components:** The paper doesn't fully ablate different components of its GPU resource management framework to understand each component's contribution.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence research in the following areas:

*   Development of more efficient and effective RL frameworks for audio LLMs.
*   Design of reward functions and data construction strategies for RL in ASR and TTS.
*   Integration of RL into practical, real-world speech processing systems.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the application of RL in audio-based LLMs, but its novelty and overall impact are limited. The paper addresses the limitation of existing studies in this area and provides a more comprehensive analysis. It offers practical insights into resource management and reward engineering. However, the experimental results do not appear to be statistically significant and do not generalize well. The impact is further limited by the limited dataset sizes.

### HarmoniFuse: A Component-Selective and Prompt-Adaptive Framework for Multi-Task Speech Language Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18570v1)
- **Authors**: Yuke Si, Runyan Yang, Yingying Gao, Junlan Feng, Chao Deng, Shilei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models have facilitated the development of unified speech language models (SLMs) capable of supporting multiple speech tasks within a shared architecture. However, tasks such as automatic speech recognition (ASR) and speech emotion recognition (SER) rely on distinct types of information: ASR primarily depends on linguistic content, whereas SER requires the integration of both linguistic and paralinguistic cues. Existing multitask SLMs typically adopt naive parameter sharing or prompt-based conditioning without explicitly modeling the differences in information composition required by each task. Such designs risk task interference and performance degradation, especially under limited data conditions. To address these limitations, we propose HarmoniFuse, a component-selective and prompt-adaptive framework for multi-task speech language modeling. HarmoniFuse is designed to harmonize heterogeneous task demands by selecting and fusing task-relevant components of speech representations. Specifically, it integrates a gated speech encoder to extract task-specific acoustic features and a prompt-adaptive dynamic fusion module to aggregate transformer layers based on task characteristics. In addition, a batch-interleaved training strategy enables leveraging separate ASR and SER datasets without requiring joint annotation. Experimental results demonstrate that HarmoniFuse improves both ASR and SER performance, offering a scalable and robust solution for multitask speech understanding under realistic data constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HarmoniFuse, a novel framework for multi-task speech language modeling (SLM) that aims to improve performance on tasks like Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Speech Emotion Recognition (SER).  HarmoniFuse addresses the challenge that these tasks rely on different information types (ASR primarily on linguistic content, SER on both linguistic and paralinguistic cues). The framework includes: a gated speech encoder for extracting task-specific acoustic features, a prompt-adaptive dynamic fusion module to aggregate transformer layers based on task characteristics, and a batch-interleaved training strategy to leverage separate ASR and SER datasets.  Experimental results demonstrate that HarmoniFuse improves both ASR and SER performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its integrated approach to address task interference in multi-task speech learning, particularly for ASR and SER.  The component-selective encoder (gated layer fusion) and the prompt-adaptive dynamic layer fusion module are the core novel elements. While individual components like gated architectures and attention mechanisms are not entirely new, their specific integration and application to dynamically adapt to ASR and SER task demands is a meaningful contribution.  The batch-interleaved training, while a practical consideration, adds to the overall framework's utility.  The combination of these parts provides a more holistic design.

* **Significance:**  The significance of this work is that it tackles a practical problem: how to effectively train multi-task SLMs when the tasks have potentially conflicting information requirements and limited joint annotations.  The improvements in both ASR and SER, especially exceeding the single-task baselines, indicate the effectiveness of the HarmoniFuse design. The framework's adaptability and ability to be trained using disjoint datasets are crucial in real-world scenarios where labeled data for all tasks isn't always readily available. The approach could potentially inspire further research into more efficient and adaptable multi-task learning techniques in the speech domain.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of task interference in multi-task SLMs, especially for tasks like ASR and SER.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:** The HarmoniFuse framework is well-defined and its components are clearly explained.
    * **Demonstrated Improvements:** The experimental results show demonstrable improvements in both ASR and SER compared to baselines.
    * **Addresses Data Limitations:** The batch-interleaved training strategy effectively addresses the scarcity of joint ASR-SER data.
    * **Analysis of Model Behavior:** The visualization of layer weights provides valuable insight into how the model adapts to different tasks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the integration of the components is novel, the individual components themselves are based on existing techniques.  A truly ground-breaking architecture would likely represent a much larger leap forward.
    * **Limited Generalization Evidence:** The experiments focus solely on ASR and SER.  It would be beneficial to see how HarmoniFuse performs on a more diverse set of speech tasks (e.g., speaker recognition, language identification).
    * **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The paper relies on a pre-trained WavLM model. It would be beneficial to explore the performance of the framework with different pre-trained models or even trained from scratch, though such experiments would require significantly more resources.
    * **Computational Cost:**  The added complexity of the gated encoder and dynamic layer fusion likely increases the computational cost of training and inference.  The paper doesn't explicitly address this trade-off.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multi-task speech learning, particularly by highlighting the importance of task-aware information routing and dynamic adaptation.  It provides a solid foundation for further exploration of more sophisticated architectures and training strategies for multi-task SLMs. However, it's not a disruptive innovation.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty and significance, I assign the paper a score of **7**.  While the individual components are not entirely groundbreaking, the specific integration of these components into a cohesive framework (HarmoniFuse) to address task interference in multi-task learning is a valuable contribution.  The experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework, and the paper addresses a relevant problem in the field. The weaknesses primarily revolve around the incremental nature of the improvements and the limited generalizability of the experiments, preventing it from achieving a higher score.
