- **Score**: 8/10

### Understanding-in-Generation: Reinforcing Generative Capability of Unified Model via Infusing Understanding into Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18639v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanhuiyi Lyu, Chi Kit Wong, Chenfei Liao, Lutao Jiang, Xu Zheng, Zexin Lu, Linfeng Zhang, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: Recent works have made notable advancements in enhancing unified models for text-to-image generation through the Chain-of-Thought (CoT). However, these reasoning methods separate the processes of understanding and generation, which limits their ability to guide the reasoning of unified models in addressing the deficiencies of their generative capabilities. To this end, we propose a novel reasoning framework for unified models, Understanding-in-Generation (UiG), which harnesses the robust understanding capabilities of unified models to reinforce their performance in image generation. The core insight of our UiG is to integrate generative guidance by the strong understanding capabilities during the reasoning process, thereby mitigating the limitations of generative abilities. To achieve this, we introduce "Image Editing" as a bridge to infuse understanding into the generation process. Initially, we verify the generated image and incorporate the understanding of unified models into the editing instructions. Subsequently, we enhance the generated image step by step, gradually infusing the understanding into the generation process. Our UiG framework demonstrates a significant performance improvement in text-to-image generation over existing text-to-image reasoning methods, e.g., a 3.92% gain on the long prompt setting of the TIIF benchmark. The project code: https://github.com/QC-LY/UiG
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel reasoning framework called "Understanding-in-Generation" (UiG) to improve the text-to-image generation capabilities of unified models.  Unlike existing methods that separate understanding and generation, UiG integrates generative guidance during the reasoning process, leveraging the model's understanding to address limitations in its generative abilities.  UiG uses "Image Editing" as a bridge to infuse understanding into the generation, by verifying initial generated images, incorporating understanding into editing instructions, and iteratively enhancing the image. The authors demonstrate performance improvements on TIIF and WISE benchmarks compared to existing reasoning methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating the model's *understanding* back into the *generation* process during reasoning is a valuable contribution. Existing CoT methods largely treat understanding as a pre-processing or post-processing step. UiG’s iterative loop is a more tightly coupled approach. The use of image editing as a vehicle for guiding this process is a practical and technically sound choice.

*   **Significance:** While the relative improvements demonstrated on the benchmarks (e.g. 3.92% on TIIF) might seem modest at first glance, the underlying concept has significant implications. It suggests a pathway for more effectively harnessing the capabilities of unified models. The paper's findings could encourage further research into similar iterative refinement strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The UiG framework is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   The "Image Editing" bridge is a practical and implementable solution.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements.
    *   The qualitative results provide compelling visual evidence of the benefits of UiG.
    *   Ablation studies clarify the importance of image editing, increasing confidence in the approach.
    *   The paper emphasizes limitations and reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance gains, while present, aren’t overwhelmingly large.  A larger margin of improvement would strengthen the impact claim.
    *   While the authors ablate the `Image Editing` bridge, there is limited analysis of the impact of other components of the Understanding prompt.
    *   The user study provides some confirmation of the quantitative results, a more detailed and robust user study could provide stronger support.
    *   Reliance on a single base model (BAGEL) limits the generalizability claims. Demonstrating effectiveness across multiple base architectures (especially state-of-the-art ones) would strengthen the paper.
    *   The complexity of the Understanding prompt, while detailed in the appendix, might introduce some engineering challenges in other contexts.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective text-to-image generation techniques, especially those based on unified models. The proposed reasoning framework could be extended to other generation tasks, such as video generation or 3D modeling.

**Justification for the Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.  Here's why: The idea is significant and innovative. It addresses a known limitation in how unified models leverage their understanding in generation. While the quantitative improvements are noticeable, they aren’t transformative, and the demonstration rests largely on one base model architecture. Further experimentation, broader model support, and a more in-depth component analysis within understanding prompt are needed to validate the effectiveness and make the work more high-impact. Nevertheless, the approach introduces a useful strategy, the clarity of its execution is commendable, and results provide a solid foundation for further investigation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### BloomIntent: Automating Search Evaluation with LLM-Generated Fine-Grained User Intents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18641v1)
- **Authors**: Yoonseo Choi, Eunhye Kim, Hyunwoo Kim, Donghyun Park, Honggu Lee, Jinyoung Kim, Juho Kim
- **Abstract**: If 100 people issue the same search query, they may have 100 different goals. While existing work on user-centric AI evaluation highlights the importance of aligning systems with fine-grained user intents, current search evaluation methods struggle to represent and assess this diversity. We introduce BloomIntent, a user-centric search evaluation method that uses user intents as the evaluation unit. BloomIntent first generates a set of plausible, fine-grained search intents grounded on taxonomies of user attributes and information-seeking intent types. Then, BloomIntent provides an automated evaluation of search results against each intent powered by large language models. To support practical analysis, BloomIntent clusters semantically similar intents and summarizes evaluation outcomes in a structured interface. With three technical evaluations, we showed that BloomIntent generated fine-grained, evaluable, and realistic intents and produced scalable assessments of intent-level satisfaction that achieved 72% agreement with expert evaluators. In a case study (N=4), we showed that BloomIntent supported search specialists in identifying intents for ambiguous queries, uncovering underserved user needs, and discovering actionable insights for improving search experiences. By shifting from query-level to intent-level evaluation, BloomIntent reimagines how search systems can be assessed -- not only for performance but for their ability to serve a multitude of user goals.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Bloomintent paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BloomIntent, a novel method for automating search evaluation by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate fine-grained user intents. The core idea is to move beyond query-level evaluation and assess search engine results based on how well they satisfy a diverse set of plausible user intents derived from a single query. BloomIntent utilizes a two-stage pipeline: (1) intent generation, where LLMs, guided by user attributes and information-seeking taxonomies, generate realistic sentence-level intents; and (2) intent contextualization, where LLMs automatically evaluate search engine results pages (SERPs) against each intent. The method also incorporates semantic clustering and a web-based interface to summarize evaluation outcomes and identify patterns.  Technical evaluations and a case study with search specialists demonstrate the potential of BloomIntent in uncovering underserved user needs, identifying intent-specific issues, and providing actionable insights for improving search experiences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic approach to generating and using fine-grained user intents for search evaluation. While LLMs have been used in search evaluation before, BloomIntent distinguishes itself by its structured intent generation process, grounded in user attributes and information-seeking taxonomies, and its focus on diverse intent representation, and its evaluation of those intents within real user task contexts. The combination of LLM-based intent generation, automated evaluation, and a user-friendly interface for analyzing results is a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of search evaluation. Traditional methods often struggle to capture the diversity of user goals behind a single query. BloomIntent addresses this limitation by providing a scalable and user-centric approach that can uncover previously overlooked user needs and identify actionable insights for improving search experiences. The case study demonstrates that BloomIntent can help search specialists in identifying intents for ambiguous queries, uncovering underserved user needs, and discovering actionable insights.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic approach:** The paper provides a clear and well-defined methodology for generating and evaluating user intents.
    *   **Technical validation:** The technical evaluations provide evidence for the quality and reliability of the generated intents and evaluation results.
    *   **Practical utility:** The case study demonstrates the practical utility of BloomIntent in a real-world search evaluation setting.
    *   **User-friendly interface:** The web-based interface provides a structured and intuitive way for search specialists to analyze evaluation results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Moderate agreement with human judgments:** The automated evaluation pipeline showed moderate agreement with human judgments, particularly for borderline cases. This suggests that human oversight may still be needed for ambiguous cases.  It is worth noting that agreement between human experts was not perfect as well.
    *   **Limited scope:**  The implementation used search logs and SERPs from a Korean commercial search engine, which may limit the generalizability of the approach to other languages or search engines.  The focus was largely textual as well.
    *   **Potential for bias:** As acknowledged by the authors, LLM-generated intents may reflect social biases embedded in language models, potentially amplifying or overlooking the needs of certain user groups.

*   **Potential Influence:** BloomIntent could potentially influence the field by shifting the focus of search evaluation towards a more user-centric and intent-driven approach. It could also inspire new methods for leveraging LLMs in search evaluation and other areas of human-computer interaction. It also provides a platform upon which to more rigorously and scalably perform user studies, and better model user behavior.

*   **Score Justification**:
    While the paper presents a significant and novel approach to search evaluation, some limitations prevent it from scoring higher. The fact that automated evaluation has moderate agreement with human judgements reduces the confidence, and also some aspects of implementation limit generalizability.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Analyzing Uncertainty of LLM-as-a-Judge: Interval Evaluations with Conformal Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18658v1)
- **Authors**: Huanxin Sheng, Xinyi Liu, Hangfeng He, Jieyu Zhao, Jian Kang
- **Abstract**: LLM-as-a-judge has become a promising paradigm for using large language models (LLMs) to evaluate natural language generation (NLG), but the uncertainty of its evaluation remains underexplored. This lack of reliability may limit its deployment in many applications. This work presents the first framework to analyze the uncertainty by offering a prediction interval of LLM-based scoring via conformal prediction. Conformal prediction constructs continuous prediction intervals from a single evaluation run, and we design an ordinal boundary adjustment for discrete rating tasks. We also suggest a midpoint-based score within the interval as a low-bias alternative to raw model score and weighted average. We perform extensive experiments and analysis, which show that conformal prediction can provide valid prediction interval with coverage guarantees. We also explore the usefulness of interval midpoint and judge reprompting for better judgment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper analyzes the uncertainty associated with using Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges for evaluating Natural Language Generation (NLG) tasks. The authors propose a novel framework that leverages conformal prediction to provide prediction intervals for LLM-based scoring. They design an ordinal boundary adjustment for discrete rating tasks and suggest a midpoint-based score within the interval as a low-bias alternative to raw model scores or weighted averages. The framework is tested across several evaluation benchmarks in summarization, dialogue summarization, and reasoning, demonstrating its ability to provide valid prediction intervals with coverage guarantees. The paper explores the effects of different LLM judges, prompting strategies, calibration data size, and other factors on the quality of prediction intervals. The analysis advocates a shift from direct scoring to uncertainty-aware evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is, to the authors' knowledge, the first to directly address uncertainty quantification in LLM-as-a-judge paradigms using conformal prediction in rating-based evaluation tasks. This represents a significant contribution, as the reliability of LLM-based evaluations remains a critical concern. The boundary adjustment technique and the investigation into the midpoint score as a better estimate than raw scores also adds to the novelty. Prior work has focused more on pairwise comparison, risk control and classification tasks rather than providing prediction intervals in a discrete rating context, which this paper directly addresses.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM-based evaluations. By quantifying uncertainty, the framework allows for more informed decision-making and helps users understand the limitations of LLM judges. This is crucial for deployment in scenarios where the consequences of unreliable evaluation are severe (e.g., healthcare, finance). The practical insights gained into how various aspects of the LLM judge (choice of LLM, prompting, calibration data) affect interval quality also provides value to the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound Methodology:** The use of conformal prediction provides statistical coverage guarantees, making it a robust and theoretically grounded approach to uncertainty quantification.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper conducts thorough experiments across a range of tasks, datasets, LLMs, and conformal prediction methods, allowing for comparative analysis and generalizable insights.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The proposed boundary adjustment significantly improves coverage in rating-based evaluations, and the recommendation of interval midpoint enhances the reliability of estimates.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, and the experimental results are thoroughly explained, making the findings accessible.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Exchangeability Assumption:**  The validity of conformal prediction relies on the exchangeability of calibration and test data. The paper acknowledges that this assumption may not always hold, particularly when there are potential distribution shifts. While the impact of distribution shift is briefly explored, further investigation into mitigating its effects could strengthen the framework.
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** Although the paper covers different scenarios in summarization and reasoning, it acknowledges that other NLG and evaluation tasks are yet to be explored. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Computational Considerations:** Some conformal prediction methods (Boosted CQR, Boosted LCP, and LVD) have significantly higher computation costs. More discussions and trade-offs between various methods are highly valuable, especially on their impacts to downstream applications in real-time settings.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in LLM-based evaluation. It establishes a concrete approach to uncertainty quantification and provides a roadmap for analyzing and improving the reliability of LLM judges. Future work could build on this framework to explore more advanced conformal prediction techniques, address distributional shift, and investigate other task areas. It could also facilitate the creation of more reliable and trustworthy AI systems that rely on LLM-based evaluations.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a significant contribution to the field of LLM-based evaluation by addressing a critical gap in the literature: uncertainty quantification. While the methodology and experiments are strong, the reliance on the exchangeability assumption and limited task scope prevents it from receiving an even higher score. However, the paper represents a solid foundation for future research and has the potential to drive the development of more reliable and trustworthy AI systems.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TERAG: Token-Efficient Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18667v1)
- **Authors**: Qiao Xiao, Hong Ting Tsang, Jiaxin Bai
- **Abstract**: Graph-based Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has become a widely studied approach for improving the reasoning, accuracy, and factuality of Large Language Models. However, many existing graph-based RAG systems overlook the high cost associated with LLM token usage during graph construction, hindering large-scale adoption. To address this, we propose TERAG, a simple yet effective framework designed to build informative graphs at a significantly lower cost. Inspired by HippoRAG, we incorporate Personalized PageRank (PPR) during the retrieval phase, and we achieve at least 80% of the accuracy of widely used graph-based RAG methods while consuming only 3%-11% of the output tokens.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TERAG: Token-Efficient Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TERAG, a graph-based RAG framework designed to minimize LLM token consumption during knowledge graph construction. It achieves this by using carefully designed prompts to extract multi-level concepts, structuring them into a knowledge graph using efficient, non-LLM methods. The retrieval phase utilizes Personalized PageRank (PPR) on the constructed graph.  The authors demonstrate TERAG's ability to reduce output token usage by 89-97% compared to other graph-based RAG methods while maintaining competitive retrieval accuracy on multi-hop question-answering benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit focus on token efficiency during graph construction *without* sacrificing retrieval accuracy. While other works have tackled RAG efficiency, TERAG's approach of lightweight concept extraction with carefully crafted prompts combined with non-LLM graph construction represents a practical engineering approach. The idea of using PPR is not new, but its integration with the specifically designed graph, coupled with a novel combination of semantic relevance and concept frequency in the restart vector, shows incremental but meaningful innovation. Compared with methods focusing on structurally simple graphs, TERAG argues for efficiency primarily via decreased LLM usage during *creation*.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical barrier that high token consumption poses to the widespread adoption of graph-based RAG. TERAG directly addresses this bottleneck. The demonstrated reduction in token usage, coupled with competitive accuracy, makes graph-based RAG more accessible and scalable, especially for resource-constrained environments. The benchmarks used are well-established, and the comparison to strong baselines (including AutoSchemaKG) strengthens the claim of TERAG's effectiveness. The performance on multiple datasets also makes the claim more generalizable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of high token costs in graph-based RAG.
    *   **Practical Approach:** TERAG offers a practical, implementable solution that can be readily adopted.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the token efficiency of TERAG while maintaining competitive accuracy.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study clarifies the importance of their combined semantic relevance and frequency weighting within the PPR.
    *   Concise and clear language throughout the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Innovation:**  The individual components (concept extraction, PPR, graph construction) are not entirely novel. The primary contribution is in their *combination* and engineering for token efficiency.
    *   **Limited Scope of Graph Structure:** The graph structure is intentionally simplified, which could limit its effectiveness for certain knowledge-intensive tasks requiring richer relational information. Although defended due to the goal of token efficiency, a discussion on the tradeoffs of expressiveness and token efficiency would add merit to the work.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for concept extraction:** LLMs are used for both concept extraction and answer generation, potentially introducing a dependency on the LLM's capabilities and biases. A discussion of this and possible solutions would add merit to the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** TERAG has the potential to influence the development of more practical and scalable graph-based RAG systems. The focus on token efficiency could inspire other researchers to explore alternative graph construction and retrieval techniques that minimize LLM usage. The method can be incorporated into existing RAG systems, expanding its reach.

**Justification of Score:**

While TERAG's components are not revolutionary individually, their specific combination and engineering towards token efficiency represent a valuable contribution to the field of RAG. The empirical results are strong and compelling, demonstrating that TERAG can significantly reduce token consumption without a major accuracy drop. Its practical nature and potential for wider adoption outweigh the incremental nature of the technical contributions. The paper is well-written and thoroughly evaluates TERAG's performance.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalable bayesian shadow tomography for quantum property estimation with set transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18674v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunho Cha, Wonjung Kim, Jungwoo Lee
- **Abstract**: A scalable Bayesian machine learning framework is introduced for estimating scalar properties of an unknown quantum state from measurement data, which bypasses full density matrix reconstruction. This work is the first to integrate the classical shadows protocol with a permutation-invariant set transformer architecture, enabling the approach to predict and correct bias in existing estimators to approximate the true Bayesian posterior mean. Measurement outcomes are encoded as fixed-dimensional feature vectors, and the network outputs a residual correction to a baseline estimator. Scalability to large quantum systems is ensured by the polynomial dependence of input size on system size and number of measurements. On Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger state fidelity and second-order R\'enyi entropy estimation tasks -- using random Pauli and random Clifford measurements -- this Bayesian estimator always achieves lower mean squared error than classical shadows alone, with more than a 99\% reduction in the few copy regime.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a scalable Bayesian machine learning framework for estimating scalar properties of quantum states, bypassing the need for full density matrix reconstruction. It combines the classical shadows protocol with a permutation-invariant set transformer architecture.  The network learns to predict and correct biases in existing estimators (like classical shadows) to approximate the Bayesian posterior mean. Input consists of fixed-dimensional feature vectors encoded from measurement outcomes, enabling polynomial scaling with system size and number of measurements. The framework is demonstrated on Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state fidelity and second-order Rényi entropy estimation tasks, showing improved mean squared error compared to classical shadows alone, especially in the few-copy regime.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central novelty lies in the integration of classical shadows with a set transformer architecture within a Bayesian framework. While Bayesian approaches to quantum state estimation exist, and classical shadows is a known technique, the combination and use of a permutation-invariant set transformer for bias correction appears to be a novel contribution. The authors claim this is the first Bayesian and scalable machine learning approach that directly estimates scalar functions of an unknown quantum state without performing full state reconstruction. This claim is supported by the paper.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is in providing a computationally efficient approach for estimating quantum state properties in large systems. Bypassing full density matrix reconstruction is crucial for scalability.  The demonstrated improvement over classical shadows, especially in the data-scarce regime, is a promising result.  Furthermore, the method provides a principled way to incorporate prior information about the state, which standard classical shadows lacks. The authors also address noisy measurement scenarios and detail their calibration mechanism, suggesting an attention to practical considerations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:** The polynomial dependence on system size and measurement number is a significant advantage, crucial for practical applications.
    *   **Performance Improvement:** The demonstrated reduction in MSE compared to classical shadows in the tested scenarios (GHZ fidelity and Rényi entropy) supports the effectiveness of the method.  The emphasis on the few-copy regime is particularly relevant for near-term quantum devices.
    *   **Principled Approach:**  The Bayesian framework allows for the incorporation of prior knowledge, a key feature missing in classical shadows.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and structured, clearly outlining the method, experiments, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Data Dependency:** As a data-driven method, the performance relies heavily on the availability of a large, accurately labeled training dataset. Generating this dataset, which requires almost exact knowledge of the target property, could be a significant bottleneck. The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Benchmarking:** While the authors compare against classical shadows, a stronger evaluation would involve comparing against other state-of-the-art quantum state estimation techniques. The "only directly comparable baseline" argument is somewhat limiting.
    *   **Scope of Tested Properties:** The evaluation is limited to GHZ state fidelity and Rényi entropy. It's unclear how well the method generalizes to other types of scalar properties and quantum states.
    *   **Inference Time:** While low for the *residual* portion, it does not include the classical shadows estimation step which needs to be considered for fair comparison.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of quantum state estimation by providing a practical and scalable Bayesian method. The integration of set transformers and residual learning provides a novel approach that can be built upon.  However, the data dependency issue needs to be addressed for wider applicability. The paper will also likely spark further research into adaptive Bayesian estimation techniques.

**Score:** 7.

**Justification:** The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution by merging classical shadows with set transformers in a Bayesian framework. The achieved improvements in scalability and performance are promising. However, the data dependency, limited benchmarking, and relatively narrow scope of tested properties temper the overall impact. While the work is valuable, further investigation and validation with more diverse quantum states and comparison against a wider range of estimation techniques is warranted.

- **Classification**: quant-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### Harnessing Multimodal Large Language Models for Personalized Product Search with Query-aware Refinement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18682v1)
- **Authors**: Beibei Zhang, Yanan Lu, Ruobing Xie, Zongyi Li, Siyuan Xing, Tongwei Ren, Fen Lin
- **Abstract**: Personalized product search (PPS) aims to retrieve products relevant to the given query considering user preferences within their purchase histories. Since large language models (LLM) exhibit impressive potential in content understanding and reasoning, current methods explore to leverage LLM to comprehend the complicated relationships among user, query and product to improve the search performance of PPS. Despite the progress, LLM-based PPS solutions merely take textual contents into consideration, neglecting multimodal contents which play a critical role for product search. Motivated by this, we propose a novel framework, HMPPS, for \textbf{H}arnessing \textbf{M}ultimodal large language models (MLLM) to deal with \textbf{P}ersonalized \textbf{P}roduct \textbf{S}earch based on multimodal contents. Nevertheless, the redundancy and noise in PPS input stand for a great challenge to apply MLLM for PPS, which not only misleads MLLM to generate inaccurate search results but also increases the computation expense of MLLM. To deal with this problem, we additionally design two query-aware refinement modules for HMPPS: 1) a perspective-guided summarization module that generates refined product descriptions around core perspectives relevant to search query, reducing noise and redundancy within textual contents; and 2) a two-stage training paradigm that introduces search query for user history filtering based on multimodal representations, capturing precise user preferences and decreasing the inference cost. Extensive experiments are conducted on four public datasets to demonstrate the effectiveness of HMPPS. Furthermore, HMPPS is deployed on an online search system with billion-level daily active users and achieves an evident gain in A/B testing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces HMPPS, a novel framework that leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for personalized product search (PPS). Addressing the limitations of existing LLM-based PPS solutions that primarily focus on textual data, HMPPS incorporates multimodal content, including visual data, to enhance the understanding of user preferences, query intent, and product characteristics. Key to HMPPS are two query-aware refinement modules: (1) a perspective-guided summarization module that reduces noise and redundancy in product descriptions by focusing on query-relevant aspects, and (2) a two-stage training paradigm that filters user history to capture precise user preferences and reduce inference costs. Experiments on public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of HMPPS, validated further by deployment on an online search system with a significant user base, showing improvements in key metrics.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The core idea of incorporating multimodal information into LLM-based PPS is a significant step forward. The design of the query-aware refinement modules, particularly the perspective-guided summarization, adds valuable novelty. The two-stage training paradigm to filter user history is a practical and clever solution to address the context limitations of MLLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the components are well-integrated, the individual techniques (e.g., summarization, two-stage training) are not entirely new in themselves. The novelty lies primarily in their application and adaptation within the context of multimodal PPS with MLLMs.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate that HMPPS improves PPS performance compared to existing methods. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each module. The deployment on a real-world online system is a strong indicator of practical significance. The ability to achieve strong performance with smaller MLLMs and limited training data adds to the framework's appeal. Addressing the data bias problem in ID-based methods through multimodal analysis is significant.
*   **Weaknesses:**  The paper mainly focuses on empirical evaluation, and could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the MLLM's representation learning capabilities in the PPS context.  Furthermore, while performance gains are reported, there should be more comparisons/analysis with SOTA approaches, as UniSAR (the underlying recommender used) may not be the most competitive baseline. Additionally, the generalizability of the learned perspectives across different product domains remains a potential concern.  Although, there is some mitigation with having per dataset perspectives (described in Section 4.2.1), some products might exhibit correlation.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of personalized product search. The HMPPS framework effectively addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based methods by incorporating multimodal information and introducing query-aware refinement techniques. The experimental results are compelling, and the online deployment demonstrates practical significance. However, some limitations exist around the components not being entirely novel by themselves, and some analysis missing from the comparisons with SOTA approaches.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.MM
- **Score**: 7/10

### LEAF-Mamba: Local Emphatic and Adaptive Fusion State Space Model for RGB-D Salient Object Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18683v1)
- **Authors**: Lanhu Wu, Zilin Gao, Hao Fei, Mong-Li Lee, Wynne Hsu
- **Abstract**: RGB-D salient object detection (SOD) aims to identify the most conspicuous objects in a scene with the incorporation of depth cues. Existing methods mainly rely on CNNs, limited by the local receptive fields, or Vision Transformers that suffer from the cost of quadratic complexity, posing a challenge in balancing performance and computational efficiency. Recently, state space models (SSM), Mamba, have shown great potential for modeling long-range dependency with linear complexity. However, directly applying SSM to RGB-D SOD may lead to deficient local semantics as well as the inadequate cross-modality fusion. To address these issues, we propose a Local Emphatic and Adaptive Fusion state space model (LEAF-Mamba) that contains two novel components: 1) a local emphatic state space module (LE-SSM) to capture multi-scale local dependencies for both modalities. 2) an SSM-based adaptive fusion module (AFM) for complementary cross-modality interaction and reliable cross-modality integration. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the LEAF-Mamba consistently outperforms 16 state-of-the-art RGB-D SOD methods in both efficacy and efficiency. Moreover, our method can achieve excellent performance on the RGB-T SOD task, proving a powerful generalization ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEAF-Mamba, a novel State Space Model (SSM) architecture designed for RGB-D Salient Object Detection (SOD). Addressing limitations of existing CNN and Transformer-based methods (local receptive fields and quadratic complexity, respectively), LEAF-Mamba leverages the linear complexity of SSMs while incorporating local and cross-modal information effectively.  The key components are: 1) a Local Emphatic State Space Module (LE-SSM) that captures multi-scale local dependencies using a windowed selective scan mechanism; and 2) an SSM-based Adaptive Fusion Module (AFM) that adaptively interacts between RGB and depth modalities. The paper claims significant performance gains in both efficacy and efficiency compared to state-of-the-art methods on several RGB-D SOD datasets. The authors also demonstrate the generalizability of their approach on the RGB-T SOD task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific architecture design for applying SSMs to RGB-D SOD. While SSMs themselves are not new, their adaptation to this task with LE-SSM and AFM appears innovative.

    *   The **LE-SSM** module, with its multi-scale windowed selective scan, is a noteworthy attempt to address the limitations of applying SSMs directly to vision tasks, particularly the challenge of maintaining local information. The four-way four-scale windowed scanning provides a more comprehensive local context than fixed window sizes used in existing approaches.

    *   The **AFM**, employing a cross-modality second-order pooling (CSOP) layer to compute modality-specific similarities and selectively interact/fuse features, offers a refined approach to cross-modality fusion, taking into account the complementarity and reliability of RGB and depth data. Previous approaches often treated the modalities equally, which is not necessarily optimal.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the efficiency and accuracy of RGB-D SOD. This is a crucial task in many applications, from robotics to autonomous driving. The authors present compelling results that demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods, with a significant reduction in computational cost.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The extensive experiments across multiple datasets provide convincing evidence of the method's effectiveness. The reported gains over SOTA methods are substantial.
    *   **Efficiency:** A key strength is the demonstrated efficiency (high FPS, low FLOPs) alongside high accuracy, addressing a known bottleneck in RGB-D SOD.
    *   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The LE-SSM and AFM modules are well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   **Generalization:**  The RGB-T SOD results further support the method's robustness and generalizability.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components (LE-SSM and AFM).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While the results are strong, the gains over SOTA models, while significant, may be seen as an incremental improvement on the current state of the art.
    *   **Complexity:** The design involves several custom components (windowed scanning, CSOP). The integration of these components increases the complexity and may make the architecture harder to understand and replicate.
    *   **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** The paper would benefit from more in-depth qualitative analysis of the failures and scenarios where the method excels. More feature visualization would enhance the understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RGB-D SOD by demonstrating the effectiveness of SSMs with specifically designed components for local and cross-modal integration. If the efficiency claims hold up in real-world applications, it could be a significant advancement.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed architecture for RGB-D SOD. The LEAF-Mamba effectively addresses key limitations of existing methods by incorporating a more efficient and comprehensive local and cross-modal learning. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, their combination in LEAF-Mamba, along with the strong empirical results and demonstrated generalizability, make this a significant contribution to the field.

However, the incremental nature of the performance improvements, and the complexity of the architecture design, the paper prevent it from achieving a score that signifies a revolutionary advancement.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Advances in Large Language Models for Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18690v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyu Kan, Wensheng Gan, Zhenlian Qi, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) technology has advanced rapidly in recent years, with large language models (LLMs) emerging as a significant breakthrough. LLMs are increasingly making an impact across various industries, with the medical field standing out as the most prominent application area. This paper systematically reviews the up-to-date research progress of LLMs in the medical field, providing an in-depth analysis of training techniques for large medical models, their adaptation in healthcare settings, related applications, as well as their strengths and limitations. Furthermore, it innovatively categorizes medical LLMs into three distinct types based on their training methodologies and classifies their evaluation approaches into two categories. Finally, the study proposes solutions to existing challenges and outlines future research directions based on identified issues in the field of medical LLMs. By systematically reviewing previous and advanced research findings, we aim to highlight the necessity of developing medical LLMs, provide a deeper understanding of their current state of development, and offer clear guidance for subsequent research.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided document and will summarize it and provide a critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper "Advances in Large Language Models for Medicine" provides a comprehensive review of the current state of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the medical field. It covers training methodologies for medical LLMs (pre-training, fine-tuning, and prompting), their adaptation in healthcare settings, a diverse range of applications (clinical decision support, personalized medicine, medical education, drug discovery, medical imaging, and patient care), and the associated benefits and limitations. The authors categorize medical LLMs into three types based on training methods and their evaluation approaches into machine and human-centered evaluation. The paper also identifies challenges in the field and proposes future research directions, such as enhancing LLMs' understanding of medical terminology, transitioning LLMs to multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), fostering interdisciplinary collaboration, introducing new evaluation benchmarks, and improving relevant laws and regulations. The paper aims to contribute to a deeper understanding of medical LLMs' development and provide guidance for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its comprehensive and up-to-date review of the rapidly evolving field of medical LLMs. While several review articles exist, the document demonstrates a more structured organization (especially with the innovative categorization of LLMs based on training methods and evaluation approaches), and includes very recent and relevant research, going up to September 2025 (a timestamp). However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the fact that it's primarily a review, and does not introduce any fundamentally new algorithms, techniques, or datasets. The categorization of LLMs by training methods and evaluation metrics could be seen as a moderately novel contribution, synthesizing information in a useful way.

**Significance:** The paper has potential significance for researchers and practitioners in the medical AI field. By providing a comprehensive overview of the current landscape, it can help newcomers quickly understand the key concepts, challenges, and opportunities. Highlighting future research directions can guide future studies and potentially accelerate advancements in the field. The systematic analysis of benefits and drawbacks is helpful for healthcare professionals considering implementing LLMs in their practice. However, the paper's significance is limited by the fact that it's a review, and the impact depends on how widely the information is disseminated and adopted. The forward-looking suggestions, while useful, are somewhat general and could be more specific. The paper doesn't contain empirical validation of the identified limitations, which could strengthen its claims. Also, the fact that the authors themselves are involved in medical LLM research is clearly stated and can be seen as a strength, but it also brings a potential bias to their survey choices.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensiveness:** Thorough coverage of LLM training techniques, applications, benefits, and limitations in medicine.
*   **Up-to-dateness:** Includes recent research findings, providing a timely overview of the field.
*   **Clear Organization:**  The categorization of LLMs and evaluation methods facilitates understanding.
*   **Future Directions:**  Identification of key challenges and proposed research directions are helpful for the community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Algorithmic Novelty:** Primarily a review and does not introduce new algorithms, techniques, or datasets.
*   **General Future Directions:** The proposed research directions could be more specific and actionable.
*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:**  The identified limitations are not always supported by empirical evidence.
*   **Potential Bias:** The research interests of the authors may influence the focus and selection of studies covered in the review.

**Justification for Score:**
Despite its comprehensiveness and timely nature, the paper's value is somewhat restricted by its nature as a literature review without empirical validation. Though helpful to other researchers, the paper falls short of introducing truly groundbreaking innovations in the area of medical LLMs. However, the innovative categorization and comprehensive insights justify more than a purely average rating.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### An overview of neural architectures for self-supervised audio representation learning from masked spectrograms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18691v1)
- **Authors**: Sarthak Yadav, Sergios Theodoridis, Zheng-Hua Tan
- **Abstract**: In recent years, self-supervised learning has amassed significant interest for training deep neural representations without labeled data. One such self-supervised learning approach is masked spectrogram modeling, where the objective is to learn semantically rich contextual representations by predicting removed or hidden portions of the input audio spectrogram. With the Transformer neural architecture at its core, masked spectrogram modeling has emerged as the prominent approach for learning general purpose audio representations, a.k.a. audio foundation models. Meanwhile, addressing the issues of the Transformer architecture, in particular the underlying Scaled Dot-product Attention operation, which scales quadratically with input sequence length, has led to renewed interest in recurrent sequence modeling approaches. Among them, Selective structured state space models (such as Mamba) and extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) are the two most promising approaches which have experienced widespread adoption. While the body of work on these two topics continues to grow, there is currently a lack of an adequate overview encompassing the intersection of these topics. In this paper, we present a comprehensive overview of the aforementioned research domains, covering masked spectrogram modeling and the previously mentioned neural sequence modeling architectures, Mamba and xLSTM. Further, we compare Transformers, Mamba and xLSTM based masked spectrogram models in a unified, reproducible framework on ten diverse downstream audio classification tasks, which will help interested readers to make informed decisions regarding suitability of the evaluated approaches to adjacent applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a comprehensive overview of self-supervised audio representation learning using masked spectrogram modeling. It focuses on architectures that move beyond the standard Transformer, specifically examining Mamba (selective state space models) and xLSTM (extended Long Short-Term Memory). The paper covers the fundamentals of masked spectrogram modeling (MSM), including patch creation, masking strategies, encoding, and reconstruction. It then delves into the details of Mamba and xLSTM, highlighting their potential advantages over Transformers, particularly in handling long sequences. A significant portion of the paper is dedicated to an empirical evaluation comparing Transformers, Mamba, and xLSTM within an SSAST-style MSM framework across ten diverse audio classification tasks. The analysis explores the impact of patch size and input sequence length on the performance of these architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary value of this paper lies in its comprehensive overview and comparative empirical analysis of Transformer alternatives (Mamba and xLSTM) within the context of masked spectrogram modeling for audio representation learning. While individual components (MSM, Mamba, xLSTM) are not entirely new, their unified treatment and direct comparison in the specific domain of audio representation learning contribute to the body of knowledge. The comparison across the sequence modeling approaches, specifically focusing on a standardized architecture, contributes to the understanding of each approach in an audio context.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial aspect of audio representation learning: moving beyond the limitations of Transformers, particularly their quadratic complexity and memory requirements when dealing with long audio sequences. By evaluating Mamba and xLSTM, the paper explores architectures that potentially offer better scalability and efficiency. The empirical results provide practical insights for researchers and practitioners choosing between these architectures for audio-related tasks. Given the surge in interest in audio foundation models, the paper is well-timed and could guide future research directions. It also focuses on a general-purpose audio representation in contrast to focusing on speech.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive overview:** The paper presents a well-structured and detailed overview of the relevant research domains.
    *   **Unified framework:** The empirical evaluation is conducted in a consistent and reproducible framework, allowing for fair comparison.
    *   **Diverse task suite:** The use of ten varied downstream tasks provides a robust assessment of the models' generalization capabilities.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Investigating the impact of patch size and input sequence length adds valuable insights.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Providing links to code and potentially pretrained models enhances the paper's practical value.
    *   Provides an analysis of where each model performed best.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental contribution:** While comprehensive, the paper's individual components (MSM, Mamba, xLSTM) are established methods. The novelty primarily comes from their integration and comparison.
    *   **Limited architectural exploration:** The empirical study is limited to the SSAST-style MSM. Exploring other architectures (e.g., MAE-style) could provide a more complete picture.
    *   **Lack of throughput results:** Although acknowledged by the authors, not including throughput measurements limits the practical insights for deployment considerations.
    *   **Limited full fine-tuning results:** The absence of results from a full fine-tuning process limits the scope of the conclusions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to be influential in the audio representation learning community, particularly for researchers interested in exploring alternatives to Transformers. The systematic evaluation and the provided resources will help facilitate further research and development in this area. The direction towards general audio representations instead of focusing solely on speech representations could also garner attention.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant contribution through its comprehensive overview, systematic comparison, and reproducible framework. While it builds upon existing methods, its focus on comparing Mamba and xLSTM within the context of masked spectrogram modeling for audio is timely and relevant. The empirical analysis provides valuable insights and will likely influence future research. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, combined with some limitations in the experimental scope (e.g., only SSAST-style MSMs tested, no throughput metrics), prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Automatic Chord Recognition through LLM Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18700v1)
- **Authors**: Chih-Cheng Chang, Bo-Yu Chen, Lu-Rong Chen, Li Su
- **Abstract**: Music Information Retrieval (MIR) encompasses a broad range of computational techniques for analyzing and understanding musical content, with recent deep learning advances driving substantial improvements. Building upon these advances, this paper explores how large language models (LLMs) can serve as an integrative bridge to connect and integrate information from multiple MIR tools, with a focus on enhancing automatic chord recognition performance. We present a novel approach that positions text-based LLMs as intelligent coordinators that process and integrate outputs from diverse state-of-the-art MIR tools-including music source separation, key detection, chord recognition, and beat tracking. Our method converts audio-derived musical information into textual representations, enabling LLMs to perform reasoning and correction specifically for chord recognition tasks. We design a 5-stage chain-of-thought framework that allows GPT-4o to systematically analyze, compare, and refine chord recognition results by leveraging music-theoretical knowledge to integrate information across different MIR components. Experimental evaluation on three datasets demonstrates consistent improvements across multiple evaluation metrics, with overall accuracy gains of 1-2.77% on the MIREX metric. Our findings demonstrate that LLMs can effectively function as integrative bridges in MIR pipelines, opening new directions for multi-tool coordination in music information retrieval tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method to enhance automatic chord recognition (ACR) performance by using large language models (LLMs), specifically GPT-40, as an integrative bridge between various state-of-the-art music information retrieval (MIR) tools. The approach involves converting audio-derived musical information (from source separation, key detection, chord recognition, and beat tracking) into textual representations, enabling the LLM to perform reasoning and correction through a five-stage chain-of-thought (CoT) framework. The LLM analyzes, compares, and refines chord recognition results, leveraging music-theoretical knowledge. Experimental results on three datasets demonstrate consistent improvements in chord recognition accuracy. The paper's central claim is that LLMs can effectively coordinate information from multiple MIR tools, improving ACR performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of LLMs within a traditional MIR pipeline to improve ACR. While using LLMs for music-related tasks isn't entirely new, the specific application of an LLM as an "intelligent coordinator" within a multi-tool framework, employing CoT reasoning for refining chord recognition, represents a distinct and interesting contribution.  The five-stage CoT framework is specifically designed for chord recognition and constitutes a structured approach not commonly found in existing literature.

**Significance:** The significance stems from potentially overcoming limitations of individual MIR tools by leveraging LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Traditionally, MIR systems rely on individual algorithms and models optimized for specific tasks. This research opens a potential pathway for more holistic music understanding by enabling cross-modal reasoning and correction. Improved ACR is, in itself, a valuable contribution to MIR, as it is a fundamental task with applications in music education, analysis, and generation. The results showing improvements across different datasets provide empirical support for the proposed approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the proposed framework and the individual MIR tools utilized.
*   **Structured CoT Reasoning:** The five-stage chain-of-thought is a valuable contribution, providing a structured method for LLM-based refinement.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate improvements across several datasets and evaluation metrics, supporting the effectiveness of the approach.
*   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** ACR is a fundamental MIR task, and improving its performance has broad applications.
*   **Clear Integration of MIR Tools:** The approach effectively combines the strengths of various MIR tools with the reasoning abilities of LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-40:** The paper uses a closed-source LLM (GPT-40). This makes reproducibility and further experimentation by the broader research community difficult. The specific prompt engineering used is not fully detailed, which further limits reproducibility.  The specific prompting strategies used should be disclosed to enable replication.
*   **Limited Error Analysis:** While the paper provides aggregate accuracy improvements, a more in-depth error analysis would strengthen the findings. The paper mentions the bass correction stage occasionally decreasing accuracy and explains why; a deeper dive into the types of errors that are made and corrected would enhance the contribution.
*   **Rule-based Beat Alignment:** The final beat alignment stage does not utilize LLMs. Although the motivation is to prevent degradation from incorrect beat tracking, it raises questions about whether an LLM could improve this final step as well.
*   **Lack of comparison with End-to-End LLM approaches:** The work does not compare against finetuning LLMs end to end for chord recognition.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in MIR by demonstrating the benefits of integrating LLMs into existing MIR pipelines. It provides a concrete example of how LLMs can be used to reason about and correct errors made by traditional MIR tools. The CoT framework can serve as a starting point for developing other LLM-based MIR applications. However, the reliance on a closed-source LLM and a lack of detailed prompt information limit the immediate impact.
**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a clear and well-structured approach to improving ACR by leveraging LLMs as an integrative bridge between multiple MIR tools. While there are some limitations regarding reproducibility and potential areas for further exploration, the empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework and its potential to improve ACR performance. The novelty lies in the structured CoT-based approach within the broader context of LLM-driven MIR, and the significance is demonstrated through empirical improvements in ACR accuracy.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Rational Pesticide Design with Graph Machine Learning Models for Ecotoxicology
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18703v1)
- **Authors**: Jakub Adamczyk
- **Abstract**: This research focuses on rational pesticide design, using graph machine learning to accelerate the development of safer, eco-friendly agrochemicals, inspired by in silico methods in drug discovery. With an emphasis on ecotoxicology, the initial contributions include the creation of ApisTox, the largest curated dataset on pesticide toxicity to honey bees. We conducted a broad evaluation of machine learning (ML) models for molecular graph classification, including molecular fingerprints, graph kernels, GNNs, and pretrained transformers. The results show that methods successful in medicinal chemistry often fail to generalize to agrochemicals, underscoring the need for domain-specific models and benchmarks. Future work will focus on developing a comprehensive benchmarking suite and designing ML models tailored to the unique challenges of pesticide discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the emerging area of applying machine learning (ML) to rational pesticide design, mirroring the successful use of *in silico* methods in drug discovery. It highlights the scarcity of high-quality, publicly available datasets and benchmarks in agrochemistry, particularly for ecotoxicology (specifically honeybee toxicity).  A key contribution is the creation of "ApisTox," a curated dataset of pesticide toxicity data, claimed to be larger and cleaner than existing alternatives.  The paper evaluates various ML models (fingerprints, graph kernels, GNNs, pretrained transformers) for molecular graph classification on ApisTox and finds that models performing well in medicinal chemistry often struggle in agrochemistry, suggesting the need for domain-specific models and benchmarks.  It also proposes two novel split strategies (MaxMin and time split) to evaluate performance of models by generating a diverse test set. Finally, a metric for comparing the datasets on inter- and intra-diversity has been proposed. The work argues for a focused effort in developing more relevant benchmarks and ML models tailored to the unique challenges of pesticide discovery.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the ApisTox dataset and the demonstration of the limited generalizability of medicinal chemistry ML models to agrochemistry. The split strategies proposed also add some novelty. While the individual ML models used are not novel in themselves, their application and evaluation within this specific context is.  The paper effectively highlights a gap in the field.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to accelerate the development of safer, more environmentally friendly pesticides. By adapting rational drug design principles, the authors aim to reduce the reliance on costly and ethically questionable traditional laboratory testing.  The availability of ApisTox is a clear benefit to the community, enabling further research and development of improved ML models. The comparison with MoleculeNet and the analysis of dataset diversity offer valuable insights for future benchmark development. The work could potentially reduce the development time and cost of new pesticides, leading to safer agricultural practices.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Dataset Contribution:** Creating ApisTox addresses a critical need for high-quality data in agrochemistry. This is a tangible and valuable contribution to the field.
    * **Comparative Analysis:** The comprehensive evaluation of diverse ML models provides a useful benchmark for future research.
    * **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of limited generalizability from medicinal chemistry and motivates the need for domain-specific approaches.
    * **Diversity Analysis:** The metric and analysis comparing ApisTox and MoleculeNet is well-done and provides valuable insights.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Innovation:** The paper focuses more on data and benchmarking than on developing fundamentally novel ML models.  It's largely an *evaluation* paper rather than a method development paper. The data cleaning and pre processing steps seems specific to the dataset in question, thus lack generalisability to other datasets.
    * **Limited Scope:** The focus on honeybee toxicity, while important, is somewhat narrow. Broader ecotoxicological considerations could be addressed in future work.

* **Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a critical gap in the application of machine learning to agrochemistry. The ApisTox dataset is a valuable resource, and the comparative analysis of ML models provides useful insights. The paper clearly demonstrates the limitations of existing benchmarks and motivates the need for domain-specific approaches. However, the lack of significant model innovation and the somewhat narrow focus on honeybee toxicity slightly limit its overall impact.
