- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking Vision-Language and Multimodal Large Language Models in Zero-shot and Few-shot Scenarios: A study on Christian Iconography
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18839v1)
- **Authors**: Gianmarco Spinaci, Lukas Klic, Giovanni Colavizza
- **Abstract**: This study evaluates the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) in the task of single-label classification of Christian Iconography. The goal was to assess whether general-purpose VLMs (CLIP and SigLIP) and LLMs, such as GPT-4o and Gemini 2.5, can interpret the Iconography, typically addressed by supervised classifiers, and evaluate their performance. Two research questions guided the analysis: (RQ1) How do multimodal LLMs perform on image classification of Christian saints? And (RQ2), how does performance vary when enriching input with contextual information or few-shot exemplars? We conducted a benchmarking study using three datasets supporting Iconclass natively: ArtDL, ICONCLASS, and Wikidata, filtered to include the top 10 most frequent classes. Models were tested under three conditions: (1) classification using class labels, (2) classification with Iconclass descriptions, and (3) few-shot learning with five exemplars. Results were compared against ResNet50 baselines fine-tuned on the same datasets. The findings show that Gemini-2.5 Pro and GPT-4o outperformed the ResNet50 baselines. Accuracy dropped significantly on the Wikidata dataset, where Siglip reached the highest accuracy score, suggesting model sensitivity to image size and metadata alignment. Enriching prompts with class descriptions generally improved zero-shot performance, while few-shot learning produced lower results, with only occasional and minimal increments in accuracy. We conclude that general-purpose multimodal LLMs are capable of classification in visually complex cultural heritage domains. These results support the application of LLMs as metadata curation tools in digital humanities workflows, suggesting future research on prompt optimization and the expansion of the study to other classification strategies and models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper benchmarks the performance of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and multimodal Large Language Models (LLMs) in the task of classifying Christian iconography within zero-shot and few-shot learning scenarios.  The study uses three datasets that natively support Iconclass labels: ArtDL, ICONCLASS Test AI Set, and a Wikidata subset.  The models are tested under three conditions: using class labels only, using Iconclass descriptions, and using a few-shot learning approach with example images. The results are compared to ResNet50 baselines. The study finds that multimodal LLMs (specifically Google Gemini 2.5 Pro and OpenAI GPT-4o) outperform ResNet50 and VLM baselines in accuracy, but performance is sensitive to dataset consistency, prompt design, and image size.  Adding class descriptions to the prompt generally improves zero-shot performance, while few-shot learning does not consistently yield better results. The paper concludes that general-purpose multimodal LLMs are capable of classifying visually complex cultural heritage content, but their application requires careful prompt engineering and an awareness of dataset biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has *moderate* novelty. While there's been prior work on image classification of art and the use of LLMs for various tasks, the specific application of benchmarking state-of-the-art *multimodal* LLMs for Christian iconography classification is relatively unexplored. It's a focused application within a niche domain, which adds to the knowledge base. The cross-dataset analysis provides a useful consistency check.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in providing empirical evidence of the capabilities and limitations of modern LLMs in a specialized cultural heritage domain. The findings regarding prompt sensitivity, dataset consistency, and the surprising lack of consistent improvement from few-shot learning are valuable. This helps inform how LLMs can be effectively used in digital humanities workflows, particularly for metadata curation tasks. The study highlights practical considerations for researchers and practitioners working with cultural heritage data and LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The methodology is sound, with clear experimental design and appropriate baselines.
    *   The use of multiple datasets and a variety of models increases the robustness of the findings.
    *   The cross-dataset similarity analysis is a strong addition, helping to understand potential biases.
    *   The paper discusses the limitations and offers directions for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The datasets used are relatively small and confined. Generalizability to other iconography or cultural heritage domains might be limited.
    *   The few-shot learning results are inconsistent and lack a deeper analysis of how example selection influences performance. It's possible that the negative results are also due to the small class size causing the models to memorize the examples.
    *   The paper would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of *why* certain prompts work better than others. The description of prompt engineering could be expanded.
    *   The consistency analysis, while useful, focuses only on identical predictions, potentially missing nuances where models provide related but not identical classifications.
    *   The paper fails to mention that, while Gemini, GPT, etc. "outperform" a ResNet50 model fine-tuned on 600 images, that is not particularly noteworthy, because that ResNet50 model is hardly optimized and is likely vastly underperforming.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6/10**. While the paper addresses a specific research gap and provides valuable empirical insights, the limitations in dataset scale, a relatively basic investigation of few-shot prompting, and other unaddressed potential flaws moderately reduces its overall impact.

The paper is novel enough to warrant publication. It highlights the potential, but also the challenges, of using LLMs in a culturally specific context. The findings are potentially useful for digital humanities scholars and practitioners. With improvements in the prompt design, it may open the way for more advanced works.

- **Score**: 6/10

### ViG-LRGC: Vision Graph Neural Networks with Learnable Reparameterized Graph Construction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18840v1)
- **Authors**: Ismael Elsharkawi, Hossam Sharara, Ahmed Rafea
- **Abstract**: Image Representation Learning is an important problem in Computer Vision. Traditionally, images were processed as grids, using Convolutional Neural Networks or as a sequence of visual tokens, using Vision Transformers. Recently, Vision Graph Neural Networks (ViG) have proposed the treatment of images as a graph of nodes; which provides a more intuitive image representation. The challenge is to construct a graph of nodes in each layer that best represents the relations between nodes and does not need a hyper-parameter search. ViG models in the literature depend on non-parameterized and non-learnable statistical methods that operate on the latent features of nodes to create a graph. This might not select the best neighborhood for each node. Starting from k-NN graph construction to HyperGraph Construction and Similarity-Thresholded graph construction, these methods lack the ability to provide a learnable hyper-parameter-free graph construction method. To overcome those challenges, we present the Learnable Reparameterized Graph Construction (LRGC) for Vision Graph Neural Networks. LRGC applies key-query attention between every pair of nodes; then uses soft-threshold reparameterization for edge selection, which allows the use of a differentiable mathematical model for training. Using learnable parameters to select the neighborhood removes the bias that is induced by any clustering or thresholding methods previously introduced in the literature. In addition, LRGC allows tuning the threshold in each layer to the training data since the thresholds are learnable through training and are not provided as hyper-parameters to the model. We demonstrate that the proposed ViG-LRGC approach outperforms state-of-the-art ViG models of similar sizes on the ImageNet-1k benchmark dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ViG-LRGC: VISION GRAPH NEURAL NETWORKS WITH LEARNABLE REPARAMETERIZED GRAPH CONSTRUCTION":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Learnable Reparameterized Graph Construction (LRGC) method for Vision Graph Neural Networks (ViG).  Instead of relying on non-parameterized statistical methods like k-NN or similarity-thresholding to build graphs, LRGC uses key-query attention between every pair of nodes, followed by soft-threshold reparameterization for edge selection. This allows for a differentiable and trainable graph construction process. The threshold for edge selection is also a learnable parameter, removing the need for manual hyperparameter tuning. The authors demonstrate that ViG-LRGC outperforms state-of-the-art ViG models of similar size on ImageNet-1k.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The key novelty lies in the learnable graph construction.  Previous ViG models used static or hyperparameter-dependent graph construction methods.  LRGC introduces learnable attention scores *and* a learnable threshold for pruning edges, giving the network more flexibility to adapt the graph structure to the data. Borrowing concepts from Transformers (key-query attention) and soft-thresholding techniques isn't entirely new, but the *combination* and *application* to graph construction in ViGs are novel. The use of learnable thresholds addresses a clear weakness of previous similarity-based approaches.

*   **Significance:**  Image representation is a fundamental problem.  ViGs are a relatively new approach, and improving their graph construction methods is significant.  A learnable graph structure has the potential to better capture relationships between image patches compared to fixed graphs. The claimed outperformance on ImageNet-1k, a standard benchmark, lends credence to the significance of the work. The hyperparameter-free nature of the approach also is important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing ViG graph construction methods.
    *   **Technical Contribution:** LRGC provides a well-defined and implementable solution.  The equations are clearly explained, and the architecture is described in detail.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The paper presents experimental results on ImageNet-1k, comparing LRGC to competitive baselines.  The authors acknowledge the batch size limitations during training, and train the baselines under the same limited-batch-size conditions for fair comparisons.
    *   **Analysis:** The analysis of pruning power helps understand how LRGC operates, showing which layers have the greatest tendency to prune non-essential edges.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Nature:** While novel, LRGC builds upon existing concepts from Transformers and soft-thresholding.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the computational complexity is argued to be comparable, the discussion reveals a significant training limitation: needing a fully-connected graph at first. The need for limited batch sizes during training due to this significantly lowers performance compared to training at a larger batch size. This limitation makes the claim of equal complexity seem somewhat misleading in practical scenarios.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  The evaluation is restricted to image classification on ImageNet-1k.  It would be stronger to demonstrate the transferability of LRGC-ViG to other downstream tasks (e.g., object detection, segmentation) or datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** LRGC has the potential to become a standard component of ViG architectures. The idea of learnable graph structures is compelling, and the authors show promising results. Future work will likely explore variations and extensions of this approach.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** This paper presents a valuable contribution to the Vision GNN literature, but it has key limitations, particularly concerning the need for the computationally expensive fully connected graph in training. The performance drop during training in small batch sizes is significant, and may hinder real-world applicability. Additionally, the evaluation is confined to ImageNet-1k, where there is only a limited analysis of the effect of batch size, rather than the true benefit that would come with a larger batch. Given the above points, and considering the incremental nature of the contributions, while novel, the paper's overall impact, is limited.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Are Smaller Open-Weight LLMs Closing the Gap to Proprietary Models for Biomedical Question Answering?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18843v1)
- **Authors**: Damian Stachura, Joanna Konieczna, Artur Nowak
- **Abstract**: Open-weight versions of large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing, with state-of-the-art models like DeepSeek-V3 now performing comparably to proprietary LLMs. This progression raises the question of whether small open-weight LLMs are capable of effectively replacing larger closed-source models. We are particularly interested in the context of biomedical question-answering, a domain we explored by participating in Task 13B Phase B of the BioASQ challenge. In this work, we compare several open-weight models against top-performing systems such as GPT-4o, GPT-4.1, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and Claude 3.7 Sonnet. To enhance question answering capabilities, we use various techniques including retrieving the most relevant snippets based on embedding distance, in-context learning, and structured outputs. For certain submissions, we utilize ensemble approaches to leverage the diverse outputs generated by different models for exact-answer questions. Our results demonstrate that open-weight LLMs are comparable to proprietary ones. In some instances, open-weight LLMs even surpassed their closed counterparts, particularly when ensembling strategies were applied. All code is publicly available at https://github.com/evidenceprime/BioASQ-13b.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Are Smaller Open-Weight LLMs Closing the Gap to Proprietary Models for Biomedical Question Answering?" investigates the performance of smaller, open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of biomedical question answering.  The authors participated in Task 13B Phase B of the BioASQ challenge and compared several open-weight LLMs (Phi-4, Gemma, Qwen) against proprietary models like GPT-4 and Claude. The study explored various techniques to enhance question answering capabilities, including retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), in-context learning, structured outputs, and ensembling. The results suggest that open-weight LLMs are becoming increasingly competitive with proprietary models, and in some cases, even surpassing them, particularly when using ensemble methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a solid empirical evaluation through the BioASQ challenge. Using a standardized benchmark allows for direct comparison of different models and techniques.
    *   **Relevant Problem:** The research addresses a relevant and important question: Can open-weight LLMs replace or supplement proprietary models, especially in specialized domains like biomedicine where access to specific knowledge is crucial? This is important because proprietary LLMs can be expensive, and their terms of use can be restrictive.
    *   **Comprehensive Techniques:** The exploration of different techniques like RAG, in-context learning, and structured outputs adds to the value.  The emphasis on structured outputs is particularly beneficial, as it simplifies the extraction and processing of LLM-generated information.
    *   **Ensembling insights**: The insight about diverse models is very useful and significant.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The analysis is mainly limited to one specific task (BioASQ). While this provides a controlled environment, it's essential to acknowledge that the results might not generalize perfectly to all biomedical QA scenarios or other domains.
    *   **Surface-Level Analysis**: The paper lacks detailed analysis of why certain models or ensembles perform better than others. What architectural or training differences are driving these results? This makes it difficult to draw strong conclusions about the fundamental capabilities of open-weight vs. proprietary LLMs.
    *   **Lack of novelty in method**: The techniques used are not novel. The setup is also limited.
    *   **Missing metrics**: The paper lacks a direct comparison on how the open-weights are comparable to Proprietary Models.
    *   **Generative-AI declaration:** The generative AI declaration should discuss where the AI was used and where the authors took over.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The core novelty of this work lies in showing that, through a careful and comprehensive ensemble method, a small, opensource model can match if not beat larger, closed-source LLMs on a biomedical question answering task.
    *   It is important because it has immediate practical implications.

*   **Justification:**

The score reflects the paper's strengths in its empirical evaluation and relevant problem, balanced by its weaknesses in generalizability, analytical depth, and novelty.  The BioASQ benchmark provides a solid foundation for the comparison, but the study is not groundbreaking, and it doesn't offer significant insights into the underlying mechanisms driving the performance differences. It shows that smaller open weights are better when carefully ensembled, but could be improved to have more analysis on the models. The method on its own is not really unique or novel.

### Model selection meets clinical semantics: Optimizing ICD-10-CM prediction via LLM-as-Judge evaluation, redundancy-aware sampling, and section-aware fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18846v1)
- **Authors**: Hong-Jie Dai, Zheng-Hao Li, An-Tai Lu, Bo-Tsz Shain, Ming-Ta Li, Tatheer Hussain Mir, Kuang-Te Wang, Min-I Su, Pei-Kang Liu, Ming-Ju Tsai
- **Abstract**: Accurate International Classification of Diseases (ICD) coding is critical for clinical documentation, billing, and healthcare analytics, yet it remains a labour-intensive and error-prone task. Although large language models (LLMs) show promise in automating ICD coding, their challenges in base model selection, input contextualization, and training data redundancy limit their effectiveness. We propose a modular framework for ICD-10 Clinical Modification (ICD-10-CM) code prediction that addresses these challenges through principled model selection, redundancy-aware data sampling, and structured input design. The framework integrates an LLM-as-judge evaluation protocol with Plackett-Luce aggregation to assess and rank open-source LLMs based on their intrinsic comprehension of ICD-10-CM code definitions. We introduced embedding-based similarity measures, a redundancy-aware sampling strategy to remove semantically duplicated discharge summaries. We leverage structured discharge summaries from Taiwanese hospitals to evaluate contextual effects and examine section-wise content inclusion under universal and section-specific modelling paradigms. Experiments across two institutional datasets demonstrate that the selected base model after fine-tuning consistently outperforms baseline LLMs in internal and external evaluations. Incorporating more clinical sections consistently improves prediction performance. This study uses open-source LLMs to establish a practical and principled approach to ICD-10-CM code prediction. The proposed framework provides a scalable, institution-ready solution for real-world deployment of automated medical coding systems by combining informed model selection, efficient data refinement, and context-aware prompting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses challenges in automating ICD-10-CM (International Classification of Diseases, 10th Revision, Clinical Modification) code prediction using Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a modular framework comprising: (1) an LLM-as-Judge evaluation protocol for selecting the best open-source LLM for the task based on intrinsic understanding of ICD-10-CM definitions; (2) a redundancy-aware sampling strategy to reduce training data by removing semantically duplicate discharge summaries; and (3) section-aware fine-tuning that leverages structured discharge summaries to improve contextualization. The framework is evaluated using datasets from two Taiwanese hospitals, demonstrating improvements in ICD-10-CM code prediction accuracy and generalizability compared to baseline LLMs and traditional methods. The study highlights the benefits of this combined approach for creating a scalable and accurate medical coding system.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components. The LLM-as-judge framework for base model selection is a valuable contribution, offering a computationally efficient way to assess LLMs' intrinsic understanding of ICD-10-CM codes before fine-tuning. The redundancy-aware sampling strategy is also novel within this specific context, demonstrating a practical approach for improving data efficiency in the presence of semantic duplication in clinical notes. The exploration of section-aware fine-tuning is valuable, although leveraging structured EHR data is not entirely new, the specific approach and rigorous evaluation within the ICD-10-CM coding domain adds a contribution.

*   **Significance:** The research has potential significance for automating and improving medical coding processes. Accurate ICD coding is critical for billing, documentation, and healthcare analytics. By addressing the challenges of base model selection, data redundancy, and contextual understanding, the proposed framework can help reduce errors, lower costs, and improve efficiency in medical coding workflows. The use of open-source LLMs makes the approach accessible and practical for wider adoption.  However, the scope of impact is somewhat limited to contexts where structured EHR data is available.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The modular design addresses multiple key challenges in LLM-based ICD coding.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed, using datasets from two hospitals and evaluating the framework's effectiveness across various scenarios.
    *   **Practical Approach:**  The use of open-source LLMs, and the focus on real-world datasets makes the framework applicable for hospital settings. The study includes analysis on computational and performance gains to ensure practical utility.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper offers detailed analysis and ablation studies on each module providing valuable insight for practical implementations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Geographic Scope:**  The datasets are limited to Taiwanese hospitals. Generalizability to different healthcare systems and coding practices outside of Taiwan needs to be further assessed.
    *   **Data Dependency:** The framework relies on the availability of structured discharge summaries. This limits its applicability in settings where EHR data is less structured.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** While the study considers several LLMs, the selection can be improved by including other SOTA and open-source models.
    *   **Coding Drift:** The paper does not explicitly address the potential for coding drift over time, which is a practical consideration in real-world deployments.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper offers valuable insights and practical solutions for automating medical coding using LLMs.  The LLM-as-judge framework can potentially be adapted for other tasks and domains where evaluating intrinsic model capabilities is important. The work could encourage further research on developing more robust and scalable medical coding systems using LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of medical coding automation using LLMs. The modular framework, particularly the LLM-as-judge evaluation and redundancy-aware sampling, demonstrates novelty and addresses key challenges in the domain.  The comprehensive evaluation and practical focus enhance the value of the research. However, the limited geographic scope of the datasets, the dependency on structured EHR data, and the unaddressed issue of coding drift somewhat limit the paper's overall impact and generalizability. The approach is practically useful but doesn’t represent a paradigm shift.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Failure Makes the Agent Stronger: Enhancing Accuracy through Structured Reflection for Reliable Tool Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18847v1)
- **Authors**: Junhao Su, Yuanliang Wan, Junwei Yang, Hengyu Shi, Tianyang Han, Junfeng Luo, Yurui Qiu
- **Abstract**: Tool-augmented large language models (LLMs) are usually trained with supervised imitation or coarse-grained reinforcement learning that optimizes single tool calls. Current self-reflection practices rely on heuristic prompts or one-way reasoning: the model is urged to 'think more' instead of learning error diagnosis and repair. This is fragile in multi-turn interactions; after a failure the model often repeats the same mistake. We propose structured reflection, which turns the path from error to repair into an explicit, controllable, and trainable action. The agent produces a short yet precise reflection: it diagnoses the failure using evidence from the previous step and then proposes a correct, executable follow-up call. For training we combine DAPO and GSPO objectives with a reward scheme tailored to tool use, optimizing the stepwise strategy Reflect, then Call, then Final. To evaluate, we introduce Tool-Reflection-Bench, a lightweight benchmark that programmatically checks structural validity, executability, parameter correctness, and result consistency. Tasks are built as mini trajectories of erroneous call, reflection, and corrected call, with disjoint train and test splits. Experiments on BFCL v3 and Tool-Reflection-Bench show large gains in multi-turn tool-call success and error recovery, and a reduction of redundant calls. These results indicate that making reflection explicit and optimizing it directly improves the reliability of tool interaction and offers a reproducible path for agents to learn from failure.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Failure Makes the Agent Stronger: Enhancing Accuracy through Structured Reflection for Reliable Tool Interactions" addresses the issue of error recovery in tool-augmented large language models (LLMs). It proposes a structured reflection approach, treating error diagnosis and correction as a trainable action rather than relying on heuristic prompting. The method involves the agent diagnosing errors based on previous steps and proposing correct follow-up calls. During training, a combination of DAPO and GSPO objective functions, along with a custom reward mechanism, optimizes the "Reflect → Call → Final" strategy.  To evaluate the approach, the authors introduce Tool-Reflection-Bench, a benchmark dataset designed to programmatically verify structural validity, executability, parameter correctness, and result consistency. Experimental results on BFCL v3 and Tool-Reflection-Bench demonstrate significant improvements in multi-turn tool-call success rates and error recovery.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its explicit and trainable approach to reflection in tool-augmented LLMs. Existing methods often rely on implicit or heuristic prompting for error correction, making them fragile in multi-turn interactions. Framing reflection as a first-class, optimizable action and incorporating it into the training process is a significant departure from existing practices. The proposed Tool-Reflection-Bench, designed specifically for evaluating error recovery in tool-calling scenarios, is another valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to improve the reliability and robustness of LLMs in real-world applications. Tool-augmented LLMs are becoming increasingly prevalent, and the ability to recover from errors is crucial for their practical utility. By making reflection explicit and trainable, the authors offer a pathway to creating more dependable and resilient agents. Improving the success rate of tool interactions in multi-turn scenarios directly addresses a critical limitation of current approaches.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a significant problem in existing tool-augmented LLM training methods: the lack of robust error recovery.
    *   The proposed structured reflection approach is well-motivated and addresses the limitations of heuristic prompting.
    *   The custom reward mechanism and training process are designed specifically for tool-calling scenarios, taking into account the unique challenges of this task.
    *   The Tool-Reflection-Bench provides a valuable resource for evaluating error recovery in LLMs.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the custom reward mechanism sounds promising, the description of the different reward components (format reward, tool-name reward, etc.) could be more comprehensive and intuitive. The paper relies too heavily on mathematical notation, which can make it difficult for readers to grasp the underlying concepts. A more intuitive, less equation-heavy explanation of the reward functions would greatly improve clarity.
    *   The success relies heavily on the Tool-Reflection-Bench dataset. The creation process is described, but the diversity and representativeness of the failure patterns are not thoroughly discussed.  It's unclear how well the crafted failures generalize to unseen, real-world error scenarios.  A more detailed analysis of the dataset's properties and limitations would be beneficial.
    *   The experiments, while promising, could benefit from comparisons to a wider range of baseline methods, including more recent self-correction techniques.
    *   The paper mentions that the original positive samples are derived from BUTTON [15] transformations and self-constructed based on few-shot prompts. The methodology behind the creation of these samples should be thoroughly explained, as it plays a vital role in the overall integrity of the experimentation process.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to focus on the importance of error recovery in tool-augmented LLMs and by providing a concrete approach for addressing this issue. The Tool-Reflection-Bench could become a standard benchmark for evaluating error recovery capabilities.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to error recovery in tool-augmented LLMs, addressing a significant limitation of existing methods. The introduction of the structured reflection framework and the Tool-Reflection-Bench is valuable contributions. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, the description of the reward mechanism could be clearer, and the analysis of the Tool-Reflection-Bench dataset's representativeness and limitations is insufficient. While the approach is significant, its widespread adoption and impact will depend on whether the method generalizes to unseen scenarios, and this requires more in-depth study of the test dataset. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's strong potential but also acknowledges the areas where further research and refinement are needed.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### NGRPO: Negative-enhanced Group Relative Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18851v1)
- **Authors**: Gongrui Nan, Siye Chen, Jing Huang, Mengyu Lu, Dexun Wang, Chunmei Xie, Weiqi Xiong, Xianzhou Zeng, Qixuan Zhou, Yadong Li, Xingzhong Xu
- **Abstract**: RLVR has enhanced the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across various tasks. However, GRPO, a representative RLVR algorithm, suffers from a critical limitation: when all responses within a group are either entirely correct or entirely incorrect, the model fails to learn from these homogeneous responses. This is particularly problematic for homogeneously incorrect groups, where GRPO's advantage function yields a value of zero, leading to null gradients and the loss of valuable learning signals. To overcome this issue, we propose NGRPO (Negative-enhanced Group Relative Policy Optimization), an algorithm designed to convert homogeneous errors into robust learning signals. First, NGRPO introduces Advantage Calibration. This mechanism hypothesizes the existence of a virtual maximum-reward sample during advantage calculation, thereby altering the mean and variance of rewards within a group and ensuring that the advantages for homogeneously incorrect samples are no longer zero. Second, NGRPO employs Asymmetric Clipping, which relaxes the update magnitude for positive samples while imposing stricter constraints on that of negative samples. This serves to stabilize the exploration pressure introduced by the advantage calibration. Our experiments on Qwen2.5-Math-7B demonstrate that NGRPO significantly outperforms baselines such as PPO, GRPO, DAPO, and PSR-NSR on mathematical benchmarks including MATH500, AMC23, and AIME2025. These results validate NGRPO's ability to learn from homogeneous errors, leading to stable and substantial improvements in mathematical reasoning. Our code is available at https://github.com/nangongrui-ngr/NGRPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NGRPO (Negative-enhanced Group Relative Policy Optimization), an algorithm designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by addressing a limitation in the existing GRPO algorithm. GRPO struggles when all responses within a group are either entirely correct or entirely incorrect, particularly in the case of homogeneously incorrect groups where the advantage function becomes zero. NGRPO overcomes this by introducing two main mechanisms: Advantage Calibration, which adds a virtual maximum-reward sample to the advantage calculation to ensure non-zero advantages for homogeneous groups, and Asymmetric Clipping, which adjusts the clipping range for positive and negative samples to stabilize training under the increased exploration pressure. The authors demonstrate that NGRPO significantly outperforms baselines like PPO, GRPO, DAPO, and PSR-NSR on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, highlighting its ability to learn from homogeneous errors and improve reasoning performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a specific and well-defined weakness in the GRPO algorithm, which is a valuable contribution to the field of reinforcement learning for LLMs. The problem of GRPO’s inability to learn from homogeneously incorrect groups is clearly articulated, and the proposed NGRPO algorithm provides a reasonable and intuitive solution.

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of Advantage Calibration and Asymmetric Clipping. While techniques like advantage normalization and clipping are common, the specific application of a virtual maximum-reward sample to alter the advantage calculation within GRPO's framework, coupled with tailored asymmetric clipping, appears to be novel.  The connection between generating *negative* learning signals from homogeneous errors and balancing exploration with *stability* is also well-articulated.  It is significantly better than simply adding a penalty (which may lead to instability). The novelty is *moderate*.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the robustness and reasoning capabilities of LLMs. By enabling learning from errors that are currently ignored by GRPO, NGRPO could lead to more effective training and improved performance on challenging tasks.  The experiments demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods on challenging mathematical benchmarks. This suggests a meaningful advance in the application of RL to LLMs for reasoning tasks. The significance is *moderate*.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-defined and motivated.
    *   The proposed solution is intuitive and well-explained.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a significant improvement over baselines.
    *   The ablation studies provide insight into the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * While the experiments are strong, the method is only demonstrated on mathematical reasoning tasks. The generalizability of the approach to other types of reasoning or different LLM architectures could be explored further.
    * The parameters, specifically epsilon_pos and epsilon_neg require careful tuning. The values are taken from another paper. While this is acceptable, it should be noted that the performance depends on the choice of these values.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of reinforcement learning for LLMs. It addresses a specific limitation of an existing algorithm and proposes a novel solution that is supported by strong experimental results. The paper's findings are likely to be of interest to researchers working on improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. While further exploration of the generalizability of the approach would be valuable, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conf-Profile: A Confidence-Driven Reasoning Paradigm for Label-Free User Profiling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18864v1)
- **Authors**: Yingxin Li, Jianbo Zhao, Xueyu Ren, Jie Tang, Wangjie You, Xu Chen, Kan Zhou, Chao Feng, Jiao Ran, Yuan Meng, Zhi Wang
- **Abstract**: User profiling, as a core technique for user understanding, aims to infer structural attributes from user information. Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a promising avenue for user profiling, yet the progress is hindered by the lack of comprehensive benchmarks. To bridge this gap, we propose ProfileBench, an industrial benchmark derived from a real-world video platform, encompassing heterogeneous user data and a well-structured profiling taxonomy. However, the profiling task remains challenging due to the difficulty of collecting large-scale ground-truth labels, and the heterogeneous and noisy user information can compromise the reliability of LLMs. To approach label-free and reliable user profiling, we propose a Confidence-driven Profile reasoning framework Conf-Profile, featuring a two-stage paradigm. We first synthesize high-quality labels by leveraging advanced LLMs with confidence hints, followed by confidence-weighted voting for accuracy improvement and confidence calibration for a balanced distribution. The multiple profile results, rationales, and confidence scores are aggregated and distilled into a lightweight LLM. We further enhance the reasoning ability via confidence-guided unsupervised reinforcement learning, which exploits confidence for difficulty filtering, quasi-ground truth voting, and reward weighting. Experimental results demonstrate that Conf-Profile delivers substantial performance through the two-stage training, improving F1 by 13.97 on Qwen3-8B.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CONF-PROFILE, a confidence-driven reasoning paradigm for label-free user profiling using Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the lack of comprehensive benchmarks in this area by proposing ProfileBench, an industrial benchmark derived from a real-world video platform.  The method uses a two-stage approach: (1) synthesizing high-quality pseudo-labels using advanced LLMs with confidence hints, then using confidence-weighted voting and calibration; (2) confidence-guided unsupervised reinforcement learning to further refine the reasoning ability of a distilled lightweight LLM. Experiments demonstrate that CONF-PROFILE improves performance significantly compared to baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Benchmark Contribution (ProfileBench):** Creating a real-world, industry-derived benchmark like ProfileBench is a valuable contribution. It provides a standardized and realistic environment for evaluating user profiling techniques, addressing a critical need in the field. The integration of heterogeneous user information and a systematic taxonomy enhances the benchmark's practical relevance.
    *   **Label-Free Approach:**  The paper tackles the key challenge of label scarcity by developing a label-free method. Synthesizing pseudo-labels with confidence hints is a clever way to leverage LLMs for initial supervision, reducing the need for costly manual annotations. The confidence-weighting and calibration further enhances the quality of these pseudo-labels.
    *   **Confidence as Uncertainty Signal:** The explicit use of confidence as an uncertainty signal throughout the pipeline is a core strength. Using confidence for difficulty filtering, quasi-ground truth generation, and reward weighting in reinforcement learning leads to more robust and reliable profiling results.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results, comparing CONF-PROFILE with strong baselines and analyzing the contribution of individual components through ablation studies. This thorough evaluation strengthens the validity of the proposed method.
    *  **Theoretically Sound Method:** The way the problem is framed and the proposed approach are both sound, making this approach a good start for addressing the label-free user profiling problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Strong Teacher LLM:** The initial data synthesis heavily relies on the capabilities of a strong "teacher" LLM (DeepSeek-R1). The quality of the pseudo-labels and, consequently, the performance of CONF-PROFILE, are directly influenced by the teacher's performance and biases.  The authors need to discuss the potential bias introduced by the DeepSeek-R1, particularly as this LLM comes from a specific cultural context.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Generalizability:** The evaluation is conducted primarily on ProfileBench, derived from a single video platform (Douyin). While this provides a realistic testbed, it raises questions about the generalizability of CONF-PROFILE to other domains and user data sources. Further experiments on diverse datasets would be needed to assess its broader applicability.
    *   **Closed-Set Assumptions:** The profiling taxonomy uses a closed-set approach, which may limit its ability to capture the full complexity of user attributes. While the inclusion of an "Unknown" option is helpful, extending the framework to open-set profiling could be a valuable direction for future research. The authors need to discuss the trade-offs between the benefits of a closed-set approach and the limitations to generalizability due to the simplified set of attributes.
    *   **Reliance on SFT and RL Training:** Both the SFT and RL stages are computationally intensive, and the performance is sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. Discussing the resource requirements and providing guidelines for optimizing these parameters would improve the practicality of the method.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The combination of confidence-driven pseudo-labeling, distillation, and confidence-guided reinforcement learning for label-free user profiling demonstrates significant novelty.
    *   The creation of ProfileBench addresses a key gap in standardized evaluation.
    *   The systematic use of confidence to manage uncertainty throughout the profiling pipeline distinguishes CONF-PROFILE from existing methods.

    However, it's worth noting that while the individual components (confidence estimation, pseudo-labeling, RL) are not entirely new, the specific combination and adaptation to the user profiling problem, along with the introduction of a dedicated benchmark, is significant.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated approach to label-free user profiling. The contributions include a valuable benchmark, a novel confidence-driven paradigm, and strong empirical results. However, the reliance on a strong teacher LLM, limited generalizability evaluation, and dependence on computationally intensive training stages should be addressed in future work.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**
The paper's value lies in its practical benchmark and methodological approach. The score reflects the significant progress made in label-free user profiling and the potential for future extensions. The 7.5 score is a balanced judgment, reflecting both the paper's strengths in tackling a critical problem and the identified weaknesses that need further refinement. The framework presents a significant advancement in leveraging large language models for user profiling, meriting the high, but not exceptional, ranking.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Memory in Large Language Models: Mechanisms, Evaluation and Evolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18868v1)
- **Authors**: Dianxing Zhang, Wendong Li, Kani Song, Jiaye Lu, Gang Li, Liuchun Yang, Sheng Li
- **Abstract**: Under a unified operational definition, we define LLM memory as a persistent state written during pretraining, finetuning, or inference that can later be addressed and that stably influences outputs. We propose a four-part taxonomy (parametric, contextual, external, procedural/episodic) and a memory quadruple (location, persistence, write/access path, controllability). We link mechanism, evaluation, and governance via the chain write -> read -> inhibit/update. To avoid distorted comparisons across heterogeneous setups, we adopt a three-setting protocol (parametric only, offline retrieval, online retrieval) that decouples capability from information availability on the same data and timeline. On this basis we build a layered evaluation: parametric (closed-book recall, edit differential, memorization/privacy), contextual (position curves and the mid-sequence drop), external (answer correctness vs snippet attribution/faithfulness), and procedural/episodic (cross-session consistency and timeline replay, E MARS+). The framework integrates temporal governance and leakage auditing (freshness hits, outdated answers, refusal slices) and uncertainty reporting via inter-rater agreement plus paired tests with multiple-comparison correction. For updating and forgetting, we present DMM Gov: coordinating DAPT/TAPT, PEFT, model editing (ROME, MEND, MEMIT, SERAC), and RAG to form an auditable loop covering admission thresholds, rollout, monitoring, rollback, and change audits, with specs for timeliness, conflict handling, and long-horizon consistency. Finally, we give four testable propositions: minimum identifiability; a minimal evaluation card; causally constrained editing with verifiable forgetting; and when retrieval with small-window replay outperforms ultra-long-context reading. This yields a reproducible, comparable, and governable coordinate system for research and deployment.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can help with that. Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Memory in Large Language Models: Mechanisms, Evaluation and Evolution."

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive framework for understanding and evaluating "memory" in large language models (LLMs). It offers a taxonomy that categorizes LLM memory into parametric, contextual, external, and procedural/episodic types. Central to the framework is the concept of a "memory quadruple" (storage location, persistence, write/access path, and controllability) and a methodology that connects mechanisms of memory, its evaluation, and governance.  The authors propose a three-setting parallel protocol (parameter-only, offline retrieval, online retrieval) designed to decouple model capability from information availability. A layered evaluation system is put forth to uniformly incorporate temporal governance and leakage auditing. For updating and forgetting, the paper proposes DMM-Gov, a dynamic governance framework coordinating various updating/editing and RAG techniques. Finally, the paper presents four testable propositions that aim to provide a shared coordinate system for memory research and deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper attempts an ambitious and much needed goal: to provide a unified and actionable framework for the increasingly complex and multifaceted landscape of LLM memory. The strength of this paper lies in its attempt to bridge the gap between academic research and the practical challenges of deploying LLMs in real-world, high-stakes environments.

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Taxonomy and Definitions:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by providing a clearer, operationalized definition of LLM memory and establishing a four-part taxonomy.  The "memory quadruple" is a helpful tool for characterizing different memory types.
*   **Comprehensive Framework:** The framework's integration of mechanisms, evaluation, and governance aspects creates a holistic perspective, encouraging a more systematic approach to LLM memory research.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper's emphasis on real-world applicability is a major advantage. The three-setting parallel protocol and the DMM-Gov dynamic governance framework are concrete proposals that could be used to guide LLM development and deployment.
*   **Addressing Key Challenges:** The paper confronts three critical challenges within the current research ecosystem: blurred conceptual boundaries, fragmented evaluation lenses, and bias in automated judging.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity and Scope:**  The framework is incredibly broad, which makes it challenging to fully grasp and implement. While aiming for comprehensiveness, the sheer number of concepts, metrics, and processes could overwhelm researchers and practitioners. This breadth might lead to a lack of depth in certain areas.
*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the paper proposes a framework and protocols, it does not present extensive empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness and practicality of the framework in a wide range of real-world scenarios. Without substantial empirical backing, the framework remains largely theoretical.
*   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** While the paper integrates several existing techniques, the novelty in many of the proposed solutions/frameworks feels incremental. The extent to which these techniques advance current practice may be limited.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty comes from the integrated framework itself, rather than radical new methods. By assembling existing techniques into a unified system, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field. The significance lies in its potential to promote more rigorous, comparable, and governable research in LLM memory, moving it beyond ad-hoc techniques toward a more principled engineering discipline. In its holistic treatment, this paper also attempts to bridge theoretical abstractions with operational deployment criteria - a quality that is highly valued by practitioners.

**Potential Influence:**

If adopted, the framework could:

*   Standardize terminology and evaluation practices in LLM memory research.
*   Guide the development of more robust and reliable LLM applications.
*   Facilitate better governance and auditing of LLM systems, particularly in sensitive domains.
*   Stimulate new research directions focused on addressing the identified gaps in knowledge and methodology.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning this paper a score of **7**. While the individual components are not revolutionary, the contribution lies in the creation of a unified and actionable framework. This paper provides a strong synthesis of existing ideas and concepts to create a practical roadmap. It earns a higher score for addressing the need for systematic and comprehensive approaches to LLM memory evaluation, governance, and responsible deployment. The score is lowered for lacking solid empirical validation, and the complexity of some of the described systems and protocols. There is considerable value for academic and industrial practitioners.

### When Ads Become Profiles: Large-Scale Audit of Algorithmic Biases and LLM Profiling Risks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18874v1)
- **Authors**: Baiyu Chen, Benjamin Tag, Hao Xue, Daniel Angus, Flora Salim
- **Abstract**: Automated ad targeting on social media is opaque, creating risks of exploitation and invisibility to external scrutiny. Users may be steered toward harmful content while independent auditing of these processes remains blocked. Large Language Models (LLMs) raise a new concern: the potential to reverse-engineer sensitive user attributes from exposure alone. We introduce a multi-stage auditing framework to investigate these risks. First, a large-scale audit of over 435,000 ad impressions delivered to 891 Australian Facebook users reveals algorithmic biases, including disproportionate Gambling and Politics ads shown to socioeconomically vulnerable and politically aligned groups. Second, a multimodal LLM can reconstruct users' demographic profiles from ad streams, outperforming census-based baselines and matching or exceeding human performance. Our results provide the first empirical evidence that ad streams constitute rich digital footprints for public AI inference, highlighting urgent privacy risks and the need for content-level auditing and governance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the algorithmic biases in Facebook ad targeting and the potential for Large Language Models (LLMs) to reconstruct users' demographic profiles from their ad streams, raising privacy concerns. It uses a multi-stage auditing framework on a large-scale dataset of over 435,000 ad impressions delivered to 891 Australian Facebook users. The study reveals:

1.  Algorithmic biases: Disproportionate targeting of socioeconomically vulnerable groups with gambling and political ads.
2.  LLM Profile Reconstruction Feasibility: LLMs can reconstruct demographic profiles from ad streams alone, outperforming census-based baselines and rivaling human performance.
3.  Risk Characterization: Longer ad sequences boost accuracy, highlighting the privacy risks posed by public AI inference on ad streams.

The paper concludes that ad streams constitute rich digital footprints accessible to public AI, emphasizing the need for content-level auditing and governance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in:

*   **Combining Statistical Auditing and LLMs:** It's innovative in integrating traditional statistical bias auditing with the powerful zero-shot inference capabilities of multimodal LLMs to assess privacy risks in advertising.  While some prior work has examined ad bias and LLM-based user profiling separately, this combination is novel.
*   **Empirical Evidence of LLM-Driven Profile Reconstruction:**  The paper provides the first robust empirical demonstration that multimodal LLMs can accurately reverse-engineer demographic profiles from ad streams alone. This was previously a suspected but undemonstrated risk.
*   **Content-Level Ad Sequence Analysis:** The study moves beyond examining individual ads to consider the *sequence* of ads, recognizing the temporal signal. This holistic content-level approach represents an advance in auditing techniques.

**Significance:**

*   **Raises Urgent Privacy Concerns:** The paper directly highlights a significant, previously unquantified privacy risk related to the increasing accessibility of powerful AI tools. This is significant for researchers, platform developers, policymakers, and consumers.
*   **Informs Policy and Governance:** The findings provide a concrete basis for advocating for stronger data protection frameworks, improved platform transparency, and algorithmic accountability mechanisms for ad targeting systems.
*   **Methodological Contribution:** The proposed auditing framework offers a valuable template for future research in this area, which can be applied to other platforms and contexts.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-Scale Empirical Validation:** The study leverages a substantial, real-world dataset, bolstering the credibility and generalizability of its findings.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The multi-stage approach, combining statistical analysis, LLM inference, and human evaluation, provides robust support for its claims. The use of census priors is strong.  The experimentation with randomized ad sequences offers additional insights.
*   **Clear Problem Definition and Scope:** The paper focuses on a well-defined, relevant problem, making it easier to understand its contributions.
*   **Human Evaluation:** Demonstrates that LLMs can match or exceed human capacity in certain inference tasks which amplify privacy risks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:**  The data is from a self-selected group of Australian Facebook users, which may limit generalizability to other populations and platforms.
*   **Causality not Established:**  While the study identifies algorithmic biases, it cannot definitively determine the *cause* of these biases (e.g., advertiser intent vs. platform optimization).
*   **Limited LLM Exploration:** The study primarily uses Gemini. While other models are incorporated at the human evaluation stage, the scope of model evaluation could be expanded.
*   **Potential for Future Work:** While the paper demonstrates the risks, it does not go very deep into potential mitigation strategies. This is understandable given the scope, but it represents an area for future research.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to:

*   Stimulate further research on privacy risks associated with LLM-based user profiling from various data sources.
*   Inform policy debates surrounding ad targeting transparency and algorithmic accountability.
*   Encourage platform developers to implement stronger safeguards to protect user privacy in ad targeting systems.
*   Highlight areas in which humans can still be the primary method for capturing sensitive information.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8. The paper presents novel findings using a robust and well-designed methodology. The demonstration of LLM-driven demographic profiling from ad streams poses significant privacy concerns, making the work highly relevant to the field of computational social science. The combination of bias auditing and LLMs is strong. While there are limitations regarding generalizability and causality, the paper's contributions are substantial and likely to influence future research and policy discussions.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### LongCat-Flash-Thinking Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18883v1)
- **Authors**: Meituan LongCat Team, Anchun Gui, Bei Li, Bingyang Tao, Bole Zhou, Borun Chen, Chao Zhang, Chao Zhang, Chengcheng Han, Chenhui Yang, Chi Zhang, Chong Peng, Chuyu Zhang, Cong Chen, Fengcun Li, Gang Xu, Guoyuan Lin, Hao Jiang, Hao Liang, Haomin Fu, Haoxiang Ma, Hong Liu, Hongyan Hao, Hongyin Tang, Hongyu Zang, Hongzhi Ni, Hui Su, Jiahao Liu, Jiahuan Li, Jialin Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Jianhao Xu, Jianing Wang, Jiaqi Sun, Jiaqi Zhang, Jiarong Shi, Jiawei Yang, Jingang Wang, Jinrui Ding, Jun Kuang, Jun Xu, Ke He, Kefeng Zhang, Keheng Wang, Keqing He, Li Wei, Liang Shi, Lin Qiu, Lingbin Kong, Lingchuan Liu, Linsen Guo, Longfei An, Mai Xia, Meng Zhou, Mengshen Zhu, Peng Pei, Pengcheng Jia, Qi Gu, Qi Guo, Qiong Huang, Quan Chen, Quanchi Weng, Rongxiang Weng, Ruichen Shao, Rumei Li, Shanglin Lei, Shuai Du, Shuaikang Liu, Shuang Zhou, Shuhao Hu, Siyu Xu, Songshan Gong, Tao Liang, Tianhao Hu, Wei He, Wei Shi, Wei Wang, Wei Wu, Wei Zhuo, Weifeng Tang, Wenjie Shi, Wenlong Zhu, Xi Su, Xiangcheng Liu, Xiangyu Xi, Xiangzhou Huang, Xiao Liu, Xiaochen Jiang, Xiaowei Shi, Xiaowen Shi, Xiaoyu Li, Xin Chen, Xinyue Zhao, Xuan Huang, Xuemiao Zhang, Xuezhi Cao, Xunliang Cai, Yajie Zhang, Yang Chen, Yang Liu, Yang Liu, Yang Zheng, Yaoming Wang, Yaqi Huo, Yerui Sun, Yifan Lu, Yiyang Li, Youshao Xiao, Yuanzhe Lei, Yuchen Xie, Yueqing Sun, Yufei Zhang, Yuhuai Wei, Yulei Qian, Yunke Zhao, Yuqing Ding, Yuwei Jiang, Zhaohua Yang, Zhengyu Chen, Zhijian Liu, Zhikang Xia, Zhongda Su, Ziran Li, Ziwen Wang, Ziyuan Zhuang, Zongyu Wang, Zunyuan Yang
- **Abstract**: We present LongCat-Flash-Thinking, an efficient 560-billion-parameter open-source Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reasoning model. Its advanced capabilities are cultivated through a meticulously crafted training process, beginning with long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) data cold-start and culminating in large-scale Reinforcement Learning (RL). We first employ a well-designed cold-start training strategy, which significantly enhances the reasoning potential and equips the model with specialized skills in both formal and agentic reasoning. Then, a core innovation is our domain-parallel training scheme, which decouples optimization across distinct domains (e.g., STEM, Code, Agentic) and subsequently fuses the resulting expert models into a single, nearly Pareto-optimal model. This entire process is powered by our Dynamic ORchestration for Asynchronous rollout (DORA) system, a large-scale RL framework that delivers a greater than threefold training speedup over synchronous methods on tens of thousands of accelerators. As a result, LongCat-Flash-Thinking achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-source models on a suite of complex reasoning tasks. The model exhibits exceptional efficiency in agentic reasoning, reducing average token consumption by 64.5% (from 19, 653 to 6, 965) on AIME-25, without degrading task accuracy. We release LongCat-Flash-Thinking to promote further advances in reasoning systems and agentic AI research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LongCat-Flash-Thinking technical report:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LongCat-Flash-Thinking, a 560-billion-parameter open-source Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reasoning model. Its training process involves a meticulously crafted two-stage process of Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) data cold-start training and then large-scale Reinforcement Learning (RL). A key innovation is a domain-parallel training scheme, decoupling optimization across STEM, code, and agentic tasks, followed by expert model fusion.  The training is enabled by their Dynamic ORchestration for Asynchronous rollout (DORA) system, a large-scale RL framework.  The model demonstrates state-of-the-art performance among open-source models on complex reasoning tasks and exhibits exceptional efficiency in agentic reasoning. The authors release the model to promote advancements in reasoning systems and agentic AI research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Demonstrated SOTA Performance:** The paper convincingly showcases LongCat-Flash-Thinking achieving state-of-the-art results on a comprehensive suite of reasoning benchmarks, especially compared to other open-source models. This is a significant achievement.
    *   **Innovative Training Methodology:**  The domain-parallel training with expert fusion is a novel approach that addresses the challenge of negative transfer in mixed-domain RL training. This addresses a real and well-known limitation in large-scale LLM training.  The DORA system addresses important practical bottlenecks in RL training.
    *   **Focus on Agentic Reasoning:** The explicit emphasis on improving agentic reasoning, specifically tool use, and demonstrating a reduction in token consumption without accuracy loss, is valuable. This tackles the practical problem of efficient and effective agentic AI.
    *   **Formal Reasoning Capabilities:** The development and integration of an expert-iteration pipeline with Lean4 for formal reasoning is a strength. Formal verification and reasoning are often lacking in LLMs, and this addresses a crucial area for improvement.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper evaluates the model across diverse tasks, including general QA, mathematical reasoning, coding, agentic reasoning, and safety, offering a well-rounded assessment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the core RL algorithm (GRPO) and many data curation techniques have roots in prior work. The degree to which the specific modifications to GRPO contribute to the overall success could be explored in more depth.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper would benefit from more ablation studies to isolate the contribution of each component (e.g., domain-parallel training *vs.* DORA, the specific loss term modifications *vs.* GRPO in general). This would strengthen the claims about the individual importance of each innovation.
    *   **Closed-Source Comparisons:** While the model beats open-source alternatives, comparisons against OpenAI and Google models are less meaningful without details of their training data/methods, making these less direct comparisons. This makes it less obvious how much of an advance LongCat-Flash-Thinking represents, with better comparisons to smaller models being needed.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty lies primarily in the **integration and orchestration** of multiple existing and slightly novel techniques into a cohesive training pipeline, combined with the application of industrial-scale RL.
    *   The **practical significance** of achieving state-of-the-art open-source performance with good efficiency is high.  The detailed description of the DORA system offers valuable insights for researchers and practitioners working on large-scale RL for LLMs.
    *   The **impact** on the broader AI research community will depend on the reproducibility and accessibility of the model and code, as well as the extent to which other researchers can adapt and build upon the techniques presented.
    *   Ultimately, while this work is a positive advance, a more open release of data and ablations would solidify the impact and advance the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects a combination of strong empirical results, a practical focus on efficiency and scalability, but also a reliance on existing techniques with limited ablation studies to pinpoint the exact contributions of each component. The model is significantly ahead of the open source competitors but comparison against the models is harder without clear comparison details, and with a lack of details on the precise architecture and training details. The release of the model *itself* is a significant contribution and worth a point on its own.

### Confidential LLM Inference: Performance and Cost Across CPU and GPU TEEs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18886v1)
- **Authors**: Marcin Chrapek, Marcin Copik, Etienne Mettaz, Torsten Hoefler
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed on converged Cloud and High-Performance Computing (HPC) infrastructure. However, as LLMs handle confidential inputs and are fine-tuned on costly, proprietary datasets, their heightened security requirements slow adoption in privacy-sensitive sectors such as healthcare and finance. We investigate methods to address this gap and propose Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) as a solution for securing end-to-end LLM inference. We validate their practicality by evaluating these compute-intensive workloads entirely within CPU and GPU TEEs. On the CPU side, we conduct an in-depth study running full Llama2 inference pipelines (7B, 13B, 70B) inside Intel's TDX and SGX, accelerated by Advanced Matrix Extensions (AMX). We derive 12 insights, including that across various data types, batch sizes, and input lengths, CPU TEEs impose under 10% throughput and 20% latency overheads, further reduced by AMX. We run LLM inference on NVIDIA H100 Confidential Compute GPUs, contextualizing our CPU findings and observing throughput penalties of 4-8% that diminish as batch and input sizes grow. By comparing performance, cost, and security trade-offs, we show how CPU TEEs can be more cost-effective or secure than their GPU counterparts. To our knowledge, our work is the first to comprehensively demonstrate the performance and practicality of modern TEEs across both CPUs and GPUs for enabling confidential LLMs (cLLMs).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance and cost-effectiveness of using Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) to enable confidential inference of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It focuses on practical deployments using Intel's TDX and SGX on CPUs (accelerated by AMX) and NVIDIA's Confidential Compute GPUs. The authors benchmark Llama2 (7B, 13B, and 70B parameters) within these TEEs, analyzing throughput and latency across different data types, batch sizes, and input lengths. The study derives key insights on performance overheads, identifies bottlenecks, and compares the cost, security, and performance trade-offs between CPU and GPU TEEs. The paper also demonstrates the use of TEEs for Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive empirical evaluation of modern TEEs (TDX and Confidential GPUs) for LLM inference. While TEEs for ML model protection have been explored before, this work is one of the first to thoroughly benchmark and analyze the performance and practicality of CPU and GPU TEEs *specifically* for resource-intensive LLMs and LLM pipelines like RAG. The inclusion of AMX acceleration and detailed performance tuning significantly adds to the value.

*   **Significance:** This research is significant because it addresses a crucial barrier to LLM adoption in privacy-sensitive domains: the need for confidential inference. By demonstrating manageable performance overheads (typically under 10% for CPUs and even less for GPUs with the correct configurations) within TEEs, the paper provides strong evidence that confidential LLM inference is becoming practically feasible.  The detailed performance analysis and cost comparisons offer actionable guidance for researchers, developers, and cloud providers aiming to deploy LLMs securely. The work also raises important practical challenges related to NUMA awareness and huge page support in TEE environments.  The comparison of CPU and GPU TEEs based on performance, security, and cost-efficiency, as well as application to a common LLM extension, RAG, strengthens the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper covers a wide range of scenarios, including different LLM sizes, hardware configurations (CPU & GPU TEEs), batch sizes, and input lengths.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The authors identify key bottlenecks (NUMA, memory encryption, EPC size), providing concrete recommendations for optimization.
    *   **Focus on Practicality:**  The study moves beyond theoretical discussions to real-world deployments, addressing the practical challenges of using TEEs for LLM inference.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors mention an open-source configuration, which is essential for reproducing and extending their results.
    *   **Security Considerations:** The discussion of security trade-offs, particularly regarding memory encryption and trust boundaries, is crucial.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Hardware Scope:**  While CPU and GPU TEEs from Intel and NVIDIA are central in CSP, exploring additional hardware TEEs (e.g. AMD SEV) could broaden applicability.
    *   **Trust Model and Attacks:**  The paper touches upon the trust model of TEEs but does not delve into potential attacks *within* the TEE environment or the complexities of remote attestation. A deeper exploration of attack vectors and mitigations specific to LLM inference would enhance the work.
    *   **Commercial GPUs Only:** The focus on Nvidia H100 CC limits the scope. Including alternatives would be more comprehensive.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper is likely to influence research and development in confidential computing and LLM deployments.  It provides a valuable baseline for future performance comparisons and optimization efforts. The insights gained can inform the design of more efficient TEEs and LLM inference frameworks. The study will encourage further exploration of using TEEs for LLM fine-tuning and other privacy-sensitive AI tasks.

**Score Rationale:**

The paper provides a strong empirical evaluation and valuable practical insights, but does not present a fundamentally groundbreaking theoretical contribution or cover aspects of attack vectors in detail. Its comprehensive approach, focus on practicality, and potential influence on the field warrant a high score. However, a narrow hardware scope limits broader application.
