### Extractive Fact Decomposition for Interpretable Natural Language Inference in one Forward Pass
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18901v1)
- **Authors**: Nicholas Popovič, Michael Färber
- **Abstract**: Recent works in Natural Language Inference (NLI) and related tasks, such as automated fact-checking, employ atomic fact decomposition to enhance interpretability and robustness. For this, existing methods rely on resource-intensive generative large language models (LLMs) to perform decomposition. We propose JEDI, an encoder-only architecture that jointly performs extractive atomic fact decomposition and interpretable inference without requiring generative models during inference. To facilitate training, we produce a large corpus of synthetic rationales covering multiple NLI benchmarks. Experimental results demonstrate that JEDI achieves competitive accuracy in distribution and significantly improves robustness out of distribution and in adversarial settings over models based solely on extractive rationale supervision. Our findings show that interpretability and robust generalization in NLI can be realized using encoder-only architectures and synthetic rationales. Code and data available at https://jedi.nicpopovic.com
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JEDI (Joint Encoder for Decomposition and Inference), a novel encoder-only architecture for Natural Language Inference (NLI) that jointly performs extractive atomic fact decomposition and interpretable inference. Unlike previous approaches that rely on computationally expensive generative Large Language Models (LLMs) for fact decomposition, JEDI directly extracts spans from the premise corresponding to atomic facts. These spans are then classified with respect to the hypothesis using logical rules, providing fact-level interpretability in a single encoder forward pass. To train JEDI, the authors create a large synthetic corpus of rationales (SYRP) covering multiple NLI benchmarks. Experimental results demonstrate that JEDI achieves competitive accuracy, improves robustness out-of-distribution and in adversarial settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in reframing atomic fact decomposition as an *extractive* task, enabling an encoder-only architecture to perform this task jointly with inference.  Existing work largely relies on generative LLMs for decomposition. The creation of the large SYRP dataset is also a significant contribution, as it addresses the scarcity of data for training interpretable NLI models. While other works have used synthetic data for related tasks, generating spans rather than full texts for fact decomposition is a relatively new avenue.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing key limitations of current atomic fact decomposition approaches: computational cost and potential inaccuracies (hallucinations) introduced by LLMs. JEDI offers a more efficient and potentially more reliable approach to interpretable NLI, making it more scalable and practical. The improved robustness, especially out-of-distribution, is a crucial advantage. The provision of the SYRP dataset is also highly significant, potentially facilitating further research in interpretable NLI and related tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Efficiency:** The encoder-only architecture avoids the computational overhead of LLMs, making it more scalable.
    *   **Interpretability:** The extractive nature provides direct traceability between predictions and spans in the input text.
    *   **Robustness:**  The model demonstrates improved generalization to out-of-distribution data.
    *   **Resource contribution:** The release of SYRP is a valuable resource for the community.
    * Joint training enables sharing signal between fact decomposition and reasoning, improving performance and reducing error propagation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Accuracy:** JEDI does not quite match the accuracy of FGLR (which uses LLMs at inference). This suggests there might be a performance cost associated with the efficiency and interpretability gains. However, JEDI exceeds the performance of a comparable architecture with explicitly defined sentence boundaries, showing that there's value in learning span extraction itself.
    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The model relies heavily on synthetic rationales. While the authors show that this is effective, the quality of these rationales is paramount. There might be biases introduced by the LLMs used for their generation that could negatively impact model performance on data with different characteristics from those used to generate SYRP.
    *   **Limited Scope of Interpretability:** JEDI's interpretability is limited to identifying relevant spans within the *premise*. It does not decompose the *hypothesis*, which might be a limitation in some scenarios, or an advantage if we focus on the premise.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has good potential to influence the field by providing a viable alternative to LLM-based fact decomposition. The architecture's efficiency and improved robustness are valuable contributions. The availability of the SYRP dataset could spur further research on interpretable NLI, including methods for generating or curating higher-quality synthetic data. The work can also potentially inspire similar approaches in other reasoning-related tasks.

*  **Further research**: While JEDI provides an interpretable analysis of atomic facts within the *premise*, it would be interesting to see this combined with a similar decomposition of *hypothesis*.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to interpretable NLI that effectively addresses some of the limitations of existing methods. The creation of SYRP is a valuable contribution, as is the demonstration of improved robustness. While JEDI does not fully match the accuracy of its LLM-based counterpart, the trade-offs in efficiency, scalability, and traceability make it a significant contribution. The limitations of relying solely on synthetic data and the scope of interpretability prevent a higher score. The approach clearly moves the field forward, and will likely be well-regarded by researchers seeking more practical interpretable NLI systems.

### Lang2Morph: Language-Driven Morphological Design of Robotic Hands
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18937v1)
- **Authors**: Yanyuan Qiao, Kieran Gilday, Yutong Xie, Josie Hughes
- **Abstract**: Designing robotic hand morphologies for diverse manipulation tasks requires balancing dexterity, manufacturability, and task-specific functionality. While open-source frameworks and parametric tools support reproducible design, they still rely on expert heuristics and manual tuning. Automated methods using optimization are often compute-intensive, simulation-dependent, and rarely target dexterous hands. Large language models (LLMs), with their broad knowledge of human-object interactions and strong generative capabilities, offer a promising alternative for zero-shot design reasoning. In this paper, we present Lang2Morph, a language-driven pipeline for robotic hand design. It uses LLMs to translate natural-language task descriptions into symbolic structures and OPH-compatible parameters, enabling 3D-printable task-specific morphologies. The pipeline consists of: (i) Morphology Design, which maps tasks into semantic tags, structural grammars, and OPH-compatible parameters; and (ii) Selection and Refinement, which evaluates design candidates based on semantic alignment and size compatibility, and optionally applies LLM-guided refinement when needed. We evaluate Lang2Morph across varied tasks, and results show that our approach can generate diverse, task-relevant morphologies. To our knowledge, this is the first attempt to develop an LLM-based framework for task-conditioned robotic hand design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Lang2Morph, a novel language-driven pipeline for designing robotic hand morphologies. It uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to translate natural language task descriptions into structured symbolic and geometric representations, enabling the automatic generation of 3D-printable, task-specific hand designs. The pipeline consists of two main stages: (1) LLM-powered Morphology Design, which analyzes task requirements and generates OPH-compatible parameters and (2) LLM-Guided Selection and Refinement, which evaluates and refines design candidates based on semantic alignment and size compatibility. The authors evaluate Lang2Morph across various tasks and demonstrate its ability to generate diverse, task-relevant morphologies, claiming it to be the first attempt to develop an LLM-based framework for task-conditioned robotic hand design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of LLMs to the *morphological* design of robotic hands.  While LLMs have seen increasing use in robotics for planning and control, their use for *design* is less common, particularly in the detailed design of manipulators.  The integration with the Open Parametric Hand (OPH) framework is a good choice, providing a structured and easily fabricable output. The two-stage pipeline combining semantic reasoning with geometric parameterization and validation is also a novel aspect. Other recent CAD systems using LLMs focus on generating object geometry rather than embodied morphologies.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is moderate to high. The paper addresses a longstanding challenge in robotics: the time-consuming and expert-dependent process of robotic hand design. Automating this process with LLMs could democratize access to task-specific manipulators and accelerate research and development in various fields. The ability to generate diverse and valid hand morphologies that align with task requirements without manual tuning is a valuable contribution. However, the method's dependency on the OPH framework may limit the types of designs that can be generated. A critical strength is that it generates *fabricable* designs instead of simply conceptual morphologies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Pipeline:** The Lang2Morph pipeline is well-defined and easy to understand. The combination of symbolic and geometric design representations is a strong feature.
    *   **LLM-Based Reasoning:** Effectively leverages the reasoning and semantic understanding capabilities of LLMs.
    *   **Diversity and Validity:** The experiments demonstrate the pipeline's ability to generate diverse and structurally valid hand designs.
    *   **Task-Specific Adaptation:** The results show that the generated morphologies adapt to the requirements of different tasks.
    *   **Qualitative Examples:** The qualitative results and visualizations effectively showcase the performance of the system on representative tasks.
    *   **Quantitative Metrics:** While the exact implementation and meaning of "Grasp Force Level" could be more rigorously defined, the presence of *some* morphology-based evaluation is a major strength over purely human-centered assessment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The dependence on the OPH framework constrains the possible morphologies. Designs are limited to the parameter space of this open parametric hand design. More freedom over design, or an attempt to generate novel mechanisms, would significantly boost the novelty.
    *   **"Grasp Force Level" Metric**: The use of a proxy like GFL instead of full simulation-based optimization has both strengths and weaknesses.  The authors are up-front that it doesn't replace full-blown simulation, but its accuracy and representativeness could be a point of weakness. It's not entirely clear that it accurately reflects actual grasp strength. Is there evidence that it's correlated with simulator results or physical testing?
    *   **LLM Reliance:**  The method is heavily dependent on the capabilities of LLMs. While LLMs are powerful, they can also be unpredictable and exhibit biases. The validation stage helps, but further robustness analysis may be required.
    *   **Manufacturing Considerations:**  While the pipeline generates fabricable designs, the evaluation does not include detailed analysis of manufacturing complexity, cost, or material properties.
    *   **Experimental Details**: Additional details on parameterization and prompt engineering for the LLMs would enhance reproducibility. The actual prompts could be included in the supplementary materials.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in robot morphology design, particularly in the application of LLMs to automate the design process. It could also inspire the development of more flexible and adaptable robotic hands for various applications. The open-source nature of the OPH framework facilitates the accessibility of this method.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of robotic hand design by presenting a novel LLM-based pipeline for automatic morphology generation.  The integration of LLMs with the OPH framework and the two-stage design process are key strengths.  The main limitation is the constrained design space due to the dependence on OPH. While not revolutionizing the field, the paper offers a significant and practical advancement.

- **Score**: 7/10

### No Labels Needed: Zero-Shot Image Classification with Collaborative Self-Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18938v1)
- **Authors**: Matheus Vinícius Todescato, Joel Luís Carbonera
- **Abstract**: While deep learning, including Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs), has significantly advanced classification performance, its typical reliance on extensive annotated datasets presents a major obstacle in many practical scenarios where such data is scarce. Vision-language models (VLMs) and transfer learning with pre-trained visual models appear as promising techniques to deal with this problem. This paper proposes a novel zero-shot image classification framework that combines a VLM and a pre-trained visual model within a self-learning cycle. Requiring only the set of class names and no labeled training data, our method utilizes a confidence-based pseudo-labeling strategy to train a lightweight classifier directly on the test data, enabling dynamic adaptation. The VLM identifies high-confidence samples, and the pre-trained visual model enhances their visual representations. These enhanced features then iteratively train the classifier, allowing the system to capture complementary semantic and visual cues without supervision. Notably, our approach avoids VLM fine-tuning and the use of large language models, relying on the visual-only model to reduce the dependence on semantic representation. Experimental evaluations on ten diverse datasets demonstrate that our approach outperforms the baseline zero-shot method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel zero-shot image classification framework named Collaborative Self-Learning. It combines a vision-language model (VLM), specifically CLIP, and a pre-trained visual model (ViT-G-14) within a self-learning cycle. The framework requires only the class names and doesn't need any labeled training data. It leverages a confidence-based pseudo-labeling strategy to train a lightweight classifier directly on the test data, facilitating dynamic adaptation.  CLIP is used to identify high-confidence samples, while the pre-trained visual model extracts informative features. This process is iteratively refined to capture complementary semantic and visual cues. The paper claims their approach outperforms the baseline zero-shot method CLIP across ten diverse datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific *combination* of existing techniques within a self-learning framework.  While zero-shot learning, VLMs, transfer learning, and self-training are individually established concepts, the way the authors integrate CLIP for sample selection, a pre-trained visual model for feature extraction, and a self-learning loop for refining the classifier *is* a novel contribution. The modular design is a plus, allowing for flexibility in substituting different pre-trained models.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical problem: the scarcity of labeled data. Zero-shot learning is a valuable area, and improving its performance is important.  The paper's results, showing an improvement over the CLIP baseline across several datasets, demonstrate the practical potential of their approach. The authors make the important distinction that by incorporating a visual-only model for feature extraction, the reliance on the VLM joint embedding space can be reduced, addressing some of the biases present in semantic representations. This is an important finding.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly identifies the challenges of data scarcity and the potential of VLMs for zero-shot classification.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:**  The proposed framework is described in detail, with clear explanations of each component and step. The algorithm description is beneficial.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes thorough experimental results across a variety of datasets and CLIP backbones, enabling a robust assessment of the approach. The inclusion of comparison with a recent state-of-the-art baseline strengthens the results.
    *   **Addresses a Limitation of VLMs**: The paper tackles the over-reliance on the joint-embedding space in VLMs by incorporating the transfer-learning approach with a visual-only model.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited ablation studies:** While there are experiments of k (number of images selected as a seed), there is not much discussion and exploration of hyperparameters such as the learning rate of the classifier.
    *   **Overfitting Potential:** Self-training methods are prone to confirmation bias, which is addressed in the paper with different stopping methods. However, there are few discussions on how to deal with it other than by limiting the training.
    *   **Reliance on CLIP:** While the paper aims to reduce reliance on CLIP's joint embedding space, the initial seed selection still heavily depends on CLIP's performance. If CLIP struggles with certain datasets or classes, the initial seed will be poor, potentially hindering the entire process.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in zero-shot learning by demonstrating an effective strategy for combining VLMs and pre-trained visual models. The modular design could inspire other researchers to explore alternative combinations of models. The focus on reducing reliance on semantic representations is a valuable direction. The code availability will also aid reproducibility and adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and practical approach to zero-shot image classification. The experimental results support the claims of improved performance over a strong baseline. The approach has clear strengths and addresses a significant challenge in the field. However, some concerns remain regarding hyperparameters and limited discussion on the issue of overfitting. Also, there is still a dependence on CLIP, even if less than the baseline method. Overall, it's a solid contribution that warrants recognition.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Data Efficient Adaptation in Large Language Models via Continuous Low-Rank Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18942v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Han, Zimo Zhao, Wanyu Wang, Maolin Wang, Zitao Liu, Yi Chang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have emphasized the critical role of fine-tuning (FT) techniques in adapting LLMs to specific tasks, especially when retraining from scratch is computationally infeasible. Fine-tuning enables LLMs to leverage task- or domain-specific data, producing models that more effectively meet the requirements of targeted applications. However, con- ventional FT approaches often suffer from catastrophic forgetting and suboptimal data efficiency, limiting their real-world applicability. To address these challenges, this paper proposes DEAL, a novel framework that integrates Low-Rank Adapta- tion (LoRA) with a continuous fine-tuning strategy. By incorporating knowledge retention and adaptive parameter update modules, the framework mitigates the lim- itations of existing FT methods while maintaining efficiency in privacy-preserving settings. Experiments on 15 diverse datasets show that DEAL consistently outper- forms baseline methods, yielding substantial gains in task accuracy and resource efficiency. These findings demonstrate the potential of our approach to advance continual adaptation in LLMs by enhancing task performance while improving resource efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DEAL (Data-Efficient Adaptation via Continuous Low-Rank Fine-Tuning), a novel continual learning framework for large language models (LLMs). DEAL addresses the challenges of catastrophic forgetting and suboptimal data efficiency in existing fine-tuning methods. It integrates Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with a continuous fine-tuning strategy that incorporates a wavelet kernel for knowledge retention and adaptive parameter updates. The framework aims to preserve historical knowledge while efficiently incorporating new information. The authors conduct experiments on 15 diverse datasets, demonstrating that DEAL consistently outperforms baseline methods in terms of task accuracy and resource efficiency. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The integration of a wavelet kernel within a LoRA-based continual learning framework appears to be a novel contribution. Using wavelet-based filtering to preserve core features of historical knowledge while updating the low-rank matrices is a sound approach. The controlled knowledge updating module with adaptive regularization further enhances the approach.
    * **Experimental Validation:** The extensive experiments on 15 datasets are a significant strength. Demonstrating consistent improvements across diverse tasks provides solid evidence for the effectiveness of DEAL. The ablation studies are also well-designed, helping to pinpoint the contributions of different components.
    * **Data Efficiency:** A key claimed benefit of DEAL is its ability to learn efficiently with limited data. The experimental results seem to back this claim.
    * **Interpretability:** The paper claims improved interpretability due to the use of a wavelet kernel, though it isn’t thoroughly demonstrated how the wavelet kernel facilitates interpretability beyond better performance.
    * **Practicality:** The code is publicly available. Moreover, DEAL leverages LoRA, making it a practical approach for fine-tuning large models with limited resources. DEAL ensures inference time remains unchanged, which is a significant factor in real-world applications.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Theoretical Depth:** While the paper proposes a novel framework, the theoretical justification for using the wavelet kernel and the specific forms of regularization could be strengthened. More rigorous analysis could further solidify the contribution. The theorem presented seems rather basic. A more impactful theorem relating to the practical performance of the method would improve the paper.
    * **Comparisons:** While comparisons to relevant LoRA continual learning baselines are included, comparisons to a broader range of CL methods with explanations of the different memory trade-offs would further enhance the evaluation.
    * **Overclaiming:**  The interpretability claims are somewhat weak and could benefit from more justification. A detailed explanation of how analyzing the wavelet-transformed low-rank matrices provides more insight into the model's learned knowledge than simply looking at the standard low-rank matrices would significantly bolster this aspect.  Moreover, while the paper mentions privacy-preserving settings, it doesn’t directly demonstrate how DEAL enhances privacy.
    * **Presentation:** Some parts of the paper lack clarity and could be more rigorously explained, such as the derivation of the equations and the details of the wavelet neural network.

* **Significance:**
    * **Impact on Continual Learning:** If the results hold up under further scrutiny, DEAL represents a significant step forward in making continual learning more practical for LLMs. Its ability to balance knowledge retention and efficient adaptation is crucial for real-world applications.
    * **Resource Efficiency:** The emphasis on data efficiency aligns with the growing need to reduce the computational costs of training and fine-tuning LLMs, potentially lowering barriers to entry for researchers and practitioners.
    * **Potential for Future Work:**  The DEAL framework provides a solid foundation for future research in continual learning, particularly exploring alternative knowledge retention mechanisms and adaptive regularization strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

DEAL presents a valuable contribution to the field of continual learning for LLMs through its novel integration of a wavelet kernel and adaptive regularization within a LoRA-based framework. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over existing baselines, and the focus on data efficiency aligns with critical needs in the field. However, some theoretical aspects require more depth, and the interpretability claim is not fully justified. While significant improvements are made, future works can expand on this research, particularly by improving the performance with additional methods.

### One-shot Embroidery Customization via Contrastive LoRA Modulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18948v1)
- **Authors**: Jun Ma, Qian He, Gaofeng He, Huang Chen, Chen Liu, Xiaogang Jin, Huamin Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have significantly advanced image manipulation techniques, and their ability to generate photorealistic images is beginning to transform retail workflows, particularly in presale visualization. Beyond artistic style transfer, the capability to perform fine-grained visual feature transfer is becoming increasingly important. Embroidery is a textile art form characterized by intricate interplay of diverse stitch patterns and material properties, which poses unique challenges for existing style transfer methods. To explore the customization for such fine-grained features, we propose a novel contrastive learning framework that disentangles fine-grained style and content features with a single reference image, building on the classic concept of image analogy. We first construct an image pair to define the target style, and then adopt a similarity metric based on the decoupled representations of pretrained diffusion models for style-content separation. Subsequently, we propose a two-stage contrastive LoRA modulation technique to capture fine-grained style features. In the first stage, we iteratively update the whole LoRA and the selected style blocks to initially separate style from content. In the second stage, we design a contrastive learning strategy to further decouple style and content through self-knowledge distillation. Finally, we build an inference pipeline to handle image or text inputs with only the style blocks. To evaluate our method on fine-grained style transfer, we build a benchmark for embroidery customization. Our approach surpasses prior methods on this task and further demonstrates strong generalization to three additional domains: artistic style transfer, sketch colorization, and appearance transfer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for one-shot embroidery customization using a contrastive learning framework based on LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) modulation. The method aims to disentangle fine-grained style features (stitches, yarns, materials) from design content using only a single reference embroidery image. The core idea involves creating an image pair (embroidery and corresponding graphic design), defining style through this pair.  The method uses a two-stage contrastive LoRA modulation technique (EmoLoRA) to capture style features in selected blocks and further decouple style from content through self-knowledge distillation. The method is evaluated on embroidery customization and also demonstrates strong generalization on artistic style transfer, sketch colorization, and appearance transfer. The paper contributes a new framework, a new task definition (one-shot embroidery customization), and strong performance compared to existing style transfer approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects.
    *   The problem definition itself:  Embroidery customization as a fine-grained style transfer task with its particular challenges (e.g., color as content, high-frequency textures as style) is arguably novel.
    *   The contrastive learning approach using a single image pair derived from "image analogy" concept for defining style is a key contribution. The motivation for using an image pair to clearly define the target style (rather than relying solely on a single image or textual descriptions) is well-articulated.
    *   The two-stage EmoLoRA modulation technique designed to address overfitting and "content leakage" in LoRA is innovative. The iterative approach of updating whole LoRA and selected style blocks is specific to this task.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multifaceted:

    *   **Retail Workflows:**  The introduction highlights the potential to transform retail workflows by enabling presale visualization and reducing inventory challenges. This has significant practical relevance.
    *   **Fine-Grained Style Transfer:** The method advances the capabilities of fine-grained style transfer, which is increasingly important in various applications (e.g., textile design, fashion).
    *   **Generalization:** The demonstration of strong generalization to artistic style transfer, sketch colorization, and appearance transfer broadens the impact of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the challenges in embroidery customization, distinguishing it from traditional style transfer.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The design choices for the contrastive learning framework and LoRA modulation technique are thoroughly motivated.
    *   **Strong Results:** Qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods in embroidery customization and good generalization to other style transfer tasks.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation with user studies, ablation studies, and comparisons to multiple state-of-the-art methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Metric Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations in existing metrics for evaluating embroidery style and design content, leading to the development of a new metric (HFRD). The reliance on HFRD may raise some concerns as it could be biased towards the method's specific design. While user studies compensate for this, a more broadly adopted or theoretically justified metric could strengthen the work.
    *   **Complexity:**  The framework involves multiple stages and components (SD3, ControlNets, EmoLoRA). A simplified version might be desirable if similar results could be achieved, but the paper does a good job of justifying each component.
    *   **Limited Styles:** As acknowledged in the limitations section, the method struggles with complex styles that combine multiple materials or overly abstract styles.  Addressing this limitation could be a direction for future research.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in fine-grained style transfer and customization, particularly in applications involving structural textures and textiles.  The proposed framework provides a solid foundation for future work.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a strong contribution with a novel problem formulation, a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated method, and promising results. While it has some limitations (complexity, metric reliance, difficulty with extremely complex styles), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The potential impact on retail workflows and the advancement of fine-grained style transfer justifies a high score.

### Benchmarking PDF Accessibility Evaluation A Dataset and Framework for Assessing Automated and LLM-Based Approaches for Accessibility Testing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18965v1)
- **Authors**: Anukriti Kumar, Tanushree Padath, Lucy Lu Wang
- **Abstract**: PDFs remain the dominant format for scholarly communication, despite significant accessibility challenges for blind and low-vision users. While various tools attempt to evaluate PDF accessibility, there is no standardized methodology to evaluate how different accessibility assessment approaches perform. Our work addresses this critical gap by introducing a novel benchmark dataset of scholarly PDFs with expert-validated accessibility annotations across seven criteria (alternative text quality, logical reading order, semantic tagging, table structure, functional hyperlinks, color contrast, and font readability), and a four-category evaluation framework with standardized labels (Passed, Failed, Not Present, Cannot Tell) to systematically assess accessibility evaluation approaches. Using our evaluation framework, we explore whether large language models (LLMs) are capable of supporting automated accessibility evaluation. We benchmark five LLMs, which demonstrate varying capabilities in correctly assessing different accessibility criteria, with GPT-4-Turbo achieving the highest overall accuracy (0.85). However, all models struggled in correctly categorizing documents with Not Present and Cannot Tell accessibility labels, particularly for alt text quality assessment. Our qualitative comparison with standard automated checkers reveals complementary strengths: rule-based tools excel at technical verification, while LLMs better evaluate semantic appropriateness and contextual relevance. Based on our findings, we propose a hybrid approach that would combine automated checkers, LLM evaluation, and human assessment as a future strategy for PDF accessibility evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for assessing the performance of automated and LLM-based approaches to PDF accessibility evaluation. The dataset comprises scholarly PDFs with expert-validated accessibility annotations across seven criteria aligned with WCAG 2.2 and PDF/UA standards. The authors evaluate five state-of-the-art LLMs using a zero-shot prompting approach and compare their performance with traditional automated checkers. The study reveals complementary strengths of LLMs and automated checkers, suggesting a hybrid approach for more effective PDF accessibility evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of the publicly available benchmark dataset for PDF accessibility evaluation. The dataset is expert-validated and spans across seven important accessibility criteria that are vital to people with disabilities who use PDF content. While there has been prior work in web accessibility benchmarking and some exploration of LLMs for accessibility tasks, this is the first comprehensive benchmark dataset dedicated to PDFs, and the criteria assessed are very important to accessibility for PDF content.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is twofold.

    1.  It provides a valuable resource for researchers and developers to evaluate and improve automated accessibility tools for PDFs, particularly LLMs. The lack of such a benchmark has been a major impediment to progress in this area.
    2.  The study offers important insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs for accessibility evaluation. The findings that LLMs excel at semantic understanding and contextual reasoning while automated checkers are better at technical verification highlight the potential for hybrid approaches.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined methodology:** The dataset creation, LLM evaluation, and comparison with automated checkers are conducted using a rigorous methodology.
*   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of LLM performance across different criteria, accessibility labels, and models.
*   **Practical implications:** The proposed hybrid evaluation framework offers a practical roadmap for improving PDF accessibility evaluation.
*   **Dataset Release:** Public release of the described dataset represents a high-impact effort that supports further advancement and research in the space.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The dataset size (125 PDFs) could be a limitation, particularly for training or fine-tuning LLMs. While the authors focused on quality and expert validation, expanding the dataset would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Scholarly Focus:** The exclusive focus on scholarly articles limits the generalizability to other PDF document types, such as government forms, legal documents, etc.
*   **Zero-Shot Limitation:** The reliance on zero-shot prompting may underestimate the potential of LLMs for accessibility evaluation. Further investigation is necessary of their fine-tuned capabilities for the task described.

**Justification of Score:**

The creation of this dataset represents a significant step forward in the field of PDF accessibility. However, the relatively small size and limited document types prevent it from being a truly transformative work. Also, while the evaluation of LLMs is valuable, the zero-shot approach could be explored further. The identified framework is also preliminary and requires more empirical validation to support the approach.

### LLM-based Agents Suffer from Hallucinations: A Survey of Taxonomy, Methods, and Directions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18970v1)
- **Authors**: Xixun Lin, Yucheng Ning, Jingwen Zhang, Yan Dong, Yilong Liu, Yongxuan Wu, Xiaohua Qi, Nan Sun, Yanmin Shang, Pengfei Cao, Lixin Zou, Xu Chen, Chuan Zhou, Jia Wu, Shirui Pan, Bin Wang, Yanan Cao, Kai Chen, Songlin Hu, Li Guo
- **Abstract**: Driven by the rapid advancements of Large Language Models (LLMs), LLM-based agents have emerged as powerful intelligent systems capable of human-like cognition, reasoning, and interaction. These agents are increasingly being deployed across diverse real-world applications, including student education, scientific research, and financial analysis. However, despite their remarkable potential, LLM-based agents remain vulnerable to hallucination issues, which can result in erroneous task execution and undermine the reliability of the overall system design. Addressing this critical challenge requires a deep understanding and a systematic consolidation of recent advances on LLM-based agents. To this end, we present the first comprehensive survey of hallucinations in LLM-based agents. By carefully analyzing the complete workflow of agents, we propose a new taxonomy that identifies different types of agent hallucinations occurring at different stages. Furthermore, we conduct an in-depth examination of eighteen triggering causes underlying the emergence of agent hallucinations. Through a detailed review of a large number of existing studies, we summarize approaches for hallucination mitigation and detection, and highlight promising directions for future research. We hope this survey will inspire further efforts toward addressing hallucinations in LLM-based agents, ultimately contributing to the development of more robust and reliable agent systems.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a comprehensive survey on hallucinations in LLM-based agents. It proposes a new taxonomy classifying agent hallucinations based on the agent's internal state and external behaviors, categorizing them into Reasoning, Execution, Perception, Memorization, and Communication hallucinations. The survey identifies eighteen triggering causes of these hallucinations and summarizes ten general approaches for mitigation, along with corresponding detection methods. Finally, it discusses promising research directions for future exploration in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The survey's novelty lies in its specific focus on hallucinations within the *context of LLM-based agents*, which is distinct from general LLM hallucination research. The proposed taxonomy is a valuable contribution, as it provides a structured way to understand and classify agent hallucinations, considering the complex interplay between the agent's internal and external actions. The identification of 18 triggering causes is also a significant effort.
*   **Significance:** Addressing hallucinations in LLM-based agents is crucial for real-world deployment and trustworthiness. This survey fills a gap in the existing literature, which primarily focuses on agent architectures and applications without sufficient attention to safety challenges like hallucinations. It contributes to the field by:

    *   Providing a consolidated view of the problem.
    *   Establishing a common vocabulary and framework.
    *   Guiding future research directions.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is comprehensive, covering a wide range of relevant research.
    *   The taxonomy is well-defined and helpful for understanding the complex nature of agent hallucinations.
    *   The identification of triggering causes offers actionable insights for mitigation strategies.
    *   The discussion of mitigation and detection methods provides a valuable overview of the current landscape.
    *   The paper clearly identifies and justifies future research directions.
    *   The open resource (GitHub repository) further enhances the survey's value to the community.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the survey is comprehensive, a limitation is that the field is rapidly evolving. Some of the discussed methods might be quickly superseded by newer developments. Therefore, the survey needs to be continuously updated.
    *   The practical impact of each method on hallucination reduction is sometimes not quantified, although this is also a reflection of the current research landscape.
    *   The taxonomy may not be exhaustive, and new types of agent hallucinations may emerge as the field evolves.
*   **Potential Influence:** The survey is likely to be highly influential, especially for researchers and practitioners working on LLM-based agents. It provides a clear roadmap for understanding, detecting, and mitigating hallucinations, ultimately contributing to the development of more robust and reliable agent systems. It can inspire future efforts and serve as a valuable starting point for new researchers entering the field.

Score: 8.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution by focusing specifically on hallucinations in LLM-based agents, which distinguishes it from prior work addressing LLM hallucinations in general. The proposed taxonomy and comprehensive analysis of triggering causes and mitigation techniques are well-structured and impactful. The paper's strengths lie in its comprehensiveness, clarity, and practical relevance, providing actionable insights for the community. While the evolving nature of the field presents a challenge for maintaining long-term accuracy, the paper significantly contributes to the current understanding and provides a strong foundation for future research. The GitHub resource adds significant practical value. The weaknesses are relatively minor, given the scope and depth of the work, justifying the score of 8.5.

- **Classification**: cs.AI

### From latent factors to language: a user study on LLM-generated explanations for an inherently interpretable matrix-based recommender system
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18980v1)
- **Authors**: Maxime Manderlier, Fabian Lecron, Olivier Vu Thanh, Nicolas Gillis
- **Abstract**: We investigate whether large language models (LLMs) can generate effective, user-facing explanations from a mathematically interpretable recommendation model. The model is based on constrained matrix factorization, where user types are explicitly represented and predicted item scores share the same scale as observed ratings, making the model's internal representations and predicted scores directly interpretable. This structure is translated into natural language explanations using carefully designed LLM prompts. Many works in explainable AI rely on automatic evaluation metrics, which often fail to capture users' actual needs and perceptions. In contrast, we adopt a user-centered approach: we conduct a study with 326 participants who assessed the quality of the explanations across five key dimensions-transparency, effectiveness, persuasion, trust, and satisfaction-as well as the recommendations themselves.To evaluate how different explanation strategies are perceived, we generate multiple explanation types from the same underlying model, varying the input information provided to the LLM. Our analysis reveals that all explanation types are generally well received, with moderate statistical differences between strategies. User comments further underscore how participants react to each type of explanation, offering complementary insights beyond the quantitative results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From latent factors to language: a user study on LLM-generated explanations for an inherently interpretable matrix-based recommender system":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of large language models (LLMs) to generate user-facing explanations for a mathematically interpretable recommendation model (BSSMF). The BSSMF model is based on constrained matrix factorization, where user types are explicitly represented and predicted item scores are constrained, which allows the internal representations to be more interpretable. The study investigates whether LLMs can effectively translate these internal representations into natural language explanations.  A user study involving 326 participants evaluated the quality of explanations across various dimensions like transparency, effectiveness, persuasion, trust, and satisfaction. The study compared different explanation strategies, varying the input information provided to the LLM, including model internals, user history, and a combination of both. The results indicate that all explanation types were generally well-received, with only moderate statistical differences between strategies, but with users having different feelings about the explanations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **User-Centric Approach:** The paper's primary strength lies in its emphasis on a user-centered approach, which is crucial for evaluating explainable AI. It directly assesses how users perceive and react to different types of explanations, moving beyond automated metrics that often fail to capture real-world user needs.
    *   **Inherently Interpretable Model:** Building upon a mathematically interpretable model (BSSMF) is a good choice. Starting from a transparent model avoids the common problem of "explaining the black box," leading to more faithful and trustworthy explanations.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study evaluates explanations along multiple dimensions (transparency, effectiveness, persuasion, trust, satisfaction), providing a holistic view of their impact.
    *   **Comparison of Strategies:** Comparing different explanation strategies (model-based, history-based, combined) is valuable for understanding the trade-offs between transparency, familiarity, and user preferences.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The inclusion of qualitative feedback provides important insights into how users perceive the explanations and complements the quantitative results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The study relies on a specific user base (students and staff at a French-speaking university) and a single domain (movies), potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings to other populations or contexts. The user base is a significant weakness.
    *   **Modest Improvements with Explanations:** The study indicates that even without explanations, the interpretable BSSMF model performs reasonably well. The improvements from adding LLM-generated explanations are only modest. This raises the question of whether the added complexity of LLM-based explanations is always justified.
    *   **Potential for Genericity/Vagueness:** The qualitative feedback highlights concerns about explanations being too generic or vague. While the LLM is fluent, it struggles to provide highly specific and concrete justifications, suggesting that more targeted prompting strategies or post-processing techniques may be needed.  It's also possible the dataset itself limits the LLM's ability to produce specific explanations.
    *   **Dependency on LLM Quality:** The quality of the explanations is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the LLM and the careful design of prompts. The authors acknowledge this dependency but don't delve deeply into the specific techniques used to optimize the LLM's performance beyond the initial model selection process.
    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the combination of an interpretable model and LLM explanations is useful, the approach builds on well-established methods in the field. The study's primary contribution is empirical.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The paper does not present a radically new theoretical framework or algorithm. Its novelty comes from the empirical evaluation of LLM-generated explanations specifically for an inherently interpretable model, compared to existing work largely focus on post-hoc explanations of black-box recommenders. Its significance lies in providing practical insights for designing explainable AI systems that are both transparent and user-friendly.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of explainable recommender systems through its user-centered evaluation of LLM-generated explanations for an inherently interpretable model. However, its limitations, including the user-base, limited generalizability and reliance on a particular LLM architecture, prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. Its strength rests in the application of LLMs to an inherently interpretable model and its user-centered methodology.

### Simulating Online Social Media Conversations on Controversial Topics Using AI Agents Calibrated on Real-World Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18985v1)
- **Authors**: Elisa Composta, Nicolo' Fontana, Francesco Corso, Francesco Pierri
- **Abstract**: Online social networks offer a valuable lens to analyze both individual and collective phenomena. Researchers often use simulators to explore controlled scenarios, and the integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) makes these simulations more realistic by enabling agents to understand and generate natural language content. In this work, we investigate the behavior of LLM-based agents in a simulated microblogging social network. We initialize agents with realistic profiles calibrated on real-world online conversations from the 2022 Italian political election and extend an existing simulator by introducing mechanisms for opinion modeling. We examine how LLM agents simulate online conversations, interact with others, and evolve their opinions under different scenarios. Our results show that LLM agents generate coherent content, form connections, and build a realistic social network structure. However, their generated content displays less heterogeneity in tone and toxicity compared to real data. We also find that LLM-based opinion dynamics evolve over time in ways similar to traditional mathematical models. Varying parameter configurations produces no significant changes, indicating that simulations require more careful cognitive modeling at initialization to replicate human behavior more faithfully. Overall, we demonstrate the potential of LLMs for simulating user behavior in social environments, while also identifying key challenges in capturing heterogeneity and complex dynamics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as agents in a simulated online social network environment (Y Social) to reproduce and study social dynamics on controversial topics. The agents are initialized with real-world data on political leaning and activity patterns derived from the 2022 Italian election. The paper extends existing simulators by incorporating opinion modeling mechanisms and examines how LLM agents interact, simulate conversations, and evolve their opinions under different scenarios ( varying LLM parameters, network structure, and recommendation systems).  The experiments compare the simulated outcomes against real-world data to assess the validity and limitations of LLM-based simulations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper makes a contribution by systematically exploring LLMs as agents within a social media simulator initialized with real-world data and extending existing tools with opinion modeling. While other studies have used LLMs for social simulation (cited in the related work), this paper uniquely leverages specific real-world data to seed the simulation (Italian election 2022) and compares performance against it. The combination of agent initialization using real-world data (activity, political leaning) and the comparison to Friedkin-Johnsen model for opinion evolution adds novelty.

* **Significance:** The work has the potential to be significant because it addresses critical questions in computational social science: how well can we realistically simulate online social interactions and opinion dynamics using LLMs? Being able to simulate complex interactions, and validate simulations against real world data is critical. Simulating potentially harmful interventions such as the spread of misinformation, or effect of ranking/recommendation algorithms becomes an ethical imperative.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Data-Driven Approach:** The use of real-world data (Italian election dataset) to initialize agent profiles enhances the realism of the simulation, allows for systematic comparisons, and provides a much stronger connection between real and simulated user behaviours.
    * **Systematic Experimentation:**  The paper explores various parameter configurations (LLM models, network initialization, recommender systems), enabling a comprehensive understanding of their impact on simulation fidelity.
    * **Multi-faceted Analysis:** The analysis covers in-group/out-group interactions, opinion dynamics (compared to a traditional model), and content toxicity.
    * **Clearly Defined Research Questions:** The research questions (RQ1 & RQ2) provide a clear focus for the investigation.
    * **Comparison of LLM driven opinion change with Friedkin-Johnsen Model:** Provides insight into the utility of LLMs for generating opinion changes that have characteristics similar to those found with traditional models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalizability:** The study focuses on a specific political context (Italian election 2022), which may limit the generalizability of the findings to other contexts. Though, this is stated as one of the future works and limitations of the research.
    * **Short Simulation Time:**  The simulation runs for 21 virtual days which may not be sufficient to capture longer-term dynamics or emergent behaviors. The paper acknowledges this limitation and justifies it with computational constraints.
    * **Moderate Fidelity for Out-Group Interactions and Toxicity:** The simulations struggle to accurately reproduce patterns of out-group interactions and toxic behavior compared to real-world data, indicating areas for improvement in modeling complex social dynamics. The reasons for this is open to interpretation.
    * **Limited scope for agent interaction:** The agents only perform the common interactions of posting, commenting, replying, reacting, and following which limits the scope of the study.
    * **Heterogeneity:** Generated content is not as heterogenous as real-world content. Cognitive modeling improvements are needed to better replicate human behavior.
    * **Reliance on existing models:** While comparing with Friedkin-Johnsen Model has its benefits, there could have been more scope for providing alternative/orthogonal insights into the dynamics being analysed.

* **Impact:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on LLM-driven social simulations. It helps to identify the potential of LLMs, highlight challenges (reproducing out-group dynamics and toxicity), and provides insights for future research directions (enhancing agent personalization, incorporating external shocks). The work is likely to influence the design and development of more realistic and accurate social media simulators.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field, but has some notable limitations. The use of real-world data and systematic experimentation strengthens the work. The identified limitations point to areas for future research and refinement of LLM-based simulation techniques. The comparison to a traditional mathematical model adds value. However, the relatively short simulation time, challenges in reproducing out-group dynamics and toxicity, and focus on a specific political context restrict the overall impact. The limitations of the models are also well-articulated and make for a worthwhile scientific contribution. The score reflects that there is a lot of room for expansion on the existing research.

### VIR-Bench: Evaluating Geospatial and Temporal Understanding of MLLMs via Travel Video Itinerary Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19002v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Wang, Eiki Murata, Lingfang Zhang, Ayako Sato, So Fukuda, Ziqi Yin, Wentao Hu, Keisuke Nakao, Yusuke Nakamura, Sebastian Zwirner, Yi-Chia Chen, Hiroyuki Otomo, Hiroki Ouchi, Daisuke Kawahara
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have significantly enhanced video understanding capabilities, opening new possibilities for practical applications. Yet current video benchmarks focus largely on indoor scenes or short-range outdoor activities, leaving the challenges associated with long-distance travel largely unexplored. Mastering extended geospatial-temporal trajectories is critical for next-generation MLLMs, underpinning real-world tasks such as embodied-AI planning and navigation. To bridge this gap, we present VIR-Bench, a novel benchmark consisting of 200 travel videos that frames itinerary reconstruction as a challenging task designed to evaluate and push forward MLLMs' geospatial-temporal intelligence. Experimental results reveal that state-of-the-art MLLMs, including proprietary ones, struggle to achieve high scores, underscoring the difficulty of handling videos that span extended spatial and temporal scales. Moreover, we conduct an in-depth case study in which we develop a prototype travel-planning agent that leverages the insights gained from VIR-Bench. The agent's markedly improved itinerary recommendations verify that our evaluation protocol not only benchmarks models effectively but also translates into concrete performance gains in user-facing applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VIR-Bench: Evaluating Geospatial and Temporal Understanding of MLLMs via Travel Video Itinerary Reconstruction":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VIR-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the geospatial and temporal understanding capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It focuses on the task of itinerary reconstruction from travel vlogs.  The benchmark consists of 200 travel videos from Japan, with manually annotated "visiting order graphs" that capture the locations visited at different granularities (prefecture, city, POI) and the relationships (inclusion and temporal transitions) between them.  The paper evaluates several state-of-the-art open-source and proprietary MLLMs on the tasks of node prediction (identifying locations) and edge prediction (inferring relationships).  The results show that current MLLMs, even proprietary ones, struggle with the task, particularly with transition edge prediction, highlighting challenges in long-context and temporal reasoning. As a proof of concept, the authors develop a travel planning agent that leverages the benchmark data (specifically, the visiting order graphs) along with video content to generate travel itineraries. Crowdsourced evaluations show that using both the itinerary information and the video leads to more attractive travel plans, demonstrating the practical utility of the benchmark and the task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects.  First, the *benchmark setting* itself is quite novel.  Existing video understanding benchmarks tend to focus on shorter time scales and often indoor or limited outdoor environments. VIR-Bench directly addresses the challenge of long-range, multi-day travel itineraries which requires reasoning about broader geospatial context and longer-term temporal dependencies. Second, the *task of itinerary reconstruction* from travel vlogs is innovative, moving beyond simple object recognition or activity classification. Finally, the *integration with a downstream application* (travel planning agent) provides strong evidence that the benchmark is relevant to real-world tasks.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical gap in MLLM evaluation.  Mastering geospatial and temporal reasoning is essential for embodied AI, autonomous navigation, and various other applications. By creating a dedicated benchmark for this type of reasoning, the authors encourage research in this important area. The paper also highlights the current limitations of MLLMs in this domain, prompting further investigation into architectures, pre-training strategies, and prompting techniques. The demonstration of a travel planning agent underscores the potential impact of improved MLLM performance in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Task and Dataset:** The visiting order graph structure provides a clear and quantifiable target for evaluation. The dataset is of reasonable size and focuses on a specific and challenging domain.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a range of open-source and proprietary MLLMs, providing a broad view of the current state of the art. The ablation studies and error analysis offer valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of the models.
    *   **Practical Application:**  The travel planning agent provides a compelling use case for the benchmark and the importance of itinerary reconstruction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Geographic Scope:** While focusing on Japan provides consistency, it also limits the generalizability of the benchmark.  Expanding the dataset to include travel vlogs from other countries with diverse geographical features and cultural contexts would be beneficial.
    *   **Data Size:** While 200 videos is a reasonable start, a larger dataset could further improve the robustness and reliability of the benchmark.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation metrics rely on exact string matching, which can be overly strict, especially for POI names.  Using more flexible metrics like fuzzy matching or considering semantic similarity could improve the evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:** VIR-Bench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating geospatial-temporal reasoning in MLLMs. It could also inspire the development of new MLLM architectures and training strategies tailored to this specific task. The benchmark data and insights could also be valuable for researchers working on travel planning agents, embodied AI, and other related applications.
*   **Rigorous Rationalization for the Score:** While a solid and valuable contribution, VIR-Bench does have room for improvement in dataset size and geographic diversity to broaden the models capabilities of the benchmark.

### Unveiling Chain of Step Reasoning for Vision-Language Models with Fine-grained Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19003v1)
- **Authors**: Honghao Chen, Xingzhou Lou, Xiaokun Feng, Kaiqi Huang, Xinlong Wang
- **Abstract**: Chain of thought reasoning has demonstrated remarkable success in large language models, yet its adaptation to vision-language reasoning remains an open challenge with unclear best practices. Existing attempts typically employ reasoning chains at a coarse-grained level, which struggles to perform fine-grained structured reasoning and, more importantly, are difficult to evaluate the reward and quality of intermediate reasoning. In this work, we delve into chain of step reasoning for vision-language models, enabling assessing reasoning step quality accurately and leading to effective reinforcement learning and inference-time scaling with fine-grained rewards. We present a simple, effective, and fully transparent framework, including the step-level reasoning data, process reward model (PRM), and reinforcement learning training. With the proposed approaches, our models set strong baselines with consistent improvements on challenging vision-language benchmarks. More importantly, we conduct a thorough empirical analysis and ablation study, unveiling the impact of each component and several intriguing properties of inference-time scaling. We believe this paper serves as a baseline for vision-language models and offers insights into more complex multimodal reasoning. Our dataset, PRM, and code will be available at https://github.com/baaivision/CoS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a score:

**Summary**

The paper "Unveiling Chain of Step Reasoning for Vision-Language Models with Fine-grained Rewards" addresses the challenge of enabling effective reasoning in Vision-Language Models (VLMs).  Current VLMs often produce coarse-grained reasoning chains, making it difficult to evaluate and optimize the reasoning process. The authors propose a Chain-of-Step (CoS) reasoning framework where the reasoning process is broken down into structured steps, each comprising a Name, Thought, and Reflection component. This structure facilitates fine-grained reward assignment using a Process Reward Model (PRM). The framework includes a step-level reasoning dataset (ShareGPT-Step-300K), a PRM trained on step-annotated data, and reinforcement learning (RL) training using the PRM-provided rewards. Experiments on various vision-language benchmarks demonstrate improved reasoning capabilities compared to existing methods, as well as insights into the scaling properties of VLMs. The key findings include the importance of step-level evaluation over solely relying on answer-level evaluation and the counterintuitive result that longer reasoning chains are not always better, emphasizing the need for high-quality steps.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects. The structured decomposition of reasoning into Name, Thought, and Reflection steps is a well-defined approach designed to improve structure and evaluation. The construction of the ShareGPT-Step-300K dataset specifically tailored for structured step-wise reasoning is a practical contribution. Using a PRM for fine-grained reward assignment within the RL framework, and using it to guide inference time scaling are significant advances. Finally, the investigation of scaling properties highlighting the importance of step quality over length adds an intriguing perspective.

*   **Significance:**  The ability to perform structured, step-by-step reasoning has significant implications for the field of VLMs.  It allows for a better understanding and control of the reasoning process, leading to more reliable and accurate results. The findings concerning reasoning length and step quality have implications for training strategies and model architectures.  The consistent improvements observed across multiple benchmarks suggest the general applicability of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The CoS framework provides a clear and structured approach to vision-language reasoning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, covering a wide range of benchmarks and ablation studies.
    *   **Empirical Insights:**  The analysis of reasoning length and step quality offers valuable insights into the behavior of VLMs.
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** Releasing the dataset, PRM, and code will facilitate further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Data Generation:** The initial ShareGPT-Step-300K dataset generation relies on GPT-4, which might introduce biases inherent to the model. A more diverse set of data generation methods could have strengthened the generalizability.
    *   **Complexity of PRM Training:** Training an effective PRM is computationally intensive and requires careful selection of base models. This adds complexity to the overall framework.
    *   **Potential for Reward Hacking:**  While fine-grained rewards help, there is still a risk that the model might learn to optimize the PRM score without necessarily improving true reasoning ability (a form of reward hacking).  More robust evaluation methods could be explored.
    *   **Limited exploration of other architectures**:  The models are based on LLaVA-NeXt and InternVL-2.5-MPO. Testing on different architectures would increase generalizability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research directions in vision-language reasoning.  The emphasis on structured reasoning and fine-grained evaluation could inspire new methods for training and improving VLMs. The provided resources (dataset, code, PRM) will likely be valuable for other researchers in the field.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of vision-language reasoning. The structured CoS framework, the detailed experimental evaluation, and the insightful findings on scaling properties make this work a significant step forward.  While there are some limitations, the novelty and potential influence of the paper are undeniable.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers solid contributions with well-designed experiments, providing valuable insight into vision-language models. The step-wise reasoning structure and the fine-grained reward mechanisms represent advancements over existing approaches. However, the reliance on large language models for data generation and reward modeling presents some limitations and could be a target for future research. Despite these shortcomings, the clear structure, comprehensive evaluations and tangible resources justify a relatively high score. Future studies that build on this work and address some of the outlined weaknesses could significantly amplify its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### OmniBridge: Unified Multimodal Understanding, Generation, and Retrieval via Latent Space Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19018v1)
- **Authors**: Teng Xiao, Zuchao Li, Lefei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (LLMs) have led to significant progress in understanding, generation, and retrieval tasks. However, current solutions often treat these tasks in isolation or require training LLMs from scratch, resulting in high computational costs and limited generalization across modalities. In this work, we present OmniBridge, a unified and modular multimodal framework that supports vision-language understanding, generation, and retrieval within a unified architecture. OmniBridge adopts a language-centric design that reuses pretrained LLMs and introduces a lightweight bidirectional latent alignment module. To address the challenge of task interference, we propose a two-stage decoupled training strategy: supervised fine-tuning and latent space alignment for aligning LLM behavior with multimodal reasoning, and semantic-guided diffusion training to align cross-modal latent spaces via learnable query embeddings. Extensive experiments across a wide range of benchmarks demonstrate that OmniBridge achieves competitive or state-of-the-art performance in all three tasks. Moreover, our results highlight the effectiveness of latent space alignment for unifying multimodal modeling under a shared representation space. Code and models are released at https://github.com/xiao-xt/OmniBridge.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "OmniBridge: Unified Multimodal Understanding, Generation, and Retrieval via Latent Space Alignment."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniBridge, a unified multimodal framework designed for vision-language understanding, generation, and retrieval. It leverages a language-centric architecture, reusing pre-trained LLMs and incorporating a lightweight bidirectional latent alignment module. A key aspect is a two-stage decoupled training strategy: the first stage involves supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning to align the LLM with multimodal reasoning tasks, and the second stage employs semantic-guided diffusion training to align cross-modal latent spaces using learnable query embeddings. The authors present extensive experimental results across various benchmarks, demonstrating competitive or state-of-the-art performance in all three target tasks. They emphasize the effectiveness of their latent space alignment approach for unifying multimodal modeling within a shared representation space.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a solid level of novelty, mainly in its architectural choices and the two-stage training procedure. While the individual components (LLMs, diffusion models, transformers) are not novel in themselves, the way they are integrated and trained shows innovation.

    *   *Strengths:* The two-stage decoupled training strategy is well-motivated and addresses a core challenge in unified multimodal learning - task interference. The bidirectional latent alignment module offers a good balance between leveraging a pre-trained LLM while incorporating cross-modal information. The semantic-guided diffusion training enables a better alignment, which leads to better performance.
    *   *Weaknesses:* The framework relies heavily on pre-trained components, which potentially limits its exploration of more radical architectural innovations. The reliance on the `<img>` tag approach, while common, isn't fundamentally novel for injecting visual context. Some specific components, such as prompt rewriting rules, are relatively simple and not heavily emphasized as key innovations. In addition, the ablation study could be better. It only presents the impact of latent space alignment and the bidirectional transformer to the framework, while the impact of the two-stage training method, learnable query embeddings is not investigated.

*   **Significance and Impact:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the field of multimodal learning: how to create unified models that can handle understanding, generation, and retrieval efficiently and effectively.

    *   *Strengths:* The paper's experimental results are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of OmniBridge across a wide range of benchmarks. The competitive or state-of-the-art performance in all three target tasks is a strong indicator of the framework's potential. The insights into latent space alignment could influence future research in multimodal representation learning. Code release makes the work more impactful.
    *   *Weaknesses:* While the framework achieves good performance, it needs more in-depth analysis of the trade-offs. For example, they mention dropping performance on certain OCR tasks, but deeper qualitative examples and analysis could be insightful. While it's computationally cheaper than pre-training from scratch, a concrete comparison of training costs to other existing unified models would strengthen the claims of efficiency. The limitations of multi-task interference may require more in-depth explanation.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured, making it easy to understand the proposed framework and the experimental results.

    *   *Strengths:* The figures and tables are well-designed and effectively illustrate the architecture and performance of OmniBridge. The writing is technically sound and easy to follow.
    *   *Weaknesses:* As mentioned above, some aspects of the design choices, as well as certain experimental outcomes (e.g., the OCR drop) warrant further, more detailed discussion.

**Overall Assessment:**

OmniBridge represents a significant step forward in unified multimodal learning by effectively integrating understanding, generation, and retrieval tasks. While it relies on existing components, its novel architecture and training strategy showcase a clear path toward more versatile and efficient multimodal models. Its strengths in the comprehensive experiment and clear writing balance its limitations on specific architectural novelties and in-depth analyses.
