### Weakly Supervised Food Image Segmentation using Vision Transformers and Segment Anything Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19028v1)
- **Authors**: Ioannis Sarafis, Alexandros Papadopoulos, Anastasios Delopoulos
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a weakly supervised semantic segmentation approach for food images which takes advantage of the zero-shot capabilities and promptability of the Segment Anything Model (SAM) along with the attention mechanisms of Vision Transformers (ViTs). Specifically, we use class activation maps (CAMs) from ViTs to generate prompts for SAM, resulting in masks suitable for food image segmentation. The ViT model, a Swin Transformer, is trained exclusively using image-level annotations, eliminating the need for pixel-level annotations during training. Additionally, to enhance the quality of the SAM-generated masks, we examine the use of image preprocessing techniques in combination with single-mask and multi-mask SAM generation strategies. The methodology is evaluated on the FoodSeg103 dataset, generating an average of 2.4 masks per image (excluding background), and achieving an mIoU of 0.54 for the multi-mask scenario. We envision the proposed approach as a tool to accelerate food image annotation tasks or as an integrated component in food and nutrition tracking applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a weakly supervised approach to food image segmentation.  It leverages Vision Transformers (ViTs) trained *only* on image-level labels to generate class activation maps (CAMs). These CAMs are then used as prompts for the Segment Anything Model (SAM) to generate segmentation masks. The authors explore the impact of image preprocessing (Gaussian blurring) and different SAM mask generation strategies (single vs. multi-mask proposals) on segmentation performance. They evaluate their method on the FoodSeg103 dataset, showing that their approach can achieve reasonable segmentation accuracy without pixel-level annotations during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of this work lies in combining a weakly supervised learning strategy, CAM generation via ViTs, and the promptability of SAM specifically for food image segmentation. While the individual components (ViTs, SAM, CAMs for WSSS) are well-established, their integration for this specific task, especially focusing on the image-level annotation constraint, has some novelty. Using image-level annotations to train the ViT and then using the resulting CAMs to prompt SAM is a reasonable approach to avoid the expensive pixel-level annotation. Exploring Gaussian blurring as a preprocessing step to aid SAM in this context also contributes modestly to the novelty. It's important to note that similar ideas have been explored in other domains (medical imaging, natural images), so the novelty is primarily in the application domain and specific configurations.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to reduce the annotation burden for food image segmentation. High-quality pixel-level annotations are a bottleneck, and this paper explores a pathway to bypass this requirement.  The results, especially the mIoU of 0.54 for the multi-mask scenario, suggest the viability of this approach as a semi-automatic annotation tool or a component in food tracking applications. The ablation study on single and multi-masking is significant, showing where improvements can be made. The findings regarding the smoothing help justify the preprocessing approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of limited pixel-level annotations in food image segmentation.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described, making it relatively easy to reproduce.
*   **Ablation Study:** The investigation of different configurations (preprocessing, single vs. multi-mask) is crucial and strengthens the paper.
*   **Relevant Evaluation Metric:** Focusing on the performance of true positive classifications aligns with the intended application (annotation assistance).
*   **Practical Application:** The paper clearly articulates the potential applications, which are relevant to the field of dietary monitoring and nutrition tracking.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Core Components:** While the combination is novel, the core components are well-established.
*   **mIoU Performance:** The mIoU of 0.54, while promising, is not groundbreaking. It suggests the method may need further refinement to be truly practical for many applications.
*   **Lack of Comparison with SOTA WSSS specifically on Food Images:** The paper acknowledges prior work in [2] but doesn't directly compare against it in terms of performance metrics in this paper. A comparative analysis with other WSSS techniques specifically tailored for food images could provide a stronger assessment of its value. Other methods in related fields (e.g. [11] in the text) should also be compared.
*   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:**  A more in-depth discussion of the types of errors SAM makes, and how these errors relate to the image content or ViT predictions, would be beneficial.  Qualitative examples alone do not provide a full picture of the limitations.
*   **Dependence on Classifier Accuracy:** The paper acknowledges the strong dependence on the classifier's accuracy. If the classifier fails to detect the correct class, the segmentation is unlikely to be successful.
*   **Generalization issues:** The food dataset is relatively small, and there may be generalization issues with other more diverse food datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable and well-executed approach to weakly supervised food image segmentation. While the core components are not entirely novel, their combination and evaluation in this specific domain are valuable. The results are promising, but the mIoU scores suggest room for improvement. The paper suffers from a lack of rigorous comparison with other state-of-the-art WSSS methods. A more thorough analysis of failure cases and a more comprehensive consideration of other prompting methods for SAM would strengthen the contribution.

### Improving Credit Card Fraud Detection through Transformer-Enhanced GAN Oversampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19032v1)
- **Authors**: Kashaf Ul Emaan
- **Abstract**: Detection of credit card fraud is an acute issue of financial security because transaction datasets are highly lopsided, with fraud cases being only a drop in the ocean. Balancing datasets using the most popular methods of traditional oversampling such as the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) generally create simplistic synthetic samples that are not readily applicable to complex fraud patterns. Recent industry advances that include Conditional Tabular Generative Adversarial Networks (CTGAN) and Tabular Variational Autoencoders (TVAE) have demonstrated increased efficiency in tabular synthesis, yet all these models still exhibit issues with high-dimensional dependence modelling. Now we will present our hybrid approach where we use a Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) with a Transformer encoder block to produce realistic fraudulent transactions samples. The GAN architecture allows training realistic generators adversarial, and the Transformer allows the model to learn rich feature interactions by self-attention. Such a hybrid strategy overcomes the limitations of SMOTE, CTGAN, and TVAE by producing a variety of high-quality synthetic minority classes samples. We test our algorithm on the publicly-available Credit Card Fraud Detection dataset and compare it to conventional and generative resampling strategies with a variety of classifiers, such as Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), and Support Vector Machine (SVM). Findings indicate that our Transformer-based GAN shows substantial gains in Recall, F1-score and Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUC), which indicates that it is effective in overcoming the severe class imbalance inherent in the task of fraud detection.
- **Summary**: Okay, I have reviewed the paper titled "Improving Credit Card Fraud Detection through Transformer-Enhanced GAN Oversampling". Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the challenge of class imbalance in credit card fraud detection, where fraudulent transactions are significantly outnumbered by legitimate ones. It proposes a hybrid approach using a Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) enhanced with a Transformer encoder block (T-GAN). The T-GAN aims to generate realistic synthetic fraudulent transactions to balance the dataset, overcoming the limitations of traditional oversampling methods like SMOTE and generative methods like CTGAN and TVAE, which struggle with complex data dependencies and high dimensionality. The authors test their algorithm on a publicly available credit card fraud dataset and compare its performance against SMOTE, CTGAN, and TVAE using various classifiers (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, SVM). The results demonstrate that the T-GAN improves Recall, F1-score, and AUC, indicating its effectiveness in addressing the class imbalance problem.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Credit card fraud detection is a critical area with significant financial implications.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The combination of GANs and Transformers for tabular data synthesis in fraud detection is relatively novel. Leveraging the self-attention mechanism of transformers to capture long-range feature dependencies within transaction data is a sound and logical approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a fairly thorough experimental evaluation, comparing the proposed method against several baselines (SMOTE, CTGAN, TVAE) and classifiers (LR, RF, XGBoost, SVM). Using multiple evaluation metrics (Recall, F1-score, AUC) is commendable, as accuracy alone is insufficient for imbalanced datasets.
    *   **Improved Performance:** The results demonstrate a significant improvement in performance, particularly in Recall, which is crucial for fraud detection (minimizing false negatives). The consistently high AUC scores across different classifiers also suggest the generalizability of the generated data.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, making the methodology and results relatively easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Variety:** The evaluation is performed on a single, albeit common, credit card fraud dataset. Testing the method on diverse datasets with varying characteristics (feature distributions, fraud rates, etc.) would strengthen the claims of robustness and generalizability.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning Details:** The paper lacks detail regarding the hyperparameter tuning process for the T-GAN and other baselines. The choices for architecture and parameters used are not adequately justified. The absence of ablation studies also makes it challenging to understand the contribution of specific components within the T-GAN.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper highlights the performance benefits, it does not adequately address the computational cost associated with training and deploying the T-GAN. Generative models, especially those with complex architectures like Transformers, can be computationally expensive.
    *   **Limited Novelty in GAN architecture:** The approach uses FastGAN-based architecture. The description of the GAN architecture itself, the loss function, and the training details are somewhat lacking. The novelty is mainly in the Transformer integration, not in the GAN itself.
    *   **Lack of real-time testing:** The real-time impact of the method in actual systems has not been presented.

*   **Novelty:**

    * The combination of GAN and transformer blocks for fraud detection is partially novel in this paper. Some literature does exist that shows GANs and transformers used for tabular data generation but little exists to test their performance for real-time results.

*   **Significance:**

    * Improving fraud detection is of significant financial and societal importance. The paper presents a promising approach for addressing class imbalance and enhancing the detection of fraudulent transactions. However, the limitations highlighted above hinder the overall significance of the contribution.

**Justification of Score**

Given the above analysis, the paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution. The core idea of using a Transformer-enhanced GAN for oversampling in fraud detection is sound, and the experimental results demonstrate improved performance compared to several baselines. However, the lack of detailed architectural and implementation details, limited dataset variety, and lack of real-time testing hinder the overall impact of the study. The absence of computational cost analysis is also a notable omission.

Overall, the paper demonstrates an interesting and promising idea, and further work is needed to address the above limitations and fully realize the potential of the proposed approach.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Score**: 6/10

### Charting a Decade of Computational Linguistics in Italy: The CLiC-it Corpus
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19033v1)
- **Authors**: Chiara Alzetta, Serena Auriemma, Alessandro Bondielli, Luca Dini, Chiara Fazzone, Alessio Miaschi, Martina Miliani, Marta Sartor
- **Abstract**: Over the past decade, Computational Linguistics (CL) and Natural Language Processing (NLP) have evolved rapidly, especially with the advent of Transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs). This shift has transformed research goals and priorities, from Lexical and Semantic Resources to Language Modelling and Multimodality. In this study, we track the research trends of the Italian CL and NLP community through an analysis of the contributions to CLiC-it, arguably the leading Italian conference in the field. We compile the proceedings from the first 10 editions of the CLiC-it conference (from 2014 to 2024) into the CLiC-it Corpus, providing a comprehensive analysis of both its metadata, including author provenance, gender, affiliations, and more, as well as the content of the papers themselves, which address various topics. Our goal is to provide the Italian and international research communities with valuable insights into emerging trends and key developments over time, supporting informed decisions and future directions in the field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the CLiC-it Corpus, a collection of metadata and textual content derived from the first ten editions of the Italian Conference on Computational Linguistics (CLiC-it). The authors analyze the corpus to track research trends within the Italian CL and NLP community over the past decade. They investigate authorship patterns (gender, affiliations, and international participation), collaboration networks between institutions, and the evolution of research topics using topic modeling techniques. The goal is to provide insights into emerging trends, key developments, and support informed decisions for future research directions in the field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Valuable Resource:** The CLiC-it Corpus itself is a significant contribution. It provides a centralized and structured resource that wasn't previously available, making it easier for researchers to study the evolution of the Italian CL/NLP community.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper offers a multi-faceted analysis, covering authorship, collaboration, and thematic trends. The use of network analysis to examine institutional collaborations is a particularly insightful approach.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The authors employ a combination of manual and semi-automatic extraction techniques for corpus creation, topic modeling (BERTopic), and network analysis, providing a well-rounded methodological framework.
    *   **Relevance and Context:** The analysis effectively connects the trends observed in the CLiC-it corpus to broader developments in the global NLP landscape, acknowledging the influence of Transformer-based models, and the growth in sentiment analysis, and more recently LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope Limitation:** The study is inherently limited by its focus on a single conference (CLiC-it). While CLiC-it is a leading Italian conference, it may not fully represent all NLP research activities in the country.
    *   **Subjectivity in Topic Modeling:** The manual merging and labeling of topic clusters can introduce subjectivity. While ChatGPT was used to generate labels, the final decisions still rely on human interpretation.
    *   **Limited Depth of Content Analysis:** While the paper identifies key topics, the analysis of the content itself could be deeper. For example, the analysis of papers on Machine Translation lacks detail as to which aspects of the field they are exploring.
    *   **Generalizability of Findings:**  The specific trends observed in the Italian CL/NLP community might not be directly generalizable to other national or linguistic contexts, requiring careful consideration when drawing broader conclusions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The creation and release of the CLiC-it corpus provide a tangible and reusable resource that didn't previously exist.
    *   The longitudinal analysis offers a unique perspective on the evolution of the Italian NLP community, identifying both continuity and change in research focus.
    *   The identification of key players and collaboration patterns within the community provides valuable insights for researchers and funding agencies.
    *   The insights into topics like Large Language models and how the community responded in terms of research.

**Justification:**

The paper provides a solid contribution through both the creation of a useful corpus and a detailed analysis of its metadata and content. However, the limitations stemming from the reliance on a single conference prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution to the wider field of study. While the topic analysis is interesting, the paper would have benefited from a deeper analysis into some of the main topics and how they evolved.

### Latent Danger Zone: Distilling Unified Attention for Cross-Architecture Black-box Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19044v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Li, Chenyu Wang, Tingrui Wang, Yongwei Wang, Haonan Li, Zhunga Liu, Quan Pan
- **Abstract**: Black-box adversarial attacks remain challenging due to limited access to model internals. Existing methods often depend on specific network architectures or require numerous queries, resulting in limited cross-architecture transferability and high query costs. To address these limitations, we propose JAD, a latent diffusion model framework for black-box adversarial attacks. JAD generates adversarial examples by leveraging a latent diffusion model guided by attention maps distilled from both a convolutional neural network (CNN) and a Vision Transformer (ViT) models. By focusing on image regions that are commonly sensitive across architectures, this approach crafts adversarial perturbations that transfer effectively between different model types. This joint attention distillation strategy enables JAD to be architecture-agnostic, achieving superior attack generalization across diverse models. Moreover, the generative nature of the diffusion framework yields high adversarial sample generation efficiency by reducing reliance on iterative queries. Experiments demonstrate that JAD offers improved attack generalization, generation efficiency, and cross-architecture transferability compared to existing methods, providing a promising and effective paradigm for black-box adversarial attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JAD, a Joint Attention Distillation framework for black-box adversarial attacks that targets cross-architecture transferability (CNNs vs. ViTs) and high query efficiency. JAD leverages a latent diffusion model guided by attention maps distilled from both CNN and ViT surrogate models. This distillation process aims to focus perturbations on image regions commonly sensitive across different network architectures. The paper shows that JAD achieves improved attack generalization, generation efficiency, and cross-architecture transferability compared to existing methods.  Extensive experiments across ImageNet, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 datasets demonstrate JAD's superior performance against state-of-the-art black-box attacks. Furthermore, the paper also presents ablation studies and analysis to understand the contributions of different components of JAD and also an interesting insight that  "cross-architecture attention guidance" did not bring the expected improvement; on the contrary, it led to decreased attack performance in certain configurations, and in these cases removing attention guidance entirely led to higher attack success.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the joint attention distillation technique that aims to bridge the architectural gap between CNNs and Transformers in black-box attacks. While latent diffusion models and attention distillation are not new concepts *per se*, their combination and adaptation specifically to create cross-architecture transferable adversarial examples is a significant contribution. Previous generative approaches often overfit to architecture-specific biases, and JAD directly tackles this limitation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in adversarial attacks: the limited transferability of perturbations across different model architectures, which has been a barrier to practical black-box attacks. By creating a method that is more architecture-agnostic, JAD enhances the robustness of attacks and potentially exposes vulnerabilities in deployed systems that mix CNNs and ViTs. The improved query efficiency is also a significant contribution, making attacks more practical in scenarios with limited access to the target model.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple datasets and architectures.
    *   Detailed ablation studies providing insights into the contributions of different components.
    *   The attention-guided loss function is well-motivated and effectively aligns perturbations with critical regions.
    *   The code should be publicly available, increasing reproducibility and adoption.
    *   The insight on the limitations of cross-architecture attention guidance during testing phase if there's misalignment of attention regions with the victim model's decision logic is valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on white-box attacks to generate training data may limit the method's ability to generalize to entirely unknown architectures (those not used in the training phase). While the method aims for architecture-agnosticism, there's still an inductive bias introduced by the CNN and ViT used for attention distillation.
    *   While query efficient, the JAD approach does require an initial training phase of the latent diffusion model which will require a lot of compute if the training of the diffusion model has to happen from scratch. (The paper mentions they use stable diffusion which has been pre-trained).
    *   The paper can improve by showing that adding cross-architecture attention guidance in testing phase hurts model performance, maybe with a plot of ASR with different number of queries for this setting.

*   **Impact:** The JAD framework provides a promising new direction for black-box adversarial attacks, and is likely to influence future research in this area. The attention distillation technique could be adapted to other tasks where cross-modal or cross-domain generalization is important. This can be further applied to other model types like LMMs, diffusion models to expose vulnerabilities in these architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper builds upon existing techniques (latent diffusion models, attention distillation), its application to cross-architecture black-box attacks is innovative. The combination of these techniques, along with the careful design of the attention-guided loss and the focus on query efficiency, yields significant improvements over existing methods. The extensive empirical evaluation and ablation studies add further value. However, the dependence on surrogate models introduces a limitation.

Considering these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 8 is appropriate.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### RELATE: Relation Extraction in Biomedical Abstracts with LLMs and Ontology Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19057v1)
- **Authors**: Olawumi Olasunkanmi, Mathew Satursky, Hong Yi, Chris Bizon, Harlin Lee, Stanley Ahalt
- **Abstract**: Biomedical knowledge graphs (KGs) are vital for drug discovery and clinical decision support but remain incomplete. Large language models (LLMs) excel at extracting biomedical relations, yet their outputs lack standardization and alignment with ontologies, limiting KG integration. We introduce RELATE, a three-stage pipeline that maps LLM-extracted relations to standardized ontology predicates using ChemProt and the Biolink Model. The pipeline includes: (1) ontology preprocessing with predicate embeddings, (2) similarity-based retrieval enhanced with SapBERT, and (3) LLM-based reranking with explicit negation handling. This approach transforms relation extraction from free-text outputs to structured, ontology-constrained representations. On the ChemProt benchmark, RELATE achieves 52% exact match and 94% accuracy@10, and in 2,400 HEAL Project abstracts, it effectively rejects irrelevant associations (0.4%) and identifies negated assertions. RELATE captures nuanced biomedical relationships while ensuring quality for KG augmentation. By combining vector search with contextual LLM reasoning, RELATE provides a scalable, semantically accurate framework for converting unstructured biomedical literature into standardized KGs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RELATE: Relation Extraction in Biomedical Abstracts with LLMs and Ontology Constraints" introduces a three-stage pipeline called RELATE for extracting biomedical relations from unstructured text (specifically, abstracts) and mapping them to standardized ontology predicates. The pipeline consists of (1) ontology preprocessing with predicate embeddings, (2) similarity-based retrieval enhanced with SapBERT, and (3) LLM-based reranking with explicit negation handling. The authors evaluate RELATE on the ChemProt benchmark and a set of HEAL Project abstracts, demonstrating its ability to achieve high accuracy and effectively reject irrelevant associations and identify negated assertions. The authors argue that RELATE addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based RE methods by ensuring standardization and alignment with ontologies, which is crucial for KG integration.  The system aims to convert unstructured biomedical literature into standardized Knowledge Graphs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its systematic approach to mapping LLM-extracted relations to established ontologies. While individual components (LLMs, embeddings, ontologies) have been used in biomedical RE before, RELATE's specific combination and orchestration of these components is a key contribution. The generation and use of *negative descriptors* for predicates is an innovative technique that enhances the model's ability to distinguish between valid and invalid predicate mappings, improving KG quality by rejecting wrong or irrelevant assertions. The hybrid retrieval leveraging SapBERT to enhance relation semantics is novel and specific to this task and not just a straight forward implementation.

*   **Significance:** RELATE's significance stems from its potential to improve the completeness and accuracy of biomedical knowledge graphs (KGs). KGs are crucial resources for drug discovery, clinical decision support, and other applications, but they are often incomplete. By automatically extracting and standardizing relations from unstructured text, RELATE can help to fill gaps in KGs and improve their usability. The focus on *ontology-constrained* relation extraction directly addresses a significant limitation of existing LLM-based approaches, which often produce inconsistent and non-standardized results. Its ability to effectively filter for negation is also a significant advantage for avoiding misinformation. By releasing the code, the authors facilitate replication and further advancement in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and well-structured:** The paper is clearly written and presents the RELATE pipeline in a logical and easy-to-understand manner.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The authors perform a comprehensive evaluation of RELATE on two independent datasets, including a standard benchmark (ChemProt) and a real-world dataset (HEAL Project abstracts).
    *   **Effective pipeline:** The results demonstrate that RELATE achieves high accuracy and effectively rejects irrelevant associations and identifies negated assertions.
    *   **Combination of techniques:** The paper successfully combines multiple techniques (LLMs, embeddings, ontologies) to address a challenging problem in biomedical RE.
    *   **Focus on standardization:** The paper's focus on standardization is a significant contribution to the field of biomedical KG construction.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** the qualitative analysis from section 5 provides valuable insight in how the system improves relationship extraction.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited ablation studies:** While the authors evaluate the overall performance of RELATE, they could provide more detailed ablation studies to assess the contribution of each component of the pipeline.
    *   **Scalability concerns:**  The paper states that the experiments occurred on two well defined datasets. However, there is little mention of the scalability constraints for this approach.
    *   **Qualitative analysis and Validation**: The integration of SMEs and the qualitative analysis are great for building the project. However, the authors could have used external validation with SMEs.

*   **Potential Influence:** RELATE has the potential to influence the field of biomedical RE by providing a framework for converting unstructured biomedical literature into standardized KGs. The approach could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to improve the completeness and accuracy of biomedical KGs. The emphasis on negation handling and rejection of irrelevant relations can lead to higher quality and more reliable knowledge graphs. The release of code will help facilitate this impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5** because of its novelty and significance within the field. The system addresses a crucial problem in biomedical relation extraction by providing a systematic framework for mapping LLM-extracted relations to established ontologies. The combination of ontology preprocessing, similarity-based retrieval, and LLM-based reranking with explicit negation handling is a novel approach that shows promising results. The use of negative descriptors for predicates is an innovative technique that enhances the model's ability to distinguish between valid and invalid predicate mappings. Furthermore, the code is provided in the paper which enables it to be replicated and improved. RELATE has the potential to improve the completeness and accuracy of biomedical knowledge graphs, which could have a significant impact on drug discovery, clinical decision support, and other applications. The analysis from section 5 further demonstrates a value.

However, there are some weaknesses in the paper. More ablation studies could be performed to assess the contribution of each component of the pipeline. Lastly, more details for the scalability constraint would provide a fuller scope of the results.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### WaveletGaussian: Wavelet-domain Diffusion for Sparse-view 3D Gaussian Object Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19073v1)
- **Authors**: Hung Nguyen, Runfa Li, An Le, Truong Nguyen
- **Abstract**: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a powerful representation for image-based object reconstruction, yet its performance drops sharply in sparse-view settings. Prior works address this limitation by employing diffusion models to repair corrupted renders, subsequently using them as pseudo ground truths for later optimization. While effective, such approaches incur heavy computation from the diffusion fine-tuning and repair steps. We present WaveletGaussian, a framework for more efficient sparse-view 3D Gaussian object reconstruction. Our key idea is to shift diffusion into the wavelet domain: diffusion is applied only to the low-resolution LL subband, while high-frequency subbands are refined with a lightweight network. We further propose an efficient online random masking strategy to curate training pairs for diffusion fine-tuning, replacing the commonly used, but inefficient, leave-one-out strategy. Experiments across two benchmark datasets, Mip-NeRF 360 and OmniObject3D, show WaveletGaussian achieves competitive rendering quality while substantially reducing training time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WaveletGaussian, a framework for 3D Gaussian object reconstruction from sparse views.  The core idea is to perform diffusion-based novel view repair in the wavelet domain instead of the RGB domain. This is achieved by applying diffusion fine-tuning and repair steps specifically on the low-resolution LL subband of the wavelet transform, while a lightweight U-Net handles the high-frequency subbands.  The authors also propose an online random masking strategy to efficiently create training data for the diffusion fine-tuning, replacing the more computationally expensive leave-one-out approach.  Experiments on Mip-NeRF 360 and OmniObject3D datasets demonstrate that WaveletGaussian achieves competitive rendering quality with significantly reduced training time compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of techniques. Applying wavelet transforms to sparse-view 3D Gaussian Splatting and using diffusion models within this domain is a significant departure from existing RGB-based approaches.  The online random masking strategy is also a valuable contribution for efficient training data generation. The frequency separation and handling them separately via U-Net is also a reasonable contribution.
* **Significance:** Addressing the computational cost of diffusion-based sparse-view 3D reconstruction is crucial for the practical application of these techniques.  WaveletGaussian offers a pathway to significantly reduce training time without sacrificing rendering quality, which is important. A method to handle HF and LF band separately is also a clever technique, which further reduces training time.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Reduced Computational Cost:** The primary strength is the significant reduction in training time compared to existing methods, achieved by shifting the diffusion process to the lower-resolution wavelet domain.
    * **Competitive Rendering Quality:** The paper demonstrates that the proposed method maintains competitive rendering quality despite the reduced computational burden.
    * **Efficient Dataset Creation:** The online random masking strategy is a more efficient alternative to the leave-one-out strategy for dataset creation.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its advantages.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on Pre-trained Diffusion Models:** While leveraging a pre-trained diffusion model is common, the performance still relies on the quality and suitability of that pre-trained model.
    * **Limited Dataset Coverage:** While the datasets used are standard benchmarks, testing on a wider range of objects and scenes would strengthen the findings.
    * **U-Net architecture is quite standard:** The use of U-Net is very standard, which has been widely used in the computer vision community.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of sparse-view 3D reconstruction by making diffusion-based methods more practical and accessible due to the reduced training time. It could also inspire further research into wavelet-domain techniques for neural rendering.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field. The combination of wavelet transforms and diffusion models for efficient sparse-view 3D reconstruction is a novel and potentially impactful idea. The reduction in training time is a significant practical advantage. However, the dependency on pre-trained models and the use of the standard U-Net architecture limit the overall impact. While the experimental results are convincing, demonstrating the method's effectiveness across a broader range of datasets would further strengthen the findings.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Code Driven Planning with Domain-Adaptive Critic
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19077v1)
- **Authors**: Zikang Tian, Shaohui Peng, Du Huang, Jiaming Guo, Ruizhi Chen, Rui Zhang, Xishan Zhang, Yuxuan Guo, Zidong Du, Qi Guo, Ling Li, Yewen Pu, Xing Hu, Yunji Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been widely adopted as task planners for AI agents in sequential decision-making problems, leveraging their extensive world knowledge. However, the gap between their general knowledge and environment-specific requirements often leads to inaccurate plans. To address this, existing approaches rely on frequent LLM queries to iteratively refine plans based on immediate environmental feedback, which incurs substantial query costs. However, this refinement is typically guided by short-term environmental feedback, limiting LLMs from developing plans aligned with long-term rewards. We propose Code Driven Planning with Domain-Adaptive Critic (CoPiC). Instead of relying on frequent queries, CoPiC employs LLMs to generate a diverse set of high-level planning programs, which iteratively produce and refine candidate plans. A trained domain-adaptive critic then evaluates these candidates and selects the one most aligned with long-term rewards for execution. Using high-level planning programs as planner and domain-adaptive critic as estimator, CoPiC improves planning while significantly reducing query costs. Results in ALFWorld, NetHack, and StarCraft II Unit Building show that CoPiC outperforms advanced LLM-based baselines, AdaPlanner and Reflexion, achieving an average (1) 23.33% improvement in success rate and (2) 91.27% reduction in query costs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Code Driven Planning with Domain-Adaptive Critic"

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Code Driven Planning with Domain-Adaptive Critic (CoPiC), a new approach to task planning for AI agents in sequential decision-making problems. CoPiC aims to address the limitations of existing LLM-based planners, which often suffer from inaccurate plans due to the gap between general knowledge and environment-specific requirements.  Instead of relying on frequent LLM queries with immediate environmental feedback, CoPiC employs LLMs to generate diverse high-level planning *programs*. These programs iteratively produce and refine candidate plans. A trained, domain-adaptive critic then evaluates these candidates and selects the best one aligned with long-term rewards. The approach reduces query costs and improves planning performance in dynamic environments. The authors demonstrate CoPiC's effectiveness on ALFWorld, NetHack, and StarCraft II unit building tasks, achieving significant improvements in success rate and token cost reduction compared to state-of-the-art baselines like AdaPlanner and Reflexion.  The critic is fine-tuned with reinforcement learning to improve domain adaptation and the paper shows how "program evolution" enhances the solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing ideas (LLMs for planning, code generation, critics) in a novel and potentially useful way. Using *programs* to encode plans provides greater flexibility than fixed actions or pre-defined skills, while the learned critic acts as a domain-specific regularizer, counteracting LLM hallucinations. The mixture-of-experts (MoE) approach with multiple planning programs allows for exploration of a more diverse solution space. However, the individual components are not entirely new; the novelty lies in their *integration* and application to complex planning tasks.
*   **Significance:** The paper's main contribution is addressing the high query cost problem inherent in many LLM-based planning approaches. The significant reduction in token costs reported in the experimental results, without sacrificing performance (and even improving it) is compelling. This makes LLM-based planning more practical, especially for resource-constrained environments or longer-horizon tasks where frequent queries are infeasible. The performance improvements also suggest that incorporating long-term reward signals via the domain-adaptive critic can lead to better planning than approaches focused solely on immediate feedback.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework combining LLMs, program generation, and a domain-adaptive critic.
    *   Strong experimental results across diverse and challenging environments, outperforming strong baselines.
    *   Detailed ablation studies demonstrating the importance of key components (program evolution, critic).
    *   Experiments also test and show how open-source LLMs can be used to support the algorithm.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The approach requires training a domain-specific critic, which might be a significant overhead for some applications. The critic fine-tuning is RL-based and therefore subject to the usual problems of RL stability. The impact of the critic's architecture and training data on overall performance is not investigated in detail.
    *   While "program evolution" is mentioned, the paper could delve deeper into how the planning programs are refined and adapted over time. More detailed examples showcasing the specific types of improvements that occur during evolution would be beneficial. The experiments are relatively narrow, although it is understood that LLM costs make extensive experiments a challenge.
    *   The environments used are still relatively constrained. While ALFWorld, NetHack, and StarCraft II unit building are challenging, scaling CoPiC to more open-ended and unstructured real-world environments will be crucial to assess its true potential.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on LLM-based planning by highlighting the importance of reducing query costs and incorporating long-term reward signals. The idea of using high-level planning programs could also inspire new approaches to task decomposition and hierarchical planning. The framework could be a key step toward more practical LLM-based agents.

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-based planning. The combination of code-driven planning, a domain-adaptive critic, and program evolution provides a compelling solution to the challenges of high query costs and limited long-term planning capabilities. While the approach has some limitations, the strong experimental results and potential impact warrant a positive evaluation.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### World4RL: Diffusion World Models for Policy Refinement with Reinforcement Learning for Robotic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19080v1)
- **Authors**: Zhennan Jiang, Kai Liu, Yuxin Qin, Shuai Tian, Yupeng Zheng, Mingcai Zhou, Chao Yu, Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao
- **Abstract**: Robotic manipulation policies are commonly initialized through imitation learning, but their performance is limited by the scarcity and narrow coverage of expert data. Reinforcement learning can refine polices to alleviate this limitation, yet real-robot training is costly and unsafe, while training in simulators suffers from the sim-to-real gap. Recent advances in generative models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in real-world simulation, with diffusion models in particular excelling at generation. This raises the question of how diffusion model-based world models can be combined to enhance pre-trained policies in robotic manipulation. In this work, we propose World4RL, a framework that employs diffusion-based world models as high-fidelity simulators to refine pre-trained policies entirely in imagined environments for robotic manipulation. Unlike prior works that primarily employ world models for planning, our framework enables direct end-to-end policy optimization. World4RL is designed around two principles: pre-training a diffusion world model that captures diverse dynamics on multi-task datasets and refining policies entirely within a frozen world model to avoid online real-world interactions. We further design a two-hot action encoding scheme tailored for robotic manipulation and adopt diffusion backbones to improve modeling fidelity. Extensive simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that World4RL provides high-fidelity environment modeling and enables consistent policy refinement, yielding significantly higher success rates compared to imitation learning and other baselines. More visualization results are available at https://world4rl.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "World4RL: Diffusion World Models for Policy Refinement with Reinforcement Learning for Robotic Manipulation" introduces a framework that leverages diffusion-based world models to refine robotic manipulation policies. It addresses the limitations of imitation learning and the challenges of real-robot reinforcement learning. World4RL consists of pre-training a diffusion world model on diverse multi-task datasets and then refining policies within this frozen world model, thus avoiding online interactions. The framework uses a two-hot action encoding scheme and diffusion backbones to improve modeling fidelity. Experiments in simulation and real-world settings demonstrate that World4RL improves policy refinement, achieving higher success rates than imitation learning and other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of diffusion models with reinforcement learning for *direct end-to-end policy optimization* in robotic manipulation is a key novelty.  While diffusion models have been used for video prediction and world models exist, this work directly optimizes policies using a diffusion-based simulator.  The tailored two-hot action encoding is also a notable contribution, addressing the specific challenges of continuous action spaces in robotics. The idea of using diffusion model to replace real-world experiments is promising.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in robotics: bridging the gap between simulation and reality, and improving sample efficiency. If the framework allows policies to be trained effectively in simulation and transfer well to real robots, it could significantly reduce the cost and risk associated with robot learning. The success in real-world experiments, although limited in the number of tasks, strengthens the practical relevance of the research. Also, the success rate improvement in simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that World4RL is not just a theoretical improvement, but also a practical one.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing approaches for robotic manipulation.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** World4RL is presented as a systematic and well-structured approach.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of World4RL in both simulation and real-world environments, compared to reasonable baselines. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of design choices.
    *   **Detailed implementation:** The details of World4RL framework are well-described in the paper, which can guide other researchers in the field.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Task Diversity:** While Meta-World is a standard benchmark, the range of tasks used in the real-world experiments is relatively limited. The framework needs to be tested on more complex and diverse robotic manipulation scenarios.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models can be computationally expensive to train and sample from.  The paper does not extensively discuss the computational resources required or compare them to other approaches. This is a significant consideration for real-world deployment.
    *   **Sim-to-Real Gap Mitigation:** The paper shows improvements but doesn't deeply analyze the specific mechanisms by which the diffusion model bridges the sim-to-real gap. A more detailed analysis of the model's ability to capture real-world dynamics and uncertainties would be valuable.
    *   **Dependence on Pre-training:** The framework is heavily reliant on the quality of the pre-trained diffusion model. While the paper describes pre-training, a deeper exploration of factors affecting the pre-training performance and robustness would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** World4RL has the potential to influence the field by providing a more efficient and safer way to train robotic manipulation policies. The use of diffusion models as high-fidelity simulators is a promising direction for future research. However, the computational cost and task diversity limitations must be addressed for wider adoption. Also, the framework could be extended to other robot learning problems beyond manipulation tasks.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a significant contribution by integrating diffusion world models with reinforcement learning for end-to-end policy optimization in robotic manipulation.  The two-hot action encoding scheme and strong experimental results further enhance its value.  However, the limited real-world task diversity, computational cost concerns, and dependence on pre-training quality prevent it from achieving a higher score. A score of 7 reflects a significant and promising contribution with clear potential but also identifiable limitations that need to be addressed in future work.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Citrus-V: Advancing Medical Foundation Models with Unified Medical Image Grounding for Clinical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19090v1)
- **Authors**: Guoxin Wang, Jun Zhao, Xinyi Liu, Yanbo Liu, Xuyang Cao, Chao Li, Zhuoyun Liu, Qintian Sun, Fangru Zhou, Haoqiang Xing, Zhenhong Yang
- **Abstract**: Medical imaging provides critical evidence for clinical diagnosis, treatment planning, and surgical decisions, yet most existing imaging models are narrowly focused and require multiple specialized networks, limiting their generalization. Although large-scale language and multimodal models exhibit strong reasoning and multi-task capabilities, real-world clinical applications demand precise visual grounding, multimodal integration, and chain-of-thought reasoning. We introduce Citrus-V, a multimodal medical foundation model that combines image analysis with textual reasoning. The model integrates detection, segmentation, and multimodal chain-of-thought reasoning, enabling pixel-level lesion localization, structured report generation, and physician-like diagnostic inference in a single framework. We propose a novel multimodal training approach and release a curated open-source data suite covering reasoning, detection, segmentation, and document understanding tasks. Evaluations demonstrate that Citrus-V outperforms existing open-source medical models and expert-level imaging systems across multiple benchmarks, delivering a unified pipeline from visual grounding to clinical reasoning and supporting precise lesion quantification, automated reporting, and reliable second opinions.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces Citrus-V, a novel multimodal medical foundation model that combines image analysis with textual reasoning for clinical applications. It unifies detection, segmentation, and multimodal chain-of-thought reasoning, enabling pixel-level lesion localization, structured report generation, and physician-like diagnostic inference in a single framework. The authors also present a multimodal training approach and release a curated open-source data suite covering reasoning, detection, segmentation, and document understanding tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that Citrus-V outperforms existing open-source medical models and expert-level imaging systems across multiple benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel aspects. The unified model architecture combining detection, segmentation, and chain-of-thought reasoning is a significant step forward. The open-source data suite, including a multimodal chain-of-thought reasoning dataset and a refined detection/segmentation benchmark, is also valuable.  The proposed multimodal training paradigm is another contribution.

**Significance:**  The potential impact of Citrus-V on clinical practice is substantial. It offers a unified pipeline from visual grounding to clinical reasoning, supporting precise lesion quantification, automated reporting, and reliable second opinions. The comprehensive benchmark results convincingly show its superiority over other medical models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Architecture:** The integration of diverse tasks into a single model is a major strength, enabling synergy and reducing the need for multiple specialized networks.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experimental results are thorough and convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of Citrus-V across multiple benchmarks.
*   **Open-Source Resources:**  The release of the curated data suite will significantly benefit the research community.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on real-world clinical applications enhances the significance of the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not discuss the computational cost associated with training and deploying such a large multimodal model. This is a crucial factor for practical adoption.
*   **Detailed Analysis of Ablations:** Although ablations are performed, a more detailed discussion of why particular components contribute to the model's superior performance is lacking.
*   **Generalizability to unseen modalities:** The breadth of modalities covered in the dataset is commendable, however, the paper provides little discussion of the zero-shot or few-shot generalizability of the model to new modalities beyond those it was trained on.

**Potential Influence:** Citrus-V has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a more generalizable and powerful foundation for medical image analysis and clinical reasoning. It could accelerate the development of AI-powered tools for diagnosis, treatment planning, and decision support.  The open-source nature of the project will further contribute to its adoption and impact.

The approach represents a move towards a more holistic AI system that emulates the comprehensive reasoning of a medical expert. However, given the limitations stated above, the paper is not completely groundbreaking, but definitely an important step forward.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Investigating Traffic Accident Detection Using Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19096v1)
- **Authors**: Ilhan Skender, Kailin Tong, Selim Solmaz, Daniel Watzenig
- **Abstract**: Traffic safety remains a critical global concern, with timely and accurate accident detection essential for hazard reduction and rapid emergency response. Infrastructure-based vision sensors offer scalable and efficient solutions for continuous real-time monitoring, facilitating automated detection of acci- dents directly from captured images. This research investigates the zero-shot capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for detecting and describing traffic accidents using images from infrastructure cameras, thus minimizing reliance on extensive labeled datasets. Main contributions include: (1) Evaluation of MLLMs using the simulated DeepAccident dataset from CARLA, explicitly addressing the scarcity of diverse, realistic, infrastructure-based accident data through controlled simulations; (2) Comparative performance analysis between Gemini 1.5 and 2.0, Gemma 3 and Pixtral models in acci- dent identification and descriptive capabilities without prior fine-tuning; and (3) Integration of advanced visual analytics, specifically YOLO for object detection, Deep SORT for multi- object tracking, and Segment Anything (SAM) for instance segmentation, into enhanced prompts to improve model accuracy and explainability. Key numerical results show Pixtral as the top performer with an F1-score of 0.71 and 83% recall, while Gemini models gained precision with enhanced prompts (e.g., Gemini 1.5 rose to 90%) but suffered notable F1 and recall losses. Gemma 3 offered the most balanced performance with minimal metric fluctuation. These findings demonstrate the substantial potential of integrating MLLMs with advanced visual analytics techniques, enhancing their applicability in real-world automated traffic monitoring systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the zero-shot capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for detecting and describing traffic accidents using infrastructure camera images.  It evaluates Gemini 1.5, Gemini 2.0, Gemma 3, and Pixtral Large models on the DeepAccident dataset (a simulated accident dataset from CARLA). The research explores the impact of enhancing prompts with outputs from computer vision techniques like YOLO (object detection), Deep SORT (multi-object tracking), and Segment Anything (SAM) on model performance.  The key findings indicate that Pixtral Large achieved the best accident detection performance (highest F1-score and recall), while Gemini models gained precision with enhanced prompts but suffered losses in F1 and recall. Gemma 3 demonstrated the most balanced performance. The study also found that enhanced prompts, despite intended improvements, did not consistently improve MLLM accident detection and could even introduce noise.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Relevance:** Traffic accident detection is a crucial problem, and leveraging infrastructure cameras and AI offers a scalable solution.
    *   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** The use of the DeepAccident dataset (simulated) effectively tackles the lack of diverse, realistic, infrastructure-based accident data, which is a common bottleneck in this area. This is a *major* practical contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper performs a comparative analysis of multiple MLLMs (Gemini, Gemma, Pixtral), which is valuable for understanding their relative strengths and weaknesses in this specific application domain.
    *   **Experimentation with Enhanced Prompts:**  Investigating the influence of integrating visual analytics techniques (YOLO, Deep SORT, SAM) into prompts is a significant contribution.  The *negative* result (enhanced prompts didn't always improve performance) is itself a valuable finding, showing the limitations of simple integration and highlighting the need for more sophisticated prompt engineering or model fine-tuning.
    *   **Justification for Prompting Strategies:** The paper details the architecture and provides the prompts used and justifies their design.
    *   **Consideration of Evaluation Metrics:** The paper recognizes the limitations of standard metrics like BLEU and ROUGE for this task and incorporates semantic similarity measures (Word2Vec, Sentence Transformers), demonstrating a thoughtful approach to evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulated Data:** The reliance on the DeepAccident dataset introduces a "sim-to-real" gap. While the dataset helps overcome data scarcity, performance in real-world conditions may differ significantly. This is a *significant* limitation.
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:** There is no validation in real-world environments or demonstrations of real-world applications that could result in traffic safety improvements.
    *   **Lack of Fine-Tuning:** The models are used in a zero-shot setting, which means they were not specifically trained for traffic accident detection. Fine-tuning the MLLMs on traffic accident data could potentially lead to significantly better performance.
    *   **Explanation of Enhanced Prompt Failure:** While the paper suggests that noise introduced by the YOLO/SAM/DeepSORT process might be the cause of a drop in performance in MLLMs, this is a hypothesis. A more in-depth error analysis could shed more light on *why* the enhanced prompts failed to deliver better results and would improve the scientific rigor. The exact nature of the error could then be addressed in future prompts.
    *   **Modest Novelty in Detection Pipeline:** Using YOLO, DeepSORT and SAM are fairly standard computer vision techniques. The novelty lies in *how* these are combined with MLLMs. There isn't much novelty in the detection pipeline itself.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of work exploring the use of MLLMs in ITS applications, specifically for traffic accident detection. While similar studies exist, this paper offers a comprehensive evaluation of multiple MLLMs and explores the impact of visually enhanced prompts, which distinguishes it from earlier work.
    *   The negative results relating to the effect of visual prompt enhancements are arguably as valuable as positive ones. It provides insight into the limitations of the MLLMs.
    *   The paper is well written and clear, and it provides an adequate number of references to related work.
    *   The most significant aspect of the study is its approach to the data scarcity problem by utilizing a simulated dataset and attempting to augment the base data to create a broader feature set.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by systematically evaluating the zero-shot capabilities of several MLLMs for traffic accident detection. The findings highlight the potential and limitations of using these models in this context and the challenges associated with integrating visual analytics techniques into prompts. However, the reliance on simulated data and lack of real-world validation limits the practical impact. The use of conventional CV techniques in the detection pipeline means the work primarily emphasizes the MLLM capabilities and their integration, rather than novel CV algorithms.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's solid contributions and clear presentation, while acknowledging its limitations. The thorough evaluation of MLLMs and the exploration of visual analytics integration into prompts are valuable, and the challenges are thoroughly addressed. The study addresses a relevant problem using suitable methodologies and explores the benefits and drawbacks of data augmentation using a simulator. The *sim-to-real* gap and lack of real-world validation limits the paper's practical impact, as the performance may not be replicated in real-world situations. The findings, while interesting, are not *exceptionally* novel or groundbreaking. More emphasis on error analysis would have been valuable and would justify a higher score. The reliance on known visual detection techniques makes it difficult to argue the work is strongly innovative.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Algorithms for Adversarially Robust Deep Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19100v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Robey
- **Abstract**: Given the widespread use of deep learning models in safety-critical applications, ensuring that the decisions of such models are robust against adversarial exploitation is of fundamental importance. In this thesis, we discuss recent progress toward designing algorithms that exhibit desirable robustness properties. First, we discuss the problem of adversarial examples in computer vision, for which we introduce new technical results, training paradigms, and certification algorithms. Next, we consider the problem of domain generalization, wherein the task is to train neural networks to generalize from a family of training distributions to unseen test distributions. We present new algorithms that achieve state-of-the-art generalization in medical imaging, molecular identification, and image classification. Finally, we study the setting of jailbreaking large language models (LLMs), wherein an adversarial user attempts to design prompts that elicit objectionable content from an LLM. We propose new attacks and defenses, which represent the frontier of progress toward designing robust language-based agents.
- **Summary**: ### Summary: The paper titled "Algorithms for Adversarially Robust Deep Learning" addresses the increasing necessity for deep learning models, especially in safety-critical applications, to withstand adversarial attacks. The author transitions through several key areas:  1. **Adversarial Examples in Computer Vision**: Here, new training methods, certification algorithms, and theoretical advancements are introduced to enhance model robustness against manipulative inputs.     2. **Domain Generalization**: The research presents innovative algorithms that improve the generalization capabilities of neural networks across unseen distributions. These advancements show marked improvements in diverse fields like medical imaging and molecular identification.     3. **Jailbreaking Large Language Models (LLMs)**: A focus on the vulnerabilities of LLMs is discussed, where strategies for both offensive (adversarial prompting) and defensive measures are proposed to mitigate the risk of exposing objectionable content. ### Critical Evaluation: The novelty of this paper lies in its comprehensive approach to various aspects of adversarial robustness across different domains. By introducing new algorithms and protocols, it attempts to bridge gaps found in existing methodologies.  **Strengths**: - **Depth and Range of Topics**: Covering multiple facets of adversarial robustness enriches the discourse. This interdisciplinary approach can inspire further research. - **Practical Applications**: Insights into medical imaging and LLMs articulate real-world implications, enhancing the paper's relevance beyond theoretical discussions. - **Innovative Solutions**: The proposed algorithms and methods showcase promising results, indicating a strong potential for practical adoption. **Weaknesses**: - **Lack of Comprehensive Evaluation**: While the paper proposes novel techniques, it does not provide exhaustive empirical comparisons with the existing state-of-the-art methods, which limits the reader’s ability to assess effectiveness fully. - **Granularity of Implementation**: The algorithms could benefit from more detailed implementation frameworks or examples, which would make them more accessible for practitioners. Overall, the paper demonstrates significant contributions to adversarial robustness in deep learning, laying groundwork for future studies. However, the limited assessment of its methods against leading alternatives detracts slightly from its impact. **Score: 8**  This score reflects its combination of innovative approaches and significant practical applications, while also noting the areas for improvement, especially in empirical validation and implementation detail.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### DRO-REBEL: Distributionally Robust Relative-Reward Regression for Fast and Efficient LLM Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19104v1)
- **Authors**: Sharan Sahu, Martin T. Wells
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning with human feedback (RLHF) has become crucial for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human intent. However, existing offline RLHF approaches suffer from overoptimization, where models overfit to reward misspecification and drift from preferred behaviors observed during training. We introduce DRO-REBEL, a unified family of robust REBEL updates with type-$p$ Wasserstein, KL, and $\chi^2$ ambiguity sets. Using Fenchel duality, each update reduces to a simple relative-reward regression, preserving scalability and avoiding PPO-style clipping or auxiliary value networks. Under standard linear-reward and log-linear policy classes with a data-coverage condition, we establish $O(n^{-1/4})$ estimation bounds with tighter constants than prior DRO-DPO approaches, and recover the minimax-optimal $O(n^{-1/2})$ rate via a localized Rademacher complexity analysis. The same analysis closes the gap for Wasserstein-DPO and KL-DPO, showing both also attain optimal parametric rates. We derive practical SGD algorithms for all three divergences: gradient regularization (Wasserstein), importance weighting (KL), and a fast 1-D dual solve ($\chi^2$). Experiments on Emotion Alignment, the large-scale ArmoRM multi-objective benchmark, and HH-Alignment demonstrate strong worst-case robustness across unseen preference mixtures, model sizes, and data scales, with $\chi^2$-REBEL showing consistently strong empirical performance. A controlled radius--coverage study validates a no-free-lunch trade-off: radii shrinking faster than empirical divergence concentration rates achieve minimax-optimal parametric rates but forfeit coverage, while coverage-guaranteeing radii incur $O(n^{-1/4})$ rates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DRO-REBEL: Distributionally Robust Relative-Reward Regression for Fast and Efficient LLM Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DRO-REBEL, a novel framework for distributionally robust reinforcement learning with human feedback (RLHF) for aligning large language models (LLMs).  It builds upon the REBEL algorithm, which uses relative reward regression, and incorporates distributional robustness to address preference shifts. DRO-REBEL offers a unified approach using Wasserstein, KL divergence, and Chi-squared ambiguity sets. The key benefits highlighted are improved sample efficiency, stability, and generalization compared to existing methods like DPO, WDPO, and PPO.  The authors provide theoretical guarantees, including sharper slow-rate bounds and minimax-optimal fast rates under certain assumptions.  They also explore the trade-off between robustness radius and estimation error. Experiments across several benchmarks (Emotion Alignment, ArmoRM, HH-RLHF) demonstrate the empirical advantages of DRO-REBEL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the principled integration of distributional robustness within the REBEL framework and in deriving improved theoretical guarantees compared to existing DRO-based RLHF methods.  While DRO and REBEL have been explored independently, their combination and the resulting theoretical analysis represent a significant contribution. The detailed analysis of the radius-coverage trade-off is also novel.
*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM alignment: the sensitivity to preference shifts. The proposed framework offers a potential pathway towards more reliable and generalizable LLMs. The theoretical results, including the bridging of the slow/fast rate gap for DRO-DPO type methods, are valuable for understanding the limitations and capabilities of DRO in this context. The practical scalability of DRO-REBEL, demonstrated through experiments with relatively large models, further increases its significance.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper presents rigorous theoretical analysis, providing sample complexity bounds and analyzing the trade-offs involved.
    *   **Unified Framework:** The framework generalizes across different ambiguity sets, making it versatile and adaptable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Experiments on multiple benchmarks, including large-scale datasets, support the practical effectiveness of DRO-REBEL.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is generally well-written and structured.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical results rely on specific assumptions, such as linear reward classes and log-linear policy classes with data-coverage conditions.  The extent to which these assumptions hold in real-world LLM alignment scenarios needs further investigation. The implications for the conclusions if some of the "known" parts of the problem (feature mapping, reward structure) are unknown or inaccurate are not fully treated.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the authors propose tractable algorithms for approximating the robust updates, the computational overhead of DRO compared to standard REBEL/DPO is not explicitly quantified or compared.  Furthermore, the algorithms are themselves "approximate tractable algorithms," rather than a provably tractable algorithm.

    *   **Limited Real-World Scenarios:** The HH-RLHF experiment, while valuable, is still based on existing datasets and a simulated reward model. Direct comparisons with human evaluators in more realistic alignment tasks would further strengthen the empirical claims.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RLHF by providing a more theoretically grounded and practically scalable approach to distributional robustness. It could inspire new algorithms and techniques for LLM alignment, particularly those that address the challenges of preference shifts and data coverage. The insights into the robustness radius and estimation error trade-off could also guide practitioners in selecting appropriate settings for DRO-based methods.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM alignment with a novel framework, improved theoretical guarantees, and empirical validation.  However, the reliance on assumptions and limited real-world evaluations prevent a higher score.  The paper represents a significant advance over prior DRO-DPO methods due to its tighter rates and empirical results, but more detailed treatment of some of the edge cases may have elevated the score to an 8 or beyond. Despite these weaknesses, its potential influence on future research is considerable.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Practical Multi-label Causal Discovery in High-Dimensional Event Sequences via One-Shot Graph Aggregation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19112v1)
- **Authors**: Hugo Math, Rainer Lienhart
- **Abstract**: Understanding causality in event sequences where outcome labels such as diseases or system failures arise from preceding events like symptoms or error codes is critical. Yet remains an unsolved challenge across domains like healthcare or vehicle diagnostics. We introduce CARGO, a scalable multi-label causal discovery method for sparse, high-dimensional event sequences comprising of thousands of unique event types. Using two pretrained causal Transformers as domain-specific foundation models for event sequences. CARGO infers in parallel, per sequence one-shot causal graphs and aggregates them using an adaptive frequency fusion to reconstruct the global Markov boundaries of labels. This two-stage approach enables efficient probabilistic reasoning at scale while bypassing the intractable cost of full-dataset conditional independence testing. Our results on a challenging real-world automotive fault prediction dataset with over 29,100 unique event types and 474 imbalanced labels demonstrate CARGO's ability to perform structured reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CARGO (Causal Aggregation via Regressive Graph Operations), a scalable method for multi-label causal discovery in high-dimensional event sequences. CARGO addresses the challenge of identifying causal relationships in complex systems where there are thousands of potential event types and imbalanced outcome labels. The method operates in two stages: first, it extracts one-shot causal graphs for each sequence using pretrained causal Transformers as domain-specific foundation models. Second, it aggregates these individual graphs via an adaptive frequency fusion technique to reconstruct the global Markov boundaries of the labels.  The method is demonstrated on a real-world automotive fault prediction dataset, showing it can perform structured reasoning where traditional causal discovery baselines are intractable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the combination of one-shot graph extraction with an adaptive graph aggregation technique specifically tailored for high-dimensional event sequences with multi-label outputs. The reinterpretation of multi-label causal discovery as Bayesian model averaging, where each sequence represents a sample from a local causal model, is a valuable conceptual framing. The use of pretrained Transformers as domain-specific causal learners is a practical aspect, though it relies on the quality of these foundation models. Prior work has explored graph aggregation, but CARGO’s adaptive thresholding strategy, designed to address the long-tail distribution of outcome labels, is a key differentiator. The application of this framework to event sequence data is relatively new, particularly with this scale.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in causal discovery: scalability to high-dimensional, sparse data. The automotive fault prediction application demonstrates the practical relevance of the method, especially in domains where understanding causal relationships is crucial for diagnostics and decision-making. The scalability achieved by CARGO, bypassing full-dataset conditional independence testing, makes it a potentially valuable tool for real-world applications.
