A key strength is addressing practical limitations of frequency approaches, particularly under class imbalance. Also, this research tackles a previously open question in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:** The two-stage approach significantly reduces computational complexity, making it tractable for datasets with thousands of event types.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** Theorem 1 provides theoretical justification for the one-shot discovery phase under standard assumptions.
    *   **Adaptive Thresholding:**  The ability to handle long-tail distributions of outcome labels enhances the method's robustness.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Results on a real-world dataset demonstrate practical superiority over established baselines. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the impact of different design choices.
    *   Clear connection to the existing literature on Bayesian Model Averaging and Structure Learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Transformer Quality:** The one-shot graph extraction relies heavily on the performance of the pretrained causal Transformers (the "Oracle Models" assumption). While the paper includes an ablation study, the dependence on these foundation models is a potential bottleneck.
    *   **Assumptions:** While standard, the assumptions of causal sufficiency, bounded lagged effects, and temporal precedence may limit the applicability of the method in certain domains. Relaxing these assumptions would broaden the impact of the work.
    *   **Limited Comparisons:** The comparisons are made against local structure learning algorithms. Comparing against other model averaging techniques and scalable causal discovery methods (if possible) would further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Real Ground Truth:** Lacking real ground truth about the markov boundaries limit the significance that is not just showing better performance than other methods on a task.

*   **Potential Influence:** CARGO could inspire further research in scalable causal discovery for complex event sequences. The framework’s modular design (e.g., using Transformers) allows for potential extensions and adaptations to different domains. The open-sourcing of the code and dataset promotes reproducibility and facilitates further development by the community.

Overall, CARGO makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a practical challenge with a theoretically sound and empirically validated method. While the reliance on foundation models and some limiting assumptions are important considerations, the approach demonstrates a viable path toward scalable causal discovery in complex systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant approach to multi-label causal discovery in high-dimensional event sequences. The adaptive thresholding aggregation and two-stage approach are key strengths. The work fills a gap in the area, demonstrating practicality on a real-world dataset. However, the reliance on the quality of Transformer foundation models and the assumption of temporal precedence limit the generality. Therefore, the score reflects that it is a solid contribution and provides an important step forward.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-based Vulnerability Discovery through the Lens of Code Metrics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19117v1)
- **Authors**: Felix Weissberg, Lukas Pirch, Erik Imgrund, Jonas Möller, Thorsten Eisenhofer, Konrad Rieck
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel in many tasks of software engineering, yet progress in leveraging them for vulnerability discovery has stalled in recent years. To understand this phenomenon, we investigate LLMs through the lens of classic code metrics. Surprisingly, we find that a classifier trained solely on these metrics performs on par with state-of-the-art LLMs for vulnerability discovery. A root-cause analysis reveals a strong correlation and a causal effect between LLMs and code metrics: When the value of a metric is changed, LLM predictions tend to shift by a corresponding magnitude. This dependency suggests that LLMs operate at a similarly shallow level as code metrics, limiting their ability to grasp complex patterns and fully realize their potential in vulnerability discovery. Based on these findings, we derive recommendations on how research should more effectively address this challenge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates why Large Language Models (LLMs) haven't significantly outperformed simpler techniques in vulnerability discovery. It contrasts LLMs with classic code metrics, finding that a classifier trained *solely* on these metrics achieves performance on par with state-of-the-art LLMs. Through root-cause analysis, the authors demonstrate a strong correlation and even a causal dependency between LLM predictions and code metrics. This suggests that LLMs operate at a relatively shallow level, relying on basic statistical properties of the code similar to code metrics, rather than truly grasping complex vulnerability patterns.  Based on these findings, the paper recommends using code metrics as a baseline for LLM vulnerability detection research, emphasizing the importance of assessing model complexity relative to detection results, and developing models that move beyond surface-level code features.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing an Important Question:** The paper tackles a critical and timely question: why haven't LLMs revolutionized vulnerability discovery as expected? It's a valuable contribution to the field because it questions established assumptions and encourages a re-evaluation of current research directions.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a sound methodology, including a comparative evaluation of several state-of-the-art LLMs, a detailed analysis of code metrics, and experiments designed to establish causal dependencies. The use of PrimeVul dataset is appropriate.
*   **Unexpected Findings:** The finding that simple code metrics can match the performance of complex LLMs is surprising and challenges the prevailing paradigm in vulnerability discovery research.
*   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper provides concrete and actionable recommendations for the research community, including using code metrics as baselines, balancing model complexity with detection results, and developing models that move beyond surface-level code features.
*   **Unified Code Metric Framework:** The creation of a generalized family of syntactic code metrics(SCM) is a helpful contribution, unifying previous works and providing a basis for further analysis.
*   **Root-cause Analysis:** The analysis to explore the correlation between code metrics and LLMs is impressive.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Code Metrics:** The paper focuses on *syntactic* code metrics. While this provides a clear and manageable scope, it doesn't consider other types of code metrics (e.g., coupling and cohesion) or dynamic metrics (e.g., test coverage). It is still important to note that different code metrics are used, and these code metrics are generalized in this paper. There could be a case that considering additional code metrics will improve the performance.
*   **Simplistic LLM Understanding:** The paper's conclusion that LLMs operate at a "shallow level" is somewhat simplistic. While the evidence supports the claim that LLMs rely on information similar to code metrics, it's possible that they also utilize more subtle patterns that the experiments didn't capture. LLMs are complex models, and concluding that the results are due to the shallowness of them is a little too bold.
*   **Causality Interpretation:** While the paper attempts to establish a causal relationship, the interventional approach (modifying code using patches) has limitations. These changes may alter multiple metrics simultaneously. It is also important to note that the changes will not be due to random causes. Thus the result is not perfect.
*   **Scalability and Generality:** The empirical evaluation relies on function-level analysis of C/C++ code. Whether these findings generalize to other languages or to vulnerability discovery at different scales (e.g., entire programs or systems) remains an open question.
*   **The root-cause analysis is also imperfect**. The analysis is not complete as the authors did not explore other possibilities.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its *comparative* approach and its focus on questioning the *assumptions* behind the use of LLMs in vulnerability discovery.  It's significant because it encourages a more critical and nuanced evaluation of the benefits and limitations of these models, potentially shifting the direction of future research toward more effective and efficient techniques.  The causal dependency experiment is particularly insightful and highlights potential areas for future work.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

*   The paper addresses a relevant question with a sound methodology and provides unexpected results that challenge existing research direction.
*   The actionable recommendations and the unified code metric are a good plus for the paper.
*   The limited scope of the code metrics, simplistic view on LLM decision making process, potential for over-interpretation of causality, and the limitation of generality are the reason for the reduction.
*   Overall, the paper’s impact is high because it can promote better research practice and a re-evaluation of research priorities, but the scope can be improved.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Analysis on distribution and clustering of weight
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19122v1)
- **Authors**: Chunming Ye, Wenquan Tian, Yalan Gao, Songzhou Li
- **Abstract**: The study on architecture and parameter characteristics remains the hot topic in the research of large language models. In this paper we concern with the characteristics of weight which are used to analyze the correlations and differences between models. Two kinds of vectors-standard deviation vector and clustering vector-are proposed to describe features of models. In the first case, the weights are assumed to follow normal distribution. The standard deviation values of projection matrices are normalized to form Standard-Deviation Vector, representing the distribution characteristics of models. In the second case, the singular values from each weight projection matrix are extracted and grouped by K-Means algorithm. The grouped data with the same type matrix are combined as Clustering Vector to represent the correlation characteristics of models' weights. The study reveals that these two vectors can effectively distinguish between different models and clearly show the similarities among models of the same family. Moreover, after conducting LoRA fine-tuning with different datasets and models, it is found that the distribution of weights represented by standard deviation vector is directly influenced by the dataset, but the correlations between different weights represented by clustering vector remain unaffected and maintain a high consistency with the pre-trained model.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the weight characteristics of large language models (LLMs) by proposing two types of vectors to analyze the correlations and differences between models: a Standard-Deviation Vector and a Clustering Vector. The Standard-Deviation Vector represents the distribution characteristics of the model's weights, assuming they follow a normal distribution. The Clustering Vector, on the other hand, represents the correlation characteristics of the model's weights based on singular values extracted from weight projection matrices and grouped using K-Means clustering.  The study finds that these vectors can distinguish between different models and show similarities within the same model family.  The paper also explores the effect of LoRA fine-tuning on these vectors, finding that the Standard-Deviation Vector is influenced by the training dataset, while the Clustering Vector largely retains the characteristics of the pre-trained model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using Standard Deviation and Clustering Vectors to analyze LLM weights offers some degree of novelty. While individual techniques like K-means clustering and analyzing weight distributions are not new in themselves, their combined application *specifically* to categorize and differentiate between LLM *architectures and the effects of fine-tuning* gives this work a slight edge. The paper also highlights that the Standard Deviation Vector can quickly reshape to mirror training dataset while clustering vector remain frozen to pre-training.
* **Significance:** The significance of the paper hinges on its potential to contribute to a better understanding of LLM behavior and model improvement. The ability to distinguish between models, identify similarities, and analyze the impact of fine-tuning offers potential benefits for model selection, transfer learning, and optimization. However, there is a significant lack of demonstration that using these feature vectors can help achieve better model performance. It's more of an analytical tool than a prescriptive one currently.  There is no experimental validation to show that these vectors can be used to predict or improve model performance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is reasonably well-written and explains the methodology clearly.
*   **Systematic Analysis:** The experiments are systematically designed to explore different models and datasets.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The observation that the Standard-Deviation Vector is dataset-driven while the Clustering Vector is model-driven is an interesting one that warrants further investigation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Validation:** The most significant weakness is the lack of validation. The paper shows that the vectors *can* distinguish between models, but it does not demonstrate how this information can be *used* to improve or optimize models.  The practical implications are unclear. There isn't any downstream task to evaluate the usefulness of the findings.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** The paper's approach builds on existing techniques. While the combination is novel, the individual components are well-established.
*   **Descriptive vs. Prescriptive:** The paper primarily provides a descriptive analysis of weight characteristics, rather than offering a prescriptive method for model improvement or optimization.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the balance of novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I would assign this paper a score of **6**.

*   **Lower than 7:** The lack of practical validation and incremental nature of the work prevent a higher score. Simply differentiating between models without demonstrating a clear application of this knowledge limits its impact.
*   **Higher than 5:** The paper is technically sound, presents interesting findings, and offers a novel analytical approach to LLM weights. The combined use of SD and Clustering vectors shows promise for analytical application.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Context-Aware Hierarchical Taxonomy Generation for Scientific Papers via LLM-Guided Multi-Aspect Clustering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19125v1)
- **Authors**: Kun Zhu, Lizi Liao, Yuxuan Gu, Lei Huang, Xiaocheng Feng, Bing Qin
- **Abstract**: The rapid growth of scientific literature demands efficient methods to organize and synthesize research findings. Existing taxonomy construction methods, leveraging unsupervised clustering or direct prompting of large language models (LLMs), often lack coherence and granularity. We propose a novel context-aware hierarchical taxonomy generation framework that integrates LLM-guided multi-aspect encoding with dynamic clustering. Our method leverages LLMs to identify key aspects of each paper (e.g., methodology, dataset, evaluation) and generates aspect-specific paper summaries, which are then encoded and clustered along each aspect to form a coherent hierarchy. In addition, we introduce a new evaluation benchmark of 156 expert-crafted taxonomies encompassing 11.6k papers, providing the first naturally annotated dataset for this task. Experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms prior approaches, achieving state-of-the-art performance in taxonomy coherence, granularity, and interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Context-Aware Hierarchical Taxonomy Generation for Scientific Papers via LLM-Guided Multi-Aspect Clustering":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel framework for generating hierarchical taxonomies of scientific papers. The approach combines LLM-guided multi-aspect encoding and dynamic clustering to organize literature in a more coherent and granular fashion.  The LLM is used to identify key aspects of each paper (e.g., methodology, dataset) and generate aspect-specific summaries.  These summaries are then encoded and clustered independently along each aspect.  A dynamic clustering algorithm adaptively searches for optimal groupings within each aspect. The authors also introduce a new benchmark dataset, TaxoBench-CS, consisting of 156 expert-crafted taxonomies covering 11.6k computer science papers.  Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing approaches in taxonomy coherence, granularity, and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:** The combination of LLM-guided multi-aspect encoding and dynamic clustering is a genuinely novel and sensible approach to taxonomy generation. By considering different facets of a paper and clustering them separately, the framework has a better chance of capturing the nuances of scientific research compared to methods that rely on single embeddings or direct LLM prompting.
    *   **High-Quality Benchmark:** The creation of TaxoBench-CS is a significant contribution. The lack of high-quality, naturally-annotated datasets has been a bottleneck in the field. This benchmark provides a valuable resource for future research. The fact that it uses human expert curated taxonomies from existing scientific surveys adds considerable value.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing experimental results demonstrating the superiority of the proposed method over existing baselines.  Both automatic and human evaluations indicate improvements in coherence, granularity, and interpretability.  The inclusion of LLMs in the human evaluation is interesting.
    *   **Well-structured and written:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the proposed method and the experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The computational overhead of the multi-aspect encoding and dynamic clustering may limit scalability to extremely large corpora, as acknowledged by the authors. This could become a critical issue for practical applications.
    *   **LLM Dependency:** The performance of the framework relies heavily on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. Improvements in LLM technology will likely benefit this method, but any limitations in the LLMs also translate to limitations in taxonomy quality. The prompt engineering aspect might also need further exploration to make the approach more robust to various LLM architectures.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** The benchmark dataset and experiments are focused on computer science literature. While the authors mention extending the framework to cross-domain settings as future work, it is not clear how well the current approach will generalize to other scientific fields without significant modifications.
    *   **Limited Robustness Analysis:** While the paper shows great promise with a strong evaluation. However, the current result mainly covers the survey paper data. More robustness tests could be performed to reveal the potential limitations of this proposed method.
    *   **Practical Retrieval Integration:** The framework assumes access to relevant documents, but it would be more valuable to demonstrate its real-world applicability by integrating it with existing retrieval systems.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty of this paper lies in the integration of LLM's capabilities with a hierarchical clustering method to organize research papers. It is a step forward as the method offers high taxonomy coherence, granularity, and interpretability in a scalable way. The TaxoBench-CS dataset is also significant. It addresses a gap in the field and provides a valuable resource for researchers working on automated taxonomy generation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of automated taxonomy generation. The proposed method is both novel and effective, and the introduction of the TaxoBench-CS dataset is a valuable resource. However, the limitations regarding computational cost, LLM dependency, and domain specificity prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PipelineRL: Faster On-policy Reinforcement Learning for Long Sequence Generatio
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19128v1)
- **Authors**: Alexandre Piché, Ehsan Kamaloo, Rafael Pardinas, Dzmitry Bahdanau
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) is increasingly utilized to enhance the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, effectively scaling these RL methods presents significant challenges, primarily due to the difficulty in maintaining high AI accelerator utilization without generating stale, off-policy data that harms common RL algorithms. This paper introduces PipelineRL, an approach designed to achieve a superior trade-off between hardware efficiency and data on-policyness for LLM training. PipelineRL employs concurrent asynchronous data generation and model training, distinguished by the novel in-flight weight updates. This mechanism allows the LLM generation engine to receive updated model weights with minimal interruption during the generation of token sequences, thereby maximizing both the accelerator utilization and the freshness of training data. Experiments conducted on long-form reasoning tasks using 128 H100 GPUs demonstrate that PipelineRL achieves approximately $\sim 2x$ faster learning compared to conventional RL baselines while maintaining highly on-policy training data. A scalable and modular open-source implementation of PipelineRL is also released as a key contribution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "PipelineRL: Faster On-policy Reinforcement Learning for Long Sequence Generation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PipelineRL, a reinforcement learning (RL) approach specifically designed to improve the efficiency of training Large Language Models (LLMs) for long sequence generation tasks. The key idea is to overlap data generation and model training concurrently using "in-flight weight updates." This allows the LLM to receive updated model weights with minimal interruption during sequence generation, maximizing AI accelerator utilization and ensuring that the training data remains largely on-policy. Experiments on long-form reasoning tasks using a substantial number of GPUs (128 H100s) demonstrate a roughly 2x speedup in learning compared to conventional RL baselines while maintaining high on-policy data quality.  The authors also release a scalable open-source implementation of PipelineRL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of PipelineRL is a clever adaptation of asynchronous RL to the specific challenges of LLM training for long sequences. The concept of "in-flight weight updates" is a clear innovation. The concurrent generation and training are not completely new, but the in-flight update mechanism applied in the context of long sequence LLM training adds a valuable contribution. However, previous work in asynchronous RL may decrease this specific point's novelty.

*   **Significance:** Scaling RL for LLMs is a critical problem. The ability to effectively use large amounts of compute power while maintaining on-policy data is vital for progress. PipelineRL addresses this challenge directly. The experimental results on substantial hardware (128 H100 GPUs) provide strong evidence for the practical benefits of the approach, and the open-source release facilitates wider adoption and further research. The ~2x speedup claimed is significant from a practical perspective. However, the significance is somewhat lessened by the fact that the method relies heavily on highly specialized hardware and is thus less accessible for research labs with smaller budgets. Furthermore, the experimental evaluation, while promising, could be strengthened by a direct comparison to veRL(2024), a similar reinforcement learning method that aims to maximize computational efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Innovative "in-flight weight updates" to manage concurrency and on-policy data.
    *   Strong empirical results on relevant tasks using substantial hardware resources.
    *   Open-source release promotes reproducibility and adoption.
    *   The inclusion of a theoretical analysis of the PipelineRL setup through the derivation of the speedup helps to strengthen the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Dependence on specialized hardware reduces accessibility for some researchers.
    *   The performance gain over veRL (2024), which also claims to achieve computational efficiency through asynchronous methods is unaddressed.
    *   The paper does not discuss potential downsides, risks or broader implications of the method.
    *   The token sampling distribution (as shown in Figure 7) appears more similar to that of the fully-off-policy tokens than the on-policy tokens, thus calling into question the effectiveness of in-flight weight updates.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient RL training methods for LLMs. The open-source implementation will encourage further research and exploration of asynchronous RL approaches in this domain.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important challenge in the field of LLMs and presents a novel and technically sound approach that is supported by strong empirical results. However, the dependence on substantial hardware resources limits broader accessibility, and performance relative to existing asynchronous RL techniques such as veRL could be more addressed. Some theoretical claims, as well as the on-policyness of the method should also be more strongly validated with thorough quantitative and qualitative analyses.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Soundness and Consistency of LLM Agents for Executing Test Cases Written in Natural Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19136v1)
- **Authors**: Sébastien Salva, Redha Taguelmimt
- **Abstract**: The use of natural language (NL) test cases for validating graphical user interface (GUI) applications is emerging as a promising direction to manually written executable test scripts, which are costly to develop and difficult to maintain. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have opened the possibility of the direct execution of NL test cases by LLM agents. This paper investigates this direction, focusing on the impact on NL test case unsoundness and on test case execution consistency. NL test cases are inherently unsound, as they may yield false failures due to ambiguous instructions or unpredictable agent behaviour. Furthermore, repeated executions of the same NL test case may lead to inconsistent outcomes, undermining test reliability. To address these challenges, we propose an algorithm for executing NL test cases with guardrail mechanisms and specialised agents that dynamically verify the correct execution of each test step. We introduce measures to evaluate the capabilities of LLMs in test execution and one measure to quantify execution consistency. We propose a definition of weak unsoundness to characterise contexts in which NL test case execution remains acceptable, with respect to the industrial quality levels Six Sigma. Our experimental evaluation with eight publicly available LLMs, ranging from 3B to 70B parameters, demonstrates both the potential and current limitations of current LLM agents for GUI testing. Our experiments show that Meta Llama 3.1 70B demonstrates acceptable capabilities in NL test case execution with high execution consistency (above the level 3-sigma). We provide prototype tools, test suites, and results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of LLM agents for executing natural language (NL) test cases to validate GUI applications. It addresses the inherent unsoundness of NL test cases (false failures) and the inconsistency of execution (varying outcomes from the same test case). The authors propose an algorithm that executes NL test cases with guardrail mechanisms and specialized agents that dynamically verify each test step's correct execution. The paper introduces measures to evaluate LLM capabilities and quantifies execution consistency. A definition of "weak unsoundness" is presented, characterizing contexts where NL test case execution remains acceptable relative to industrial quality levels. The experimental evaluation utilizes eight publicly available LLMs (3B-70B parameters) and assesses their potential and limitations in GUI testing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a timely problem:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: automating GUI testing using LLMs, which is an active research area with high practical value.
    *   **Novel approach:** The guardrail mechanisms and specialized agents to dynamically verify test steps and handle errors represent a novel approach to the challenges of NL test case execution.  The definition of "weak unsoundness" is a useful concept.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, utilizing a range of LLMs and providing quantitative measures for evaluating LLM capabilities and execution consistency. Publicly available code and data enhance reproducibility and allow for further research.
    *   **Clearly defined metrics:**  The paper defines metrics such as consistency and weak unsoundness, enabling a more rigorous assessment of LLM performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope:** While the evaluation is thorough, it focuses on a specific set of websites and LLMs. The generalizability of the results to more complex and diverse applications might be limited.
    *   **Dependence on LLM capabilities:** The success of the proposed approach relies heavily on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. The performance of smaller LLMs was significantly lower, indicating a dependency on model size and architecture. The constant evolution of LLMs can affect the validity of the results over time.
    *   **Threats to validity:**  Acknowledges potential threats to validity (internal and external) such as the design of NL test cases and prompts may introduce biases, and reliance on Stagehand limits the evaluation scope. While the authors acknowledge these limitations, they could provide more concrete mitigation strategies. The "ground truth" for correctness of execution may not be perfect and could skew the results.
    *   **Incremental contribution:** While the ideas are novel, they represent an incremental step forward rather than a disruptive breakthrough. The concepts are building upon existing work in LLM prompting, GUI interaction, and software testing.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Root Causes:** While the study identifies general areas of LLM limitations (context length, content extraction), it doesn't deeply dive into *why* certain LLMs fail in particular scenarios.  A deeper analysis of the types of errors made by each LLM, and correlation to factors like model architecture or training data, would strengthen the insights.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically investigating the use of LLM agents for NL test case execution. The introduced algorithm and metrics are useful for evaluating the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in this context. While the findings are somewhat limited by the scope of the evaluation and the rapid advancements in LLM technology, the paper provides a solid foundation for future research and development in this area. The notion of weak unsoundness and the metrics for evaluation are valuable for the testing domain.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper is a well-executed study that explores a relevant and emerging area.  The experimental methodology is sound and the results are clearly presented. However, the heavy reliance on the specific capabilities of existing LLMs, the narrow scope of evaluation, and the somewhat incremental nature of the contributions, limit its overall impact and prevent it from receiving a higher score. The limitations could have been addressed by exploring broader categories of LLMs, a deeper analysis of the failure modes, and mitigation strategies.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLMs as verification oracles for Solidity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19153v1)
- **Authors**: Massimo Bartoletti, Enrico Lipparini, Livio Pompianu
- **Abstract**: Ensuring the correctness of smart contracts is critical, as even subtle flaws can lead to severe financial losses. While bug detection tools able to spot common vulnerability patterns can serve as a first line of defense, most real-world exploits and losses stem from errors in the contract business logic. Formal verification tools such as SolCMC and the Certora Prover address this challenge, but their impact remains limited by steep learning curves and restricted specification languages. Recent works have begun to explore the use of large language models (LLMs) for security-related tasks such as vulnerability detection and test generation. Yet, a fundamental question remains open: can LLMs serve as verification oracles, capable of reasoning about arbitrary contract-specific properties? In this paper, we provide the first systematic evaluation of GPT-5, a state-of-the-art reasoning LLM, in this role. We benchmark its performance on a large dataset of verification tasks, compare its outputs against those of established formal verification tools, and assess its practical effectiveness in real-world auditing scenarios. Our study combines quantitative metrics with qualitative analysis, and shows that recent reasoning-oriented LLMs can be surprisingly effective as verification oracles, suggesting a new frontier in the convergence of AI and formal methods for secure smart contract development and auditing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as verification oracles for Solidity smart contracts. It explores whether LLMs can accurately determine the truth value of custom properties written in natural language, and whether they can provide correct explanations or counterexamples to support their answers. The authors create a dataset of 2034 verification tasks, covering various Solidity features and complexity levels, and evaluate the performance of GPT-4 and GPT-5.  They also compare LLMs with symbolic verification tools like SolCMC and Certora Prover. The paper demonstrates that recent LLMs, particularly GPT-5, can be surprisingly effective as verification oracles and can complement formal verification tools. Finally, the paper studies the use of LLMs for verification auditing in real-world scenarios by analyzing properties from the Safe and InfiniFi protocols.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by systematically evaluating the potential of reasoning-oriented LLMs as verification oracles for Solidity smart contracts. While LLMs have been applied to vulnerability detection and test generation, their use for verifying arbitrary contract-specific properties is relatively unexplored. The paper also presents the largest dataset of Solidity verification tasks to date, which is a valuable contribution in itself.

*   **Significance:** The findings of the paper have significant implications for smart contract development and auditing. The results show that LLMs can potentially lower the barrier to entry for formal verification by allowing developers to express properties in natural language. Furthermore, LLMs can complement existing formal verification tools by handling properties that are difficult or impossible to express in formal specification languages. The identified synergy between LLMs and formal tools for counterexample validation is also a significant finding. The real-world audit scenario analysis highlights specific areas where LLMs can assist in identifying specification errors or detecting subtle vulnerabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs using a large dataset and multiple metrics, including accuracy, precision, recall, specificity, and F1-score.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The paper includes a qualitative analysis of the explanations and counterexamples produced by the LLMs, providing valuable insights into their reasoning processes.
    *   **Comparison with Formal Tools:** The paper compares LLMs with state-of-the-art symbolic verification tools, allowing for a rigorous assessment of their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Real-World Auditing Scenario:** The paper demonstrates the practical applicability of LLMs by analyzing properties from real-world smart contract audits.
    *   **Dataset and Code Availability:** The paper releases its dataset and code, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Ground Truth Reliability:** Determining the ground truth for complex properties remains challenging, and the reliability of the ground truth in the more complex use cases could be questioned.
    *   **LLM-Specific Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of LLMs, such as hallucinations, non-determinism, and math errors.  While these limitations are discussed, further research is needed to mitigate these issues.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the paper covers a range of Solidity features and complexity levels, the results may not generalize to all types of smart contracts or properties. More comprehensive validation of these capabilities is needed.
    *   **Dependency on Prompt Engineering:** The paper relies on a carefully crafted prompt, raising concerns about the robustness of the approach to different prompts. This makes the reproducibility tricky if prompts are changed substantially.
    *   **Tool Unsoundness**: Some comparisons were made against other unsound automated tools, but that is not a sufficient argument for LLMs' efficacy, and the point should be clearly stated to ensure there is no implicit recommendation.
    *   **Limited comparison to formal tool**: This would be a better paper if LLMs were to be compared to formal specification tools and not only formal verification tools.
    *   **Dataset Bias**: Is unclear if the dataset is balanced towards a single use case, and if the tool has any bias towards a single use case or if there are any cases in particular that it cannot solve well.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting the use of LLMs as assistive tools for smart contract development and auditing. The findings could lead to the development of hybrid verification approaches that combine the strengths of LLMs and formal verification tools. The dataset released by the authors can serve as a benchmark for future research in this area.

*   **Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by exploring the potential of LLMs as verification oracles for Solidity smart contracts. The systematic evaluation and comparison with formal verification tools provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in this context. The real-world auditing scenario demonstrates the practical applicability of the approach. However, the limitations of LLMs, the potential dependency on prompt engineering, tool unsoundness and dataset bias, need to be addressed in future research to further improve the certifiability and generalizability of the approach. The novelty and comprehensive nature of the work, along with the publicly available dataset, warrant a score of 7, acknowledging its valuable contribution while highlighting the need for further refinement and analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Soft Tokens, Hard Truths
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19170v1)
- **Authors**: Natasha Butt, Ariel Kwiatkowski, Ismail Labiad, Julia Kempe, Yann Ollivier
- **Abstract**: The use of continuous instead of discrete tokens during the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) phase of reasoning LLMs has garnered attention recently, based on the intuition that a continuous mixture of discrete tokens could simulate a superposition of several reasoning paths simultaneously. Theoretical results have formally proven that continuous tokens have much greater expressivity and can solve specific problems more efficiently. However, practical use of continuous tokens has been limited by strong training difficulties: previous works either just use continuous tokens at inference time on a pre-trained discrete-token model, or must distill the continuous CoT from ground-truth discrete CoTs and face computational costs that limit the CoT to very few tokens. This is the first work introducing a scalable method to learn continuous CoTs via reinforcement learning (RL), without distilling from reference discrete CoTs. We use "soft" tokens: mixtures of tokens together with noise on the input embedding to provide RL exploration. Computational overhead is minimal, enabling us to learn continuous CoTs with hundreds of tokens. On math reasoning benchmarks with Llama and Qwen models up to 8B, training with continuous CoTs match discrete-token CoTs for pass@1 and surpass them for pass@32, showing greater CoT diversity. In systematic comparisons, the best-performing scenario is to train with continuous CoT tokens then use discrete tokens for inference, meaning the "soft" models can be deployed in a standard way. Finally, we show continuous CoT RL training better preserves the predictions of the base model on out-of-domain tasks, thus providing a softer touch to the base model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Soft Tokens, Hard Truths":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of continuous (soft) tokens in Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning with Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors propose a novel reinforcement learning (RL) based method for training LLMs to generate continuous CoTs without requiring ground-truth discrete CoT examples.  Their approach uses "soft" tokens (mixtures of token embeddings with added noise for RL exploration) and achieves performance comparable to discrete CoT fine-tuning on math reasoning benchmarks.  Importantly, they find that training with continuous CoTs and then using discrete tokens at inference time yields the best results. The authors also show that training with continuous CoTs better preserves the base model's performance on out-of-domain tasks, suggesting improved robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its RL-based approach to training LLMs with continuous CoTs *without* relying on distilling knowledge from discrete CoTs, unlike prior work. This addresses a key scalability limitation of previous methods and enables the use of much longer CoTs.  The specific technique of adding noise to token embeddings for exploration within an RL framework is also a novel contribution in this context. However, the core idea of soft tokens and reinforcement learning are well-established in the field.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for CoT reasoning and RL fine-tuning of LLMs. The ability to train continuous CoTs without relying on ground-truth discrete CoTs opens up possibilities for exploring more diverse and potentially more efficient reasoning paths. The empirical results, while focused on math reasoning, are compelling and demonstrate that continuous CoTs can be trained and deployed effectively. The out-of-domain robustness results are also significant, suggesting that continuous CoT training can mitigate the overfitting often seen in discrete CoT fine-tuning. The result showing that "soft" models perform best when deployed with "hard" inference has significant practical implications, as the trained models can be deployed via standard methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:**  The proposed RL method overcomes the computational limitations of prior continuous CoT approaches by enabling fine-tuning of models with hundreds of tokens.
    *   **Practicality:** The "soft training, hard inference" result is practically relevant as it means the models can be integrated into existing discrete-token based inference pipelines.
    *   **Robustness:** The study presents strong evidence for the superior robustness of continuous CoT training, preventing the collapse commonly observed in discrete models when deployed out-of-domain.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough empirical evaluation across multiple datasets, models, and inference settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Domain:** The empirical evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning.  It is unclear how well the approach would generalize to other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical deduction) or generation tasks more generally.
    *   **Incrementality:** While the method addresses some limitations of previous work on continuous CoTs, it builds heavily upon existing concepts of soft tokens and RL fine-tuning. The innovation is more about *how* these techniques are combined and applied to the CoT setting, rather than introducing entirely new theoretical foundations.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical justification for optimal settings:** While the ablation studies show a robustness to scale factor and temperature in the soft-token finetuning approach, there is little theoretical justification or insight as to *why* certain settings lead to the optimal performance. This makes the finetuning procedure somewhat reliant on empirical studies to justify the setting.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in CoT reasoning by promoting the use of continuous representations. The proposed RL method provides a practical way to train and deploy these models. If the benefits observed in this work can be replicated in other domains, it could lead to more robust and flexible LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field. While it leverages existing concepts, the novel RL approach for training continuous CoTs *without* distillation from discrete CoTs, the practical deployment strategy using hard inference, and the demonstrated out-of-domain robustness make it a worthwhile advancement. The limitations regarding the domain-specific evaluation and the lack of ground breaking theoretical innovation are considered.

The work is clearly well-executed, the experimental methodology is thorough, and the results are presented with appropriate caveats. The empirical evidence supports the claim that continuous CoT training can be a valuable alternative to discrete CoT fine-tuning, particularly when robustness is a concern. The practical finding that soft-token training and hard-token inference can give better results than hard-token training is notable, as it means that the advantages of soft-token training can be integrated into LLM pipelines without substantial alteration. For these reasons, a score of 7.5 is justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unveiling the Role of Learning Rate Schedules via Functional Scaling Laws
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19189v1)
- **Authors**: Binghui Li, Fengling Chen, Zixun Huang, Lean Wang, Lei Wu
- **Abstract**: Scaling laws have played a cornerstone role in guiding the training of large language models (LLMs). However, most existing works on scaling laws primarily focus on the final-step loss, overlooking the loss dynamics during the training process and, crucially, the impact of learning rate schedule (LRS). In this paper, we aim to bridge this gap by studying a teacher-student kernel regression setup trained via online stochastic gradient descent (SGD). Leveraging a novel intrinsic time viewpoint and stochastic differential equation (SDE) modeling of SGD, we introduce the Functional Scaling Law (FSL), which characterizes the evolution of population risk during the training process for general LRSs. Remarkably, the impact of the LRSs is captured through an explicit convolution-type functional term, making their effects fully tractable. To illustrate the utility of FSL, we analyze three widely used LRSs -- constant, exponential decay, and warmup-stable-decay (WSD) -- under both data-limited and compute-limited regimes. We provide theoretical justification for widely adopted empirical practices in LLMs pre-training such as (i) higher-capacity models are more data- and compute-efficient; (ii) learning rate decay can improve training efficiency; (iii) WSD-like schedules can outperform direct-decay schedules. Lastly, we explore the practical relevance of FSL as a surrogate model for fitting, predicting and optimizing the loss curves in LLM pre-training, with experiments conducted across model sizes ranging from 0.1B to 1B parameters. We hope our FSL framework can deepen the understanding of LLM pre-training dynamics and provide insights for improving large-scale model training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Functional Scaling Law" (FSL) to model the impact of learning rate schedules (LRSs) on the training dynamics of large language models (LLMs).  Unlike existing scaling law research that focuses on final-step loss, this work analyzes the evolution of the loss curve during training. The FSL leverages a teacher-student kernel regression setup trained via online SGD and an "intrinsic time" viewpoint. It captures the influence of different LRSs through a convolution-type functional term. The authors analyze three common LRSs (constant, exponential decay, and warmup-stable-decay) in both data-limited and compute-limited regimes, providing theoretical justifications for several empirical observations in LLM training. Finally, they demonstrate the practical relevance of the FSL by using it as a surrogate model to fit, predict, and optimize loss curves in LLM pre-training experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly modeling the effect of *learning rate schedules* on scaling laws. This is a significant departure from prior work that largely overlooks this crucial hyperparameter. The FSL framework itself, with its intrinsic time concept and convolution-type functional term, provides a new way to analyze training dynamics. Modeling the interaction of LRS and scaling laws theoretically is novel and much needed. The prior work using heuristic methods demonstrates the importance of the question addressed by this paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to be influential.
    *   **Theoretical Understanding:** It offers a more nuanced theoretical understanding of LLM training by explaining how LRS influences scaling exponents. It provides theoretical rationales for established empirical practices, such as the benefits of learning rate decay and warmup-stable-decay schedules.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The FSL can be used as a surrogate model for LLM pre-training. Specifically, It could help practitioners predict loss curves for varying architectures, data set size, and LRS. The framework allows one to also compare/optimize different training setups, including LRS and dataset size.
    *   **Future Research:** The intrinsic-time SDE framework could be a versatile foundation for further theoretical investigations of learning rate scheduling and LLM pre-training dynamics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Theoretical Framework:** The paper presents a well-defined theoretical framework with strong mathematical support.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The analysis of different LRSs in various regimes offers a thorough investigation of the topic.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments, ranging from kernel regression to LLM pre-training, provide evidence for the FSL's practical relevance.
    *   **Practical implications**: Provides a theoretically sound framework to compare and analyze pre-training setups and optimize LRSs.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplified Setup:** The teacher-student kernel regression setting, while analytically tractable, is a simplification of the complexities of modern LLMs. This could limit the direct applicability of the theoretical results. This paper, while important, has limited impact because of the setting considered.
    *   **Limited LLM Experiments:** The LLM experiments, while promising, are limited to relatively small models (0.1B to 1B parameters) trained on 20B tokens. Further experiments with larger models and datasets are needed to fully validate the FSL's effectiveness at scale.
    *   **Assumptions:** The model is subject to assumptions (e.g. 5.1). Although they may be common and required for theoretical tractability, they may impact its real-world applicability.

*   **Impact on the Field:** The paper could shift the focus of scaling law research towards a more comprehensive understanding of training dynamics and the role of optimization hyperparameters. The FSL framework could be adopted by researchers and practitioners for analyzing and designing better training strategies for LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses an important and under-explored problem with a novel theoretical framework and provides empirical support for its findings. However, the simplifying assumptions and limited LLM experiments prevent it from achieving a higher score. The theoretical contributions are strong and could be highly influential. However, its impact will be limited by the gap between the teacher-student setting and the complexity of modern LLMs. Overall, it is a well-executed study with significant potential for future research.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Online Process Reward Leanring for Agentic Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19199v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoqian Liu, Ke Wang, Yuchuan Wu, Fei Huang, Yongbin Li, Junge Zhang, Jianbin Jiao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trained with reinforcement learning (RL) as autonomous agents that reason and act over long horizons in interactive environments. However, sparse and sometimes unverifiable rewards make temporal credit assignment extremely challenging. Recent work attempts to integrate process supervision into agent learning but suffers from biased annotation, reward hacking, high-variance from overly fine-grained signals or failtures when state overlap is rare. We therefore introduce Online Process Reward Learning (OPRL), a general credit-assignment strategy for agentic RL that integrates seamlessly with standard on-policy algorithms without relying on additional rollouts or explicit step labels. In OPRL, we optimize an implicit process reward model (PRM) alternately with the agent's policy to transform trajectory preferences into implicit step rewards through a trajectory-based DPO objective. These step rewards are then used to compute step-level advantages, which are combined with episode-level advantages from outcome rewards for policy update, creating a self-reinforcing loop. Theoretical findings guarantee that the learned step rewards are consistent with trajectory preferences and act as potential-based shaping rewards, providing bounded gradients to stabilize training. Empirically, we evaluate OPRL on three distinct agent benmarks, including WebShop and VisualSokoban, as well as open-ended social interactions with unverfiable rewards in SOTOPIA. Crucially, OPRL shows superior performance over frontier LLMs and strong RL baselines across domains, achieving state-of-the-art results with higher sample-efficiency and lower variance during training. Further analysis also demonstrates the efficient exploration by OPRL using fewer actions, underscoring its potential for agentic learning in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Online Process Reward Learning (OPRL), a novel credit assignment strategy for training Large Language Model (LLM) agents in interactive Reinforcement Learning (RL) environments. OPRL addresses the challenge of sparse and delayed rewards by learning an implicit process reward model (PRM) alongside the agent's policy. The PRM transforms trajectory preferences into step-level rewards via a trajectory-based DPO objective. These step rewards are then used to compute step-level advantages, which are combined with episode-level advantages to update the policy.  The approach theoretically guarantees consistency with trajectory preferences and potential-based shaping for stability. Experiments on WebShop, VisualSokoban, and SOTOPIA demonstrate superior performance compared to frontier LLMs and strong RL baselines, exhibiting higher sample efficiency, lower variance, and more efficient exploration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's core novelty lies in its online learning approach to process reward modeling *without* relying on explicit step labels or additional rollouts. This distinguishes it from existing process supervision methods that often require expensive and potentially biased human annotations or crafted heuristics.  The conversion of trajectory-level preferences into dense step-level guidance through a DPO objective is also a significant contribution.  While process reward models and DPO are established techniques, their combination and online adaptation for *agentic* RL in complex, multi-turn environments is relatively new. The theoretical justification, demonstrating preference consistency and potential-based shaping for stability, strengthens the theoretical foundations.
* **Significance:**  The significance of OPRL stems from its practical improvements in training LLM agents for challenging tasks. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the method's superiority over existing RL baselines and strong prompting techniques using frontier LLMs in multiple domains. The improved sample efficiency and reduced variance are crucial for scaling RL to more complex and real-world agent tasks. Furthermore, the method's robustness in open-ended environments with unverifiable rewards (SOTOPIA) increases its applicability to real-world interactions. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different components of OPRL, further highlighting its design strengths.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in training LLM agents in interactive environments, particularly the credit assignment problem.
    * **Novel Method:** OPRL presents a novel and well-motivated approach to address the credit assignment challenge.
    * **Theoretical Guarantees:** The theoretical analysis provides confidence in the stability and convergence properties of OPRL.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The method is evaluated on a diverse set of challenging benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness across different domains.
    * **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components of OPRL.
    * **Well-written:** The paper is well-organized and easy to follow.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions setting beta to 0.05 and alpha to 1, but a detailed analysis on the sensitivity of these hyperparameters and their impact on performance isn't thoroughly explored, and how these might vary across different problems.
    * **Computational Cost:**  While sample-efficient compared to other RL methods, the computational overhead of training a PRM alongside the policy isn't explicitly discussed, and how that adds to the computational time needed for training. A clear comparison of training time would strengthen the paper.
    * **Generalization of theoretical results:** It would be insightful to clarify whether the proven theoretical properties are applicable to all the different RL algorithms it's compatible with, or if those require a certain RL algorithm.
* **Potential Influence:**  OPRL has the potential to significantly influence the field of agentic RL. Its ability to train LLM agents in complex interactive environments with sparse rewards and limited supervision is a crucial step towards building more capable and autonomous agents. The method's robustness and sample efficiency make it a promising approach for scaling RL to real-world applications. The paper will likely inspire further research on process reward modeling and credit assignment for LLM agents.
* **Critical Questions:** A few critical questions remain:
    * How does the PRM scale with increasingly complex environments and longer trajectories?
    * How does OPRL handle environments with highly stochastic rewards, where the trajectory preferences may be noisy?
    * Can the PRM be used for transfer learning to new tasks or environments?

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of agentic RL, meriting a score of 8. OPRL addresses a key challenge (credit assignment) with an innovative online process reward learning approach, supported by theoretical analysis and comprehensive experimental evaluation. While there are minor weaknesses regarding hyperparameter analysis and computational cost details, the strengths of the paper far outweigh the limitations. OPRL is likely to have a considerable impact on the field and influence future research on training LLM agents for interactive tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Knowledge Graph and a Tripartite Evaluation Framework Make Retrieval-Augmented Generation Scalable and Transparent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19209v1)
- **Authors**: Olalekan K. Akindele, Bhupesh Kumar Mishra, Kenneth Y. Wertheim
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly enhanced conversational Artificial Intelligence(AI) chatbots; however, domain-specific accuracy and the avoidance of factual inconsistencies remain pressing challenges, particularly for large datasets. Designing an effective chatbot with appropriate methods and evaluating its effectiveness is among the challenges in this domain. This study presents a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) chatbot that harnesses a knowledge graph and vector search retrieval to deliver precise, context-rich responses in an exemplary use case from over high-volume engineering project-related emails, thereby minimising the need for document chunking. A central innovation of this work is the introduction of RAG Evaluation (RAG-Eval), a novel chain-of-thought LLM-based tripartite evaluation framework specifically developed to assess RAG applications. This framework operates in parallel with the chatbot, jointly assessing the user's query, the retrieved document, and the generated response, enabling a holistic evaluation across multiple quality metrics like query relevance, factual accuracy, coverage, coherence and fluency. The resulting scoring system is provided directly to users as a confidence score (1 to 100%), enabling quick identification of possible misaligned or incomplete answers. This proposed approach promotes transparency and rapid verification by incorporating metadata email IDs, timestamps into responses. Experimental comparisons against BERTScore and G-EVAL for summarisation evaluation tasks confirm its effectiveness, and empirical analysis also shows RAG-Eval reliably detects factual gaps and query mismatches, thereby fostering trust in high demand, data centric environments. These findings highlight a scalable path for developing accurate, user-verifiable chatbots that bridge the gap between high-level conversational fluency and factual accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) chatbot that leverages a knowledge graph and vector search retrieval to provide accurate and context-rich responses, using a dataset of over 100,000 engineering project-related emails. The core innovation is the RAG-Eval framework, a novel LLM-based tripartite evaluation system that assesses the user's query, retrieved document, and generated response simultaneously.  This framework delivers a confidence score to users, promoting transparency and rapid verification by including metadata.  Experiments demonstrate the system's effectiveness in identifying factual gaps and query mismatches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of RAG systems, focusing on both practical application and a novel evaluation method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Evaluation Framework (RAG-Eval):** The tripartite approach of RAG-Eval is a significant contribution.  Evaluating the query, retrieved context, and generated response in tandem provides a much more holistic and reliable assessment compared to traditional metrics like ROUGE or BLEU, which primarily focus on token overlap or semantic similarity with reference texts.  The integration of a confidence score is also a valuable feature, making the system's reliability immediately apparent to the user. Its integration with the chatbot is commendable and gives the user an indication of how well aligned the query, the data, and the generated response are. This is especially useful for domain-specific application with a large dataset.
    *   **Practical Application with a Large Dataset:** The system's deployment on a large dataset of real-world engineering emails makes it more practically relevant.  The use of a knowledge graph avoids the issues of chunking documents and losing contextual integrity.
    *   **Focus on Transparency and Verifiability:**  The emphasis on metadata inclusion to enable source verification is commendable and addresses a critical need in high-demand, data-centric environments. This is another distinguishing factor, that improves the usefulness of the system. The paper also focuses on rigorous anonymization and pseudonymization to ensure data is safe and private.
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Existing Evaluation Metrics:** The paper explicitly identifies and tackles the limitations of existing evaluation metrics (BLEU, ROUGE, BERTScore, and even LLM-retEval) in accurately assessing the end-to-end performance of RAG chatbots, further highlighting the value of the proposed RAG-Eval framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs (Cypher Generation and RAG-Eval):**  The reliance on LLMs for both Cypher query generation and the RAG-Eval framework introduces a potential bottleneck. The quality of these components hinges on the specific LLM used (GPT-4O in this case) and its prompt engineering.  While the paper mentions model agnosticism, the extent to which other LLMs can perform as effectively needs further investigation. As mentioned in the limitations section, the dependence on LLM adds to the response latency.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Dataset:** While the specific dataset used (engineering project emails) is a strength in demonstrating practical application, it also limits the generalizability of the findings.  The performance of the system on different types of datasets (e.g., legal documents, scientific articles) may vary. It will be helpful to test the RAG-Eval metric in a different domain, to see its applicability in other domains.
    *   **Database Scalability Issues:** As the Neo4j AuraDB graph database scales, latency may increase, which would limit the speed of the chatbot. The authors mention this in the limitations, which shows that they have thought about possible issues and issues surrounding the use of their framework.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   It provides a practical, working RAG system with a novel evaluation framework.
    *   It addresses the critical need for accurate and verifiable chatbot responses in data-intensive environments.
    *   It highlights the importance of a holistic evaluation approach that considers the query, retrieved context, and generated response.
    *   It contributes to the ongoing efforts to improve the transparency and trustworthiness of LLM-based systems.
    *   It provides a robust and scalable way to develop accurate chatbots.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of RAG-based chatbots, particularly through its innovative RAG-Eval evaluation framework. This framework directly addresses a key limitation of existing evaluation metrics, offering a more complete and nuanced assessment of system performance. However, the reliance on LLMs for Cypher query generation and evaluation, along with potential database scalability issues, temper the overall impact of the work. A higher score would be warranted with further demonstration of generalizability and mitigation of reliance on specific LLMs. Taking the strengths and weaknesses into consideration, the paper warrants a score of:

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Steering Multimodal Large Language Models Decoding for Context-Aware Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19212v1)
- **Authors**: Zheyuan Liu, Zhangchen Xu, Guangyao Dou, Xiangchi Yuan, Zhaoxuan Tan, Radha Poovendran, Meng Jiang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, yet their ability to make context-aware safety decisions remains limited. Existing methods often fail to balance oversensitivity (unjustified refusals of benign queries) and undersensitivity (missed detection of visually grounded risks), leaving a persistent gap in safety alignment. To address this issue, we introduce Safety-aware Contrastive Decoding (SafeCoDe), a lightweight and model-agnostic decoding framework that dynamically adjusts token generation based on multimodal context. SafeCoDe operates in two stages: (1) a contrastive decoding mechanism that highlights tokens sensitive to visual context by contrasting real and Gaussian-noised images, and (2) a global-aware token modulation strategy that integrates scene-level reasoning with token-level adjustment to adapt refusals according to the predicted safety verdict. Extensive experiments across diverse MLLM architectures and safety benchmarks, covering undersensitivity, oversensitivity, and general safety evaluations, show that SafeCoDe consistently improves context-sensitive refusal behaviors while preserving model helpfulness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Safety-aware Contrastive Decoding" (SafeCoDe), a novel decoding framework for steering Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to make more context-aware safety decisions. The framework addresses the challenges of balancing oversensitivity (unjustified refusals) and undersensitivity (missed detection of visual risks), which existing methods often struggle with.  SafeCoDe operates in two stages: (1) contrastive decoding to highlight tokens sensitive to visual context using real and Gaussian-noised images, and (2) global-aware token modulation integrating scene-level reasoning to adapt refusals based on safety verdicts.  The paper presents extensive experimental results across diverse MLLM architectures and safety benchmarks, demonstrating improved context-sensitive refusal behavior while maintaining model helpfulness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the SafeCoDe framework's architecture and the combination of techniques it employs. While contrastive decoding and safety-aware prompt engineering aren't entirely new concepts individually, SafeCoDe's specific application to MLLM safety with visual grounding and its two-stage process offers a distinct approach. The specific way visual cues are contrasted, along with the integration of a global safety verdict into token modulation, constitutes a significant contribution. It moves beyond simply treating images as "attack vectors" alongside text, and truly integrates them as vital elements in determining appropriateness.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and increasingly important problem: ensuring the safe and reliable deployment of MLLMs in real-world applications. The ability to make nuanced, context-aware safety decisions is essential for these models to be trusted and widely adopted. By mitigating both oversensitivity and undersensitivity, SafeCoDe tackles a genuine problem, offering a more practical and robust safety alignment solution. The paper demonstrates the limitations of existing techniques and provides a convincing alternative. This can have a meaningful impact on how we build and deploy MLLMs, making them more useful and less prone to generating harmful or inappropriate content.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a rigorous evaluation, employing a variety of safety benchmarks covering different types of safety concerns. This demonstrates the broad applicability of SafeCoDe.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies are performed to isolate the contributions of each component of the framework, strengthening the claims about the importance of both contrastive decoding and global-aware token modulation.
    *   **Generalizability Analysis:** The generalizability analysis further validates the robustness and adaptability of SafeCoDe across different MLLM architectures.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of balancing oversensitivity and undersensitivity and demonstrates the limitations of existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Access to Model Logits:** A key limitation, acknowledged by the authors, is the reliance on internal model logits, restricting its direct applicability to fully black-box models (e.g., closed-source APIs). While they propose exploring surrogate modulation in the future, this is a significant barrier to adoption.
    *   **Heuristic Modulation Design:** The early-stage modulation design (stopping after a few steps) is heuristically determined. Adaptive step lengths are stated as future work, which would make SafeCoDe more flexible and effective.
    *   **Judge Dependency** : The framework depends on a "MLLM Judge". The choice of judge influences outcome. They explore this with different judges, but future work should consider robust lightweight judges.
    *   **Limited Explanation in Refusals:** The paper admits a key limitation, where SafeCoDe refusals are often blunt and lack explanations of why the content was blocked or alternative courses of action. This reduces model helpfulness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SafeCoDe framework has the potential to influence future research on MLLM safety by shifting the focus from simple prompt-based defenses to more sophisticated decoding strategies that consider visual context. It provides a solid foundation for developing more robust and reliable safety mechanisms for multimodal models, which is crucial as they become more integrated into everyday life.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the idea of decoding strategies for better performance in MLLMs is not new, the specific design of the SafeCoDe framework and its focus on both over- and under-sensitivity for *contextual* safety alignment offers a substantial contribution. The thorough experimental validation is another strong point. However, the practical limitation regarding access to model logits reduces the overall impact of the work. The dependency on the Judge model and the limited explanations of the refusals reduces the influence slightly as well.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CompLLM: Compression for Long Context Q&A
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19228v1)
- **Authors**: Gabriele Berton, Jayakrishnan Unnikrishnan, Son Tran, Mubarak Shah
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) face significant computational challenges when processing long contexts due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. While soft context compression methods, which map input text to smaller latent representations, have shown promise, their real-world adoption is limited. Existing techniques typically compress the context as a single unit, which leads to quadratic compression complexity and an inability to reuse computations across queries with overlapping contexts. In this work, we introduce CompLLM, a soft compression technique designed for practical deployment. Instead of processing the context holistically, CompLLM divides it into segments and compresses each one independently. This simple design choice yields three critical properties: efficiency, as the compression step scales linearly with the context length; scalability, enabling models trained on short sequences (e.g., 1k tokens) to generalize to contexts of 100k tokens; and reusability, allowing compressed segments to be cached and reused across different queries. Our experiments show that with a 2x compression rate, at high context lengths CompLLM speeds up Time To First Token (TTFT) by up to 4x and reduces the KV cache size by 50%. Furthermore, CompLLM achieves performance comparable to that obtained with the uncompressed context, and even surpasses it on very long sequences, demonstrating its effectiveness and practical utility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CompLLM: Compression for Long Context Q&A":

**Summary:**
